Integrability and exact large deviations of the weakly-asymmetric exclusion process
Alexandre Krajenbrink, Pierre Le Doussal

Abstract
The weakly asymmetric exclusion process (WASEP) in one dimension is a paradigmatic system of interacting particles described by the macroscopic fluctuation theory (MFT) in the presence of driving. We consider an initial condition with densities ϱ 1 , ϱ 2 \varrho_{1},\varrho_{2} on either side of the origin, so that for ϱ 1 = ϱ 2 \varrho_{1}=\varrho_{2} the gas is stationary. Starting from the microscopic description, we obtain exact formulae for the cumulant generating functions, and large deviation rate functions of the time-integrated current and the position of a tracer. As the asymmetry/driving is increased, these describe the crossover between the symmetric exclusion process (SSEP) and the weak noise regime of the Kardar-Parisi-Zhang (KPZ) equation: we recover the two limits and describe the crossover from the WASEP cubic tail to the 5 / 2 5/2 and 3 / 2 3/2 KPZ tail exponents. Finally, we show that the MFT of the WASEP is classically integrable, by exhibiting the explicit Lax pairs, which are obtained through a novel mapping between the MFT of the WASEP and a complex extension of the classical anisotropic Landau-Lifshitz spin chain. This shows integrability of all MFTs of asymmetric models with quadratic mobility as well as their dual versions.



Introduction
.
Interacting particle systems in one dimension are a playground
to test the developing tools of non-equilibrium physics
Spohn 2012
;
Chou et al. 2011
;
Derrida 2007
. It has practical applications, e.g., to single-file diffusion which describes the transport of non-crossing particles in narrow channels

Lea 1963
;
Hahn et al. 1996
;
Wei et al. 2000
;
Kollmann 2003
;
Lin et al. 2005
;
Chowdhury et al. 2000
;
Lipowsky et al. 2001
.
Solvable microscopic models, and fluctuating hydrodynamic theories play an important role.
When the particle hopping is symmetric, as for the symmetric exclusion process (SSEP),
the fluctuations of the
density and current obey diffusive scaling at large scale.
The macroscopic fluctuation theory (MFT)
Bertini et al. 2015
;
foo a
then provides a powerful framework in which all microscopic details are replaced by two model-dependent transport coefficients
D(\varrho)
and
\sigma(\varrho)

Arita et al. 2017
,
and the particle density
\varrho
obeys a conservative stochastic equation with weak noise.
While the typical fluctuations are Gaussian, these systems exhibit nontrivial
cumulants and large deviation tails well described by MFT
Derrida 2007
.
For driven systems, such as the asymmetric exclusion process (ASEP)

Ferrari and Fontes 1994
;
Mallick 2015

these fluctuations
fall instead in the realm of the
1D Kardar-Parisi-Zhang (KPZ) universality class
Tracy and Widom 2009
;
Sasamoto and Spohn 2010
,
and are beyond the reach of the MFT. Nevertheless,
a MFT/weak noise approach is possible when the asymmetry/driving
is small and scaled appropriately: this is the
weakly
asymmetric exclusion process
(WASEP)

Bodineau and Derrida 2006
;
Prolhac and Mallick 2009
;
Vanicat et al. 2021
;
Zhao 2024
.
Note that the KPZ equation itself, in its short time regime, can also
be studied using a weak noise theory (WNT)
Kolokolov and Korshunov 2009
;
Kolokolov and Korshunov 2007
;
Meerson et al. 2016
;
Kamenev et al. 2016
.
Another avenue of applications of MFT was opened recently by considering quantum extensions
of the SSEP
Bauer et al. 2017
;
Bernard and Jin 2021
;
Bernard 2021
;
Costa et al. 2025
.

Recently there has been notable progress in obtaining exact results for particle systems described
by MFT or weak noise in one dimension. The fluctuations of the current and density
in the steady state have been much studied for an open
interval with two reservoirs
Bodineau and Derrida 2006
;
Derrida et al. 2004
,
often using
exact solutions for microscopic models (such as WASEP and ASEP)
Derrida et al. 2002
;
Enaud and Derrida 2004
;
de Gier and Essler 2011
;
Gorissen et al. 2012
;
Lazarescu 2013
,
as well as for particles on a ring

Derrida and Lebowitz 1998
;
Lee and Kim 1999
;
Derrida and Appert 1999
;
Appert-Rolland et al. 2008
;
Prolhac and Mallick 2009
;
Dagallier 2023
.
The problem on the infinite line, with an initial condition which may or may not be stationary,
is another challenge.
Some observables of interest are the time-integrated current,
i.e., the total particle flux through the origin up to time
t
,
or
the position of a tracer particle at time
t
. The aim is
to obtain the cumulants of their fluctuations, encoded
in the cumulant generating
function (CGF), and the corresponding
large deviation rate functions.
Whenever the noise is weak, one can use saddle point methods (akin to a "classical" limit)
and reduce the problem to systems of non linear PDE’s with mixed boundary conditions.
Perturbative methods then allow to compute a few lowest order cumulants (quickly
intractable with increasing order)
Krapivsky et al. 2014
;
Grabsch and Bénichou 2024
;
Berlioz et al. 2024
,
as well as some asymptotics of the
rate functions
Meerson et al. 2016
;
Kamenev et al. 2016
.
To go beyond and obtain the complete exact solution there are presently two main routes. When the microscopic model is
integrable, e.g., from Bethe ansatz methods, moment or Fredholm determinant
formula are available for some observables, and it may be
possible to extract their weak noise/"classical" asymptotics.
This was applied to obtain the integrated current and tracer
CGF at large time for the SSEP with step or stationary initial condition

Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009a
;
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009b
;
Imamura et al. 2017
;
Imamura et al. 2021
;
Dandekar and Mallick 2022
.
It was also done for the KPZ equation at short time,
with various initial conditions

Le Doussal et al. 2016
;
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
;
Smith and Meerson 2018
.
The second route, opened by us in the case of the WNT of the KPZ equation

Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2021
;
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2022
;
Tsai 2023
, and subsequently applied to the MFT of the SSEP

Mallick et al. 2022
;
Mallick et al. 2024
, and of the KMP model

Bettelheim et al. 2022a
;
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2023
;
Bettelheim et al. 2022b
,
is to directly identify the classical Lax integrability structure of the saddle point equations
and to use inverse scattering methods to obtain large deviation quantities.
This was also achieved in a parallel series of works

Grabsch et al. 2022
;
Poncet et al. 2021
;
Grabsch et al. 2024
,
and using duality

Rizkallah et al. 2023
, for review see
Grabsch et al. 2023
.
Until now however the WASEP has resisted these approaches,
a notable exception.

The aim of this Letter is to obtain the exact cumulant generating
functions, and large deviations rate functions for the integrated current and the tracer position
in the WASEP. We will start with the first method, taking
a limit from the ASEP. Then we will indicate how
the second method can be applied, by
exhibiting an explicit Lax pair.

Let us first recall that in the ASEP
each particle attempts a simple
random walk on
\mathbb{Z}
, with continuous time denoted
{\sf t}\in[0,{\sf T}]
,
jumping left at rate
L
and right at rate
R
, with the caveat that jumps
are suppressed if the destination site is already occupied.
The WASEP is defined as the large-time/weak-asymmetry limit, where the asymmetry
is scaled as
1/\sqrt{{\sf T}}
where
{\sf T}\gg 1
is the observation time.
To this aim, we introduce a small parameter
\varepsilon
,
choose
R=1+\varepsilon\nu
and
L=1-\varepsilon\nu
,
and rescale time and space
as
t=\varepsilon^{2}{\sf t}
,
x=\varepsilon m
, where
m\in\mathbb{Z}

65
.
so that
x\in\mathbb{R}
in the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
. The rescaled final time is thus

T={\sf T}\varepsilon^{2}
, typically chosen
T=\mathcal{O}(1)
.

In the limit
\varepsilon\ll 1
it has been shown
Derrida and Lebowitz 1998
;
Derrida 2007
;
Bodineau and Derrida 2004
;
Bodineau and Derrida 2006
;
Mallick 2015
;
Bertini et al. 2023
;
Kipnis et al. 1989

that the (coarse grained) density field
\varrho(x,t)
of the
WASEP is described by fluctuating hydrodynamics, i.e., by a stochastic equation in terms of the current
j(x,t)

\displaystyle j=-D_{0}\partial_{x}\varrho+\nu\sigma(\varrho)+\sqrt{\varepsilon\sigma(\varrho)}\eta

(1a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varrho+\partial_{x}j=0,

(1b)

\displaystyle\sigma(\varrho)=2D_{0}\varrho(1-\varrho)\>

(1c)

with
D_{0}=(L+R)/2=1
, and where
\eta(x,t)
is a Gaussian space-time white noise. Since there is
a factor
\varepsilon
in front of the noise, the system is in a weak noise regime.
Setting
\nu=0
recovers the MFT of the SSEP, and (see below), letting
\nu\to+\infty
leads to
the weak noise regime of the KPZ equation.

It is useful to define a height function
h(x,t)=\int_{0}^{+\infty}dy\varrho(y,0)-\int_{x}^{+\infty}dy\varrho(y,t)=h(0,t)+\int_{0}^{x}dy\varrho(y,t)
and the integrated current
J(x,t)=-h(x,t)
. Here
h(0,t)=-Q_{t}
, where

Q_{t}=\int_{0}^{+\infty}dx(\varrho(x,t)-\varrho(x,0))
is the number of particles
which have crossed the origin from left to right minus right to left during time
t
,
and is the integrated current
J(0,T)=Q_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}j(0,t^{\prime})

foo b
.

We consider the ASEP with a two-sided Bernoulli initial condition with
mean density
\varrho_{1}
for
x<0
and
\varrho_{2}
for
x>0
,
with
\varrho_{2}<\varrho_{1}
. In the WASEP limit it corresponds to choosing
the initial density field
\varrho(x,0)
with probability measure

{\cal P}[\varrho(\cdot,0)]\sim e^{-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\bar{\varrho}(x)}^{\varrho(x,0)}\mathrm{d}z\frac{\varrho(x,0)-r}{r(1-r)}}

(2)

with
\bar{\varrho}(x)=\varrho_{1}\Theta(-x)+\varrho_{2}\Theta(x)
. The case
\varrho_{1}=\varrho_{2}=\varrho

corresponds to a stationary initial condition and the case
\varrho_{1}=1
,
\varrho_{2}=0
is
the step initial condition.

Main result
.
Our first main result is an exact expression for an appropriately defined generating function (GF)
for the distribution of the observable

z(X,T)=e^{2\nu h(X,T)}=e^{-2\nu J(X,T)}
at the observation time
T
and position
X
.
This GF involves an additional auxiliary real random variable
\omega\geqslant 1
of probability density function (PDF)

P(\omega)=e^{-\frac{1}{2\nu\varepsilon}F(\omega)}
,
where the function

F(\omega)=\mathrm{Li}_{2}\left(1/\omega\right)-\log\omega\log\alpha+\mathrm{Li}_{2}(\alpha)-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(1)

(3)

has a unique minimum at the typical value
\omega_{\rm typ}=1/(1-\alpha)

with
F(\omega_{\rm typ})=0
,
0\leqslant\alpha=\frac{\varrho_{2}(1-\varrho_{1})}{\varrho_{1}(1-\varrho_{2})}\leqslant 1
,
and
{\rm Li}_{2}
is the dilogarithm function.
Our result is then expressed as

\begin{split}&\int_{1}^{+\infty}\!\!\!\!\!\!d\omega\,\big\langle e^{\frac{1}{2\nu\varepsilon}[{\rm Li}_{2}(-u\omega z(X,T))-F(\omega)]}\big\rangle\underset{\varepsilon\to 0}{\sim}e^{-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Psi(u)}\end{split}

(4)

where the l.h.s is a double expectation value, one over
the noise
\eta
in (
1
) and the initial
density (
2
), denoted here and below
as
\langle\cdot\rangle
, and the second over
\omega
.
The rate function on the r.h.s. is given by

\displaystyle\Psi(u)=-\frac{1}{2\nu}\int_{\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R}+\delta}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{2\mathbf{i}\pi y(1-y)}

(5)

\displaystyle\times\mathrm{Li}_{2}\left(-u\frac{\varrho_{1}\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)(1-y)y}{\left(\varrho_{1}-y\right)\left(y-\varrho_{2}\right)}e^{-4\nu^{2}y(1-y)T+2\nu yX}\right)

where
\varrho_{2}<\delta<\varrho_{1}
. Here
u
a generalized Laplace parameter, the above formula hold for
u>0

but can be continued for
u<0
as we discuss below. Note that
\Psi(u)
implicitly depends on
X,T
and that
each term in the expansion

\Psi(u)=\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{u^{n}}{n!}\Psi_{n}(X,T)
satisfies a heat equation

\partial_{T}\Psi_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\partial_{X}^{2}\Psi_{n}-2\nu\partial_{X}\Psi_{n}

(6)

In the stationary limit
\varrho_{1}\to\varrho_{2}
,
\Psi(u)
admits an expansion in powers of
\sqrt{u}
, see
SM
.
Finally, the result for the step initial condition is obtained setting
\varrho_{1}=1
and
\varrho_{2}=0
, i.e.,

\alpha=0
, where
\omega=1
becomes deterministic.

The above generating
function (
4
) has an unusual form, as compared to the
SSEP or to the weak noise KPZ equation.
This happens
because the Fredholm determinant formulae in the ASEP, from which we
obtain our main result, see Appendix
A
,
involve
q
-deformed
Laplace transforms. In some models such as the
q
-TASEP
it can neatly be traced to underlying Poissonian statistics
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2025
,
but the interpretation here is less clear
YanQSeries a
. Despite this
complication, we can extract the cumulants and the large deviation rate
functions of the
integrated current and of the tracer position.
Below one may set
T=1

rescalingnu b
, in which case

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}=\sqrt{{\sf T}}\gg 1
.

Results for the integrated current
.
The PDF
\mathcal{P}(J)
of the integrated current
J=-h
(leaving implicit the dependence in
X
)
takes the large deviation form

\mathcal{P}(J)\underset{\varepsilon\to 0}{\sim}e^{-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Phi(J)}

(7)

From the knowledge of
\Psi(u)
one can obtain
\Phi(J)
as well
as the cumulants, which scale as

\langle J^{n}\rangle^{c}=\varepsilon^{n-1}\kappa_{n}

(8)

The coefficients
\kappa_{n}=\mathcal{O}(1)

can be extracted from their generating function
\phi(P)
, which
takes the form

\big\langle e^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}PJ}\big\rangle\sim e^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\phi(P)}\quad,\quad\phi(P)=\sum_{n\geqslant 0}\frac{\kappa_{n}}{n!}P^{n}

(9)

From
the above one shows
SM
that
\phi(P)
and
\Phi(J)

are obtained parametrically from
\Psi(u)
(by eliminating
u
) through the system

eco
;
foo c

P=\Phi^{\prime}(J)=2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)=\log(1+u\omega_{uz}e^{-2\nu J})

(10)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\phi^{\prime}(P)=J

(11)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!=\frac{-1}{2\nu}\log\left(\frac{(1-e^{-2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)})(e^{2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)}-\alpha)}{u}\right)

(12)

where

2u\omega_{u}=\alpha-1+u+\sqrt{\left(\alpha+u-1\right)^{2}+4u}
and
z=e^{-2\nu J}
.

The
n

\kappa_{n}=\left(\frac{1}{2\nu\partial_{u}(u\Psi^{\prime}(u))}\partial_{u}\right)^{n-1}J|_{u=0}
with

given

explicit from

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}=-\frac{1}{2\nu}\log\left(2\nu(1-\alpha)\Psi^{\prime}(0)\right)

\displaystyle\kappa_{2}=\frac{1+\alpha}{4\nu(\alpha-1)}-\frac{r_{2}}{4\nu^{2}}

(14)

This gives the cumulants of

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\log(\frac{1}{2}e^{-2\nu j_{\varrho_{1}}}{\rm Erfc}(\frac{z_{\varrho_{1}}}{\sqrt{T}})+\frac{1}{2}e^{-2\nu j_{\varrho_{2}}}{\rm Erfc}(-\frac{z_{\varrho_{2}}}{\sqrt{T}}))}{2\nu}

(15)

\displaystyle\underset{\varrho_{i}=\varrho}{=}

\displaystyle j_{\varrho}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=2\nu\varrho(1-\varrho)T-\varrho X

with

,

footnotesound c

We give a formula for the higher cumulants in

Surprisingly, the higher cumulants

Results for the tracer
. Let us denote
M_{\sf t}
the position of a tagged particle in the ASEP, i.e.,
the tracer. Its position in the WASEP is
Y_{t}=\varepsilon M_{{\sf t}=t/\varepsilon^{2}}
.
It
is defined by the conservation of particle number to the right of the tracer

\int_{Y_{t}}^{\infty}dx\varrho(x,t)=\int_{Y_{0}=0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}x\varrho(x,0)\quad\Leftrightarrow h(Y_{t},t)=0

(17)

equivalent to
J(Y_{t},t)=0
(we
choose initial condition
Y_{0}=0
).
The constraint of following the tracer can thus be implemented
by setting
J=0
in the above equations. Let us set
T=1
in this tracer section,
and study the fluctuations of the scaled position
Y_{1}=M_{\sf T}/\sqrt{{\sf T}}
for
{\sf T}\gg 1
.
Making explicit the dependence in
X

in the rate functions, we can obtain the full statistics of the tracer’s position
from the
X
dependence of the rate function
\Phi(J)\equiv\Phi(J,X)
.
We find that its PDF takes the large deviation form

\mathcal{P}(Y_{1}=X)=\mathcal{P}(J(X,1)=0)\sim e^{-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Phi(0;X)}

(18)

Next we introduce the CGF

C(\lambda)
defined by

\langle e^{\lambda M_{\sf T}}\rangle=\langle e^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\lambda Y_{1}}\rangle\sim e^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}C(\lambda)}\\

(19)

It can be obtained from the relation
C(\lambda)=\max_{Y\in\mathbb{R}}[\lambda Y-\Phi(0;Y)]
,
and its Taylor expansion coefficients
c_{n}
then give the cumulants of the tracer position

C(\lambda)=\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{c_{n}}{n!}\lambda^{n}\quad,\quad\langle M_{\sf T}^{n}\rangle^{c}\simeq c_{n}\sqrt{{\sf T}}

(20)

i.e.,
\langle Y_{1}^{n}\rangle^{c}\simeq\varepsilon^{n-1}c_{n}
. There is
a simple combinatorics to compute the
c_{n}
from the cumulants
\kappa_{n}=\kappa_{n}(X)

(making explicit their
X
dependence)
obtained above (setting
T=1
). The typical tracer position
c_{1}
is the root of

\kappa_{1}(c_{1})=0
, which simplifies as
c_{1}=2\nu(1-\varrho)
for
\varrho_{1}=\varrho_{2}
.
The variance is given by

c_{2}=\frac{\kappa_{2}\left(c_{1}\right)}{\kappa_{1}^{\prime}\left(c_{1}\right){}^{2}}\underset{\varrho_{i}=\varrho}{=}\frac{2(1-\varrho)}{\varrho}{\cal G}(\nu\varrho)

(21)

in agreement with the recent perturbative calculation
(
Berlioz et al. 2024
, Eq. (S135))
.
Higher cumulants are displayed in
SM
, and exhibit
a Poissonian ballistic limit for large
\nu

Ferrari and Fontes 1996
.

Limit to the SSEP
. As
\nu\to 0
our results match the known results for the SSEP
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009a
;
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009b
;
Imamura et al. 2017
;
Imamura et al. 2021
;
Mallick et al. 2024
;
Mallick et al. 2024
.
Since
z=e^{-2\nu J}
, this limit is quite delicate.
One first shows that
\Psi(u)
has the following expansion

\Psi(u)=\frac{1}{2\nu}\Psi_{-1}(u)+\Psi_{0}(u)+\mathcal{O}(\nu)

(22)

with
u\Psi^{\prime}_{-1}(u)=\log(1+u\omega_{u})
.
Next, evaluating the factor in the l.h.s of (
4
)
at the saddle point
\omega=\omega_{uz}
and expanding
z(X,T)=z=1-2\nu J

the factor
e^{-\frac{1}{2\nu\varepsilon}\Psi_{-1}(u)}
cancels
on both sides, and one obtains

\langle e^{\frac{\log(1+u\omega_{u})J}{\varepsilon}}\rangle\sim e^{-\frac{\Psi_{0}(u)}{\varepsilon}}

(23)

Hence comparing with (
9
) we see that in the limit we can identify
P=\log(1+u\omega_{u})
and
obtain
\phi(P)=-\Psi_{0}(u)|_{u=\left(1-e^{-P}\right)\left(e^{P}-\alpha\right)}
.
In the case
X=0
and
T=1
we obtain
SM

\Psi_{0}(u)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{(-\Omega)^{n}}{n^{3/2}}

where
\Omega=u\varrho_{1}(1-\varrho_{2})
. One can
then check that the CGF

is identical to

in

(
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009a
, Eqs. (1-3))
, with

and

.

More generally, the function
\Psi_{0}(u)
is computed for any

in
SM
,
and it is checked there that the result for

(setting

) coincides with the one displayed in
(

.
This implies that we also recover correctly all the tracer
quantities in the SSEP limit.

Limit to the KPZ equation
. In the limit of large driving
\nu\to+\infty
, upon proper rescaling one
obtains the KPZ equation. Indeed, let us expand the density field around the maximum of

\nu\sigma(\varrho)
by writing
\varrho=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\tilde{\varrho}}{2\nu}
.
Inserting in (
1
) and
expanding at large
\nu
, we find that the dynamics of
\tilde{\varrho}
is governed by the
stochastic Burgers equation

\partial_{t}\tilde{\varrho}=\partial^{2}_{x}\tilde{\varrho}+\partial_{x}\tilde{\varrho}^{2}+2\nu\partial_{x}\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}\eta

(24)

In terms of the height field defined above
\varrho=\partial_{x}h
, upon rescaling

h=-J=\frac{H}{2\nu}
,
z=e^{H}
,
it leads to the KPZ equation for
H

\partial_{t}H=\partial^{2}_{x}H+(\partial_{x}H)^{2}+\sqrt{2\varepsilon_{\rm KPZ}}\eta

(25)

with
\varepsilon_{\rm KPZ}=\nu^{2}\varepsilon
. Hence
here
\varepsilon_{\rm KPZ}\ll 1
, which
corresponds to the weak noise regime of the KPZ equation,
equivalently to
its short-time regime
res
. We thus expect that the cumulants and rate functions
will converge to those obtained for the
WNT of the KPZ equation. The way it works however
is not so trivial.
First one should rescale the initial condition (IC), i.e.,
one writes
\varrho_{1,2}=\frac{1}{2}\pm\frac{\tilde{w}}{2\nu}
,
and the convergence then should be to the KPZ equation with (i) the two-sided
Brownian IC for generic
\tilde{w}
(ii) the stationary IC for
\tilde{w}=0

(iii) the droplet IC for
\tilde{w}\to+\infty
. Let us rescale
the Laplace parameter
u
and the partition function
z
as

u=\frac{4\tilde{u}e^{\nu^{2}T-\nu X}}{\nu^{2}},\;z=Ze^{-\nu^{2}T+\nu X}

(26)

and set
T=1
. Defining then the KPZ time as
T_{\rm KPZ}=\nu^{4}\varepsilon^{2}\ll 1
one shows

SM
that in the limit
\nu\to\infty
Eq. (
4
) becomes

\int_{0}^{+\infty}d\tilde{\omega}\langle e^{-\frac{\tilde{u}\tilde{w}Z+\tilde{F}(\tilde{\omega})}{\sqrt{T_{\rm KPZ}}}}\rangle\sim e^{-\frac{\tilde{\Psi}(\tilde{u})}{\sqrt{T_{\rm KPZ}}}}

(27)

where the auxiliary function
\tilde{F}(\tilde{\omega})

matches exactly the one in
(
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
, Eq. (71))

(with
\tilde{\omega}=e^{\chi^{\prime}}
), and the rate function
\tilde{\Psi}(\tilde{u})

reproduces the one for the double-sided Brownian initial condition.
We performed additional checks of convergence, up to the fourth order cumulant, see
SM
.

Tails
. It is known
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009a
;
Meerson and Sasorov 2014
for
the SSEP that the distribution of the integrated current
{\cal P}(J)\sim e^{-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Phi(J)}

exhibits a cubic tail at large
|J|
, i.e.,
\Phi(J)\simeq\frac{\pi^{2}}{12}|J|^{3}
(setting
T=1
here and below).
We show from our result that the WASEP
exhibits the same cubic tail
\Phi(J)\simeq\frac{\pi^{2}}{12}J^{3}
for
J\to+\infty
.
The effect of the driving is thus negligible in the
tail unless it is taken large
\nu\sim J
. In that regime
one finds a nontrivial crossover to the KPZ lower tail.
Defining
\tilde{J}=\frac{J}{2\nu}-\frac{1}{4}

we obtain the crossover scaling form

\Phi(J)\simeq(2\nu)^{3}\Phi_{+}(\tilde{J})
, which interpolates between the WASEP

\Phi_{+}(\tilde{J})\simeq\frac{\pi^{2}}{12}\tilde{J}^{3}
for
\tilde{J}\gg 1
,
and the known KPZ tail
5/2
exponent
Kolokolov and Korshunov 2007

and prefactor
Le Doussal et al. 2016
;
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
;
Janas et al. 2016
;
Meerson et al. 2016
,

\Phi_{+}(\tilde{J})\simeq\frac{16}{15\pi}\tilde{J}^{5/2}
for
\tilde{J}\ll 1
.
It admits the parametric representation
81

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime}_{+}(\tilde{J})=\frac{\left(4k^{2}+1\right)\arctan(2k)-2k}{2\pi}

(28)

\displaystyle\tilde{J}=\frac{k}{\pi}+k^{2}-(k^{2}+\frac{1}{4})\frac{2}{\pi}\arctan(2k)

(29)

where
k\in[0,+\infty[
should be eliminated.

As for the KPZ equation and other models
Le Doussal et al. 2016
;
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
;
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2021
;
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2022
;
Bettelheim et al. 2022b
;
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2023
;
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2024
, to obtain the other tail requires continuing
(
5
), which gives only
the
main branch
of
\Psi(u)=\Psi_{0}(u)
, valid
for
u>u_{c}=-(\sqrt{\alpha}-1)^{2}e^{\nu^{2}T}

81

and which describes
J>J_{c}
(where
J_{c}<\langle J\rangle
).
For
J<J_{c}
, all formula still apply, but with
\Psi(u)=\Psi_{0}(u)+\Delta(u)
,

u>u_{c}
,
where
\Delta(u)
is the contribution of "solitons".
As for stationary KPZ there can be multiple solitonic branches,
and even a phase transition (breaking the symmetry
x\to-x
)
Janas et al. 2016
;
Smith et al. 2018
;
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2022

as also found in open driven diffusive systems
Baek et al. 2017
.
The details are involved and analyzed elsewhere
Krajenbrink and Doussal
. We
restrict here to the simpler case of the step initial condition,

\varrho_{1}=1
,
\varrho_{2}=0
, where there is a "wall" at
J=0
,
since
J=J(0,1)
is
the number of particles to the right of zero and is positive. In the regime
\nu\gg 1

we find that
\Phi(J)
takes the following scaling form
for
0<J<J_{c}
,

\Phi(J)\simeq 4J_{c}(2\kappa+(4\kappa^{2}-1)\mathrm{arctanh}(2\kappa)),\penalty\

(30)

where
2\kappa=\sqrt{1-\frac{J}{J_{c}}}
and
J_{c}\simeq\langle J\rangle\simeq\frac{\nu}{2}
,
with
\Phi(J)\simeq 4J_{c}+2J\log(J/4J_{c})+\mathcal{O}(J)
and

\Phi(J)\simeq\frac{8}{3}J_{c}(1-\frac{J}{J_{c}})^{3/2}
for
J/J_{c}
near
1
.
The
3/2
exponent coincides with the one of the upper-tail of
the KPZ equation. Indeed and remarkably, we find
that the function in the r.h.s. of (
30
) coincides
with the recent result in
(
Das and Zhu 2022
, Eq. (1.4))

for the upper tail of the ASEP in the a priori very
different regime of fixed asymmetry
R-L=\mathcal{O}(1)
.
This coincidence indicates that no intermediate regime exists
in the upper tail when the asymmetry is scaled to small values.

Integrability of the MFT of the WASEP
.
The stochastic hydrodynamics equations (
1
) of the WASEP can also be described using the MSR dynamical path integral formalism
SM
. In the limit of weak asymmetry
\varepsilon\to 0
, the MSR action is concentrated at its saddle point yielding the non-linear system which underpin the macroscopic fluctuation theory of the WASEP

\displaystyle\partial_{t}q

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}\left[D_{0}\partial_{x}q-\sigma(q)\partial_{x}p-\nu\sigma(q)\right]\>,

(31a)

\displaystyle-\partial_{t}p

\displaystyle=D_{0}\partial_{x}^{2}p+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{\prime}(q)(\partial_{x}p)^{2}+\nu\sigma^{\prime}(q)\partial_{x}p\>,

(31b)

where
p
is the response field associated to
q
which is the optimal density. Here
D_{0}=1
and
\sigma(q)=2q(1-q)
. In the case of the Bernoulli initial condition
(
2
), these equations are completed with initial and terminal conditions for
p(x,0)
and
p(x,T)
while for the step initial condition, the initial condition is replaced by one for
q(x,0)
, see
SM
. Solving (
31
) perturbatively to order
n
in the amplitude of the response field,
one can obtain the cumulant
\kappa_{n+1}
of
J
, in a calculation which becomes extremely
tedious as
n
increases
Berlioz et al. 2024
.
It would thus be of great interest if an integrable structure exist, allowing to go beyond perturbative methods.

We now unveil the integrable structure of the WASEP.
We start by transforming the MFT equations (
31
) using the following change of functions

\begin{split}q(x,t)&=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2\nu}\partial_{x}\log Z(x,t),\\
\partial_{x}p(x,t)&=-\frac{2\nu R(x,t)Z(x,t)}{1+R(x,t)Z(x,t)}\end{split}

(32)

where the first transformation has the Cole-Hopf form, and the second
defines a response field. The new functions
Z
and
R
obey the following system

\begin{split}\partial_{t}Z&=\partial_{x}^{2}Z-\frac{2R}{1+RZ}\left((\partial_{x}Z)^{2}-(\nu Z)^{2}\right)\\
-\partial_{t}R&=\partial_{x}^{2}R-\frac{2Z}{1+RZ}\left((\partial_{x}R)^{2}-(\nu R)^{2}\right)\end{split}

(33)

It turns out that this system describes
the dynamics
of a complex extension of the classical anisotropic Landau-Lifshitz spin chain
of Hamiltonian
SM

\mathcal{H}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\,(\partial_{x}S_{+}\partial_{x}S_{-}+(\partial_{x}S_{z})^{2}-\nu^{2}(S_{z}-1)^{2})

(34)

through the following stereographic representation
Lakshmanan and Nakamura 1984
;
Lakshmanan 2011

of the spin
\mathcal{S}

in terms of the fields

R
and
Z

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}&=\begin{pmatrix}S_{z}&S_{+}\\
S_{-}&-S_{z}\\
\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{{1+RZ}}\begin{pmatrix}1-RZ&2R\\
2Z&-(1-RZ)\\
\end{pmatrix}\end{split}

(35)

The properties of the spin are as follows
\mathcal{S}^{2}=I_{d}
,
\mathrm{Det}\mathcal{S}=-1
and
S_{-}S_{+}+S_{z}^{2}=1

and note that
S_{\pm}
are real.
Such mapping have been considered before but only in the case
\nu=0
(SSEP)

Tailleur et al. 2008
;
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009b
(see
Essler and Rittenberg 1996
;
Alcaraz et al. 1994

for related references).
The anisotropic LL model is integrable
Nakamura and Sasada 1982
.
As a consequence, the system (
33
) is also integrable
in the Lax sense, allowing for its exact solvability. The Lax representation of the problem is given by the linear system
\partial_{x}\vec{v}=L\vec{v}
,
\partial_{t}\vec{v}=M\vec{v}
, with
\vec{v}
a two-component vector, which compatibility gives the zero curvature condition
\partial_{t}L-\partial_{x}M+[L,M]=0
. In the present case, the Lax matrices read

\begin{split}&L=-\frac{\mathbf{i}k}{2}\mathcal{S}+\mu[\sigma_{3},\mathcal{S}]\\
&=\frac{1}{1+RZ}\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{\mathbf{i}k}{2}\left(1-RZ\right)&(\nu-\mathbf{i}k)R\\
-(\nu+\mathbf{i}k)Z&\frac{\mathbf{i}k}{2}\left(1-RZ\right)\\
\end{pmatrix}\end{split}

(36)

and

\begin{split}M=&\frac{k^{2}}{2}\mathcal{S}+\frac{\mathbf{i}k}{2}\mathcal{S}\partial_{x}\mathcal{S}+\mathbf{i}\mu k[\sigma_{3},\mathcal{S}]\\
&-\mu[\sigma_{3},\mathcal{S}\partial_{x}\mathcal{S}]+4\mu^{2}\{\sigma_{3},\mathcal{S}\}\sigma_{3}-\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}\sigma_{3}\end{split}

(37)

with
\mu=\pm\frac{\nu}{4}
,
\sigma_{3}=\rm{Diag}(1,-1)
and
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
(resp.
[\cdot,\cdot]
) is the standard anti-commutator (resp. commutator). The zero curvature is verified if the pair
(R,Z)
verifies (
33
). We have chosen
\mu=\frac{\nu}{4}
in the second line of (
36
).

Discussion and outlook
. We have obtained the exact large deviation rate functions
and cumulants of the time-integrated current and of the
tracer position for the WASEP.
In addition we constructed the explicit Lax pair for the WASEP,
by unveiling a mapping to the anisotropic Landau-Lifshitz chain.
This opens the way for using inverse scattering
in future work, and investigate a broader
family of initial conditions and observables.
As a byproduct we also obtain, see Appendix
C
,
rate functions, cumulants and integrability
for several other MFT’s. This
includes the weakly asymmetric inclusion process (WASIP),
defined in
Giardinà et al. 2010
;
Opoku and Redig 2015
;
Reuveni et al. 2011
;
Bettelheim and Meerson 2024
,
the weakly asymmetric KMP model,
as well as the three dual models, including
the weakly asymmetric zero range process
with geometric stationary measure (upon exchanging by duality
integrated current and tracer position

SM
;
Rizkallah et al. 2023
;
Berlioz et al. 2024
).
It would finally be interesting to consider quantum
extensions of the WASEP and of the
WASIP along the lines of
Jin et al. 2020
;
Bernard et al. 2025
.

We thank Aurelien Grabsch for very useful discussions on the WASEP.
We also thank G. Barraquand, A. Borodin, B. Derrida and V. Pasquier for discussions on related topics.

and hospitality from LPTMS-Orsay. We thank the Harvard University Center of Mathematical Sciences
and Applications for hospitality and partial support.

Appendix A
Derivation of the rate function
\Psi(u)

To obtain the rate function
\Psi(u)
of the WASEP we start from an exact
formula obtained for the ASEP in
(
Aggarwal 2018
, Thm. 4.8)
,
see also
Borodin et al. 2014
;
Aggarwal and Borodin 2019
. Upon some
changes of notations, let us define
\tau=L/R<1
,

\theta_{i}=\varrho_{i}/(1-\varrho_{i})
, and
\alpha=\theta_{2}/\theta_{1}
. It is assumed that
\varrho_{2}<\varrho_{1}
.
The above theorems characterize the distribution of the
discrete current
{\sf J}_{\sf t}(m)

of the ASEP, defined by
(
Aggarwal 2018
, Eq. (1.1))
,
in terms of a Fredholm determinant

\mathbb{E}\left[\big\langle\frac{1}{(-u\tau^{{\sf J}_{\sf t}(m)-\sf{j}},\tau)_{\infty}}\big\rangle\right]=\mathrm{Det}(I+K_{u})_{L^{2}(C_{0})}

(SA.1)

where
\sf{j}
is an auxiliary random variable following a
\tau
-deformed geometric distribution with PDF

\begin{split}\mathbf{p}_{\alpha}(\sf{j})&=\alpha^{\sf{j}}\frac{(\alpha;\tau)_{\infty}}{(\tau;\tau)_{\sf{j}}}\mathds{1}\{0\leqslant{\sf{j}}\}\end{split}

(SA.2)

with
\alpha=\theta_{2}/\theta_{1}
and we recall the notation
(a,q)_{\infty}=\prod_{\ell=0}^{\infty}(1-q^{\ell}a)
and
(a;q)_{n}=\frac{(a;q)_{\infty}}{(aq^{n};q)_{\infty}}
. The kernel in (
SA.1
) has several equivalent expressions, and we use the one in

(
Aggarwal and Borodin 2019
, Thm. 4.11)

K_{u}(v,v^{\prime})=\int_{D}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{2\mathbf{i}\pi}\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi s)}\frac{g(v)}{g(\tau^{s}v)}\frac{u^{s}}{\tau^{s}v-v^{\prime}}

(SA.3)

with the function
g(v)
being defined as

g(v)=e^{(R-L){\sf t}\frac{\tau}{\tau+v}}\left(\frac{\tau}{\tau+v}\right)^{-m}\frac{((\tau\theta_{2})/v;\tau)_{\infty}}{(v/(\tau\theta_{1});\tau)_{\infty}}

(SA.4)

we refer to
Aggarwal 2018
;
Aggarwal and Borodin 2019
for the precise definition of the contours
D
and
C_{0}
.
In order to study the WASEP limit, we choose, with
\nu>0

\begin{split}&R=1+\varepsilon\nu,L=1-\varepsilon\nu,\tau\simeq e^{-2\nu\varepsilon}\\
&{\sf{T}}=\frac{T}{\varepsilon^{2}},m=\frac{X}{\varepsilon},z(X,T)=\tau^{{\sf J}_{\sf t}(m)}\end{split}

(SA.5)

Using the asymptotics of
q
-Pochhammer functions (see e.g., Section
F
)

\log(x,q)_{\infty}\underset{q\to 1}{\to}-\frac{1}{1-q}{\rm Li}_{2}(x)+\frac{1}{2}\log(1-x)+\mathcal{O}(1-q)

(SA.6)

we obtain that in the WASEP limit
\varepsilon\ll 1
the auxiliary random variable
\omega=\tau^{-{\sf j}}
has a pdf proportional to
\mathbf{p}_{\alpha}({\sf{j}})\sim e^{-\frac{1}{2\nu\varepsilon}F(\omega)}

so that the double expectation value in the l.h.s of (
SA.1
) becomes

\int_{1}^{+\infty}\!\!d\omega\,\big\langle e^{\frac{1}{2\nu\varepsilon}[{\rm Li}_{2}(-u\omega z(X,T))-F(\omega)]}\big\rangle

(SA.7)

Next we extend to the present case the first cumulant method developed in
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2018
;
Krajenbrink et al. 2018
;
Krajenbrink 2019

to obtain the asymptotics of such Fredholm determinants. This requires the following asymptotics

\begin{split}&\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}2\nu\varepsilon\log g(v)=\varphi(v)\\
&=\frac{4\nu^{2}T}{(1+v)}+{2\nu X}\log(1+v)+\mathrm{Li}_{2}\left(\frac{v}{\theta_{1}}\right)-\mathrm{Li}_{2}\left(\frac{\theta_{2}}{v}\right)\end{split}

(SA.8)

obtained from (
SA.4
) and (
SA.6
). The details
are given in
SM
and the result is

\mathrm{Det}(I+K_{u})\underset{\varepsilon\to 0}{\sim}\exp\left(-\frac{\Psi(u)}{\varepsilon}\right)

(SA.9)

with

\Psi(u)=-\frac{1}{2\nu}\int_{C}\frac{\mathrm{d}v}{2\mathbf{i}\pi v}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-ue^{v\varphi^{\prime}(v)})

(SA.10)

where the contour
C
encloses
0
, remains to the right of
-1

and crosses the positive real axis between
\theta_{2}
and
\theta_{1}
.
Upon the change of variable
y=v/(1+v)
and the choice
of contour
y=\delta+\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R}
, with
\varrho_{2}<\delta<\varrho_{1}
one obtains
our result (
5
). Note that for the step initial condition
\varrho_{1}=1
,
\varrho_{2}=0
,
we can alternatively use
(
Borodin et al. 2014
, Thm. 5.3)
.

The above derivation is restricted to
\nu>0
. Indeed
since here
\varrho_{1}>\varrho_{2}
, if one considers instead the ASEP
with
L>R
, a shock appears. Here, in the WASEP limit however this
shock is rounded and one can check
that the cumulants are indeed well behaved at
\nu=0
, so their
expressions straightforwardly extend to
\nu<0
.
It is only in the large WASEP time limit
T\gg 1
(or equivalently for large
\nu
)
that the shock develops
SM
.

Appendix B
Stationary cumulants

In the case
\varrho_{1}=\varrho_{2}=\varrho
the rate function
\Psi(u)=\psi(v)

where
v=\sqrt{u}
. The cumulants
\kappa_{n}
for
n\geqslant 2

can be obtained as polynomials in the ratios
R_{n}=\frac{\psi^{(n)}(0)}{\psi^{\prime}(0)^{n}}

upon expanding

\displaystyle\kappa_{n}=-\frac{B_{n-1}}{\nu(n-1)}\delta_{n\geqslant 3,{\rm odd}}

(SB.11)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\nu^{n}}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\partial_{v}(v\psi^{\prime}(v))}\partial_{v}\right)^{n-1}\log(\nu\psi^{\prime}(v))\right]_{v=0}

where
B_{n}
are Bernoulli numbers,
giving
\kappa_{2}=-\frac{R_{2}}{\nu^{2}}
,

\kappa_{3}=-\frac{1}{12\nu}+\frac{1}{\nu^{3}}(3R_{2}^{2}-R_{3})

and so on. We found explicit formula for the
R_{n}

(setting
T=1
)

\begin{split}R_{2q}=&-\frac{(\nu^{2}\varrho(1-\varrho))^{q}}{2q\nu}\partial_{p}^{2q-1}\bigg(((\varrho+p)(1-\varrho-p))^{q-1}\\
&\times e^{4q\nu p(\nu p-y)}{\rm Erf}(\sqrt{q}(2p\nu-y))\bigg)|_{p=0}\end{split}

(SB.12)

and

R_{2q+1}=\frac{\nu^{2q}}{(2q+1)}\partial_{\tilde{z}}^{2q}\left(\frac{\partial_{\tilde{z}}p}{(\varrho+p)(1-\varrho-p)}\right)|_{\tilde{z}=0}

(SB.13)

with
y=\nu(1-2\varrho)-X/2
and where
p=p(\tilde{z})

is obtained by inverting the series
\tilde{z}=\tilde{z}(p)

with

\tilde{z}=\frac{p\,e^{-2\nu p(\nu p-y)}}{\sqrt{\varrho(\varrho-1)(\varrho+p)(\varrho+p-1)}}

(SB.14)

These formulae allow iterative calculation of the cumulants to an arbitrary order,
for more details see
SM
.

Appendix C
Extension to other quadratic MFT models

Extending to the driven case the mapping introduced in
(
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009b
, Eq. (33))

between the MFT’s where
\sigma(\varrho)
is
quadratic in
\varrho
and
D(\varrho)=1
, we obtain
SM
the cumulants of the integrated current
for two additional MFT models starting from their (two-sided)
stationary initial conditions (with densities
\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2}
on each
side of the origin). For the WASIP, i.e.,
for
\sigma(\varrho)=2\varrho(1+\varrho)
, we find

\kappa_{n}^{\rm WASIP}(\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2},\nu)=-\kappa_{n}^{\rm WASEP}(-\varrho_{1},-\varrho_{2},\nu)

(SC.15)

understood as an analytical continuation in the parameters.
For the weakly asymmetric KMP model, i.e., for
\sigma(\varrho)=2\varrho^{2}
,
we find

\displaystyle\kappa_{n}^{\rm WAKMP}(\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2},\nu)

(SC.16)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n+1}\lim_{B\to 0}B^{n}\kappa_{n}^{\rm WASEP}(\frac{\varrho_{1}}{B},\frac{\varrho_{2}}{B},-B\nu)

Since the map (
32
) extends to these models, see
SM
,
it also shows their integrability. Finally these results extend to
the three dual models, see
SM
.

Appendix D
Second Lax matrix for the MFT of the WASEP

We give here the explicit form of the second Lax matrix
M
in terms
of the fields
R
and
Z
defined in (
32
).

\begin{split}&M=\frac{1}{(1+RZ)^{2}}\\
&\begin{pmatrix}\frac{k^{2}}{2}-\nu^{2}ZR-\mathbf{i}k(Z\partial_{x}R-R\partial_{x}Z)-R^{2}Z^{2}\left(\frac{k^{2}}{2}+\nu^{2}\right)&(k+\mathbf{i}\nu)\left(R\left(k+R\left(kZ+\mathbf{i}\partial_{x}Z\right)\right)+\mathbf{i}\partial_{x}R\right)\\
(k-\mathbf{i}\nu)\left(Z\left(k+Z\left(kR-\mathbf{i}\partial_{x}R\right)\right)-\mathbf{i}\partial_{x}Z\right)&-\frac{k^{2}}{2}+\nu^{2}ZR+\mathbf{i}k(Z\partial_{x}R-R\partial_{x}Z)+R^{2}Z^{2}\left(\frac{k^{2}}{2}+\nu^{2}\right)\\
\end{pmatrix}\end{split}

(SD.17)

Supplementary Material for

Macroscopic fluctuations in the weakly-asymmetric exclusion process

Appendix E
First cumulant approximation for the Fredholm determinant of the ASEP

Important remark
. Everywhere in this section the parameter
\tau
defined in the text is
denoted
q
.

E.1
Fredholm determinant factorization

We first rewrite the Fredholm determinant
\mathrm{Det}(I+K_{u})_{L^{2}(C_{0})}
in (
SA.1
) in a more convenient form
for the asymptotic analysis. The ASEP kernel
(
SA.3
) is part a of a larger family of kernels for the various
q
-deformed models
which have the following form.

K_{u}(v,v^{\prime})=\int_{D}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{2\mathbf{i}\pi}\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi s)}u^{s}\frac{g(v)}{g(q^{s}v)}\frac{1}{q^{s}v-v^{\prime}}

(SE.18)

These kernels differ mainly with the choice of function
g(v)
as well as some details about the choice of the integration contour
D
on
s
, and the contour
C_{0}
on which
v
,
v^{\prime}
belong.

Let us perform some manipulations on the kernel valid for any
q
. One first sets
\zeta=q^{s}v
and gets

K_{u}(v,v^{\prime})=\frac{1}{\log q}\int_{D^{\prime}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\zeta}{2\mathbf{i}\pi\zeta}\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi s)}u^{s}\frac{g(v)}{g(\zeta)}\frac{1}{\zeta-v^{\prime}}

(SE.19)

with the identification
s\log q=\log\zeta-\log v
.
Introducing the identity

\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi s)}u^{s}=|\log q|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}r\,\frac{u}{u+q^{-r}}e^{-sr\log q},\quad 0<\Re(s)<1

(SE.20)

The kernel further reads (since
q<1
)

K_{u}(v,v^{\prime})=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}r\,\int_{D^{\prime}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\zeta}{2\mathbf{i}\pi\zeta}\frac{u}{u+q^{-r}}\frac{v^{r}}{\zeta^{r}}\frac{g(v)}{g(\zeta)}\frac{1}{\zeta-v^{\prime}}

(SE.21)

One now factorises the kernel into

K_{u}(v,v^{\prime})=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}r\,\int_{D^{\prime}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\zeta}{2\mathbf{i}\pi\zeta}A_{1}(v,r)\sigma(r)A_{2}(r,\zeta)A_{3}(\zeta,v^{\prime})

(SE.22)

where we have defined the kernels

A_{1}(v,r)=v^{r}g(v),\quad,\quad A_{2}(r,\zeta)=\frac{1}{\zeta^{r}g(\zeta)}\quad,\quad A_{3}(\zeta,v^{\prime})=\frac{1}{\zeta-v^{\prime}}\quad,\quad\sigma_{u}(r)=\frac{1}{1+q^{-r}/u}

(SE.23)

Inserting this factorization into the Fredholm determinant one obtains

\begin{split}\mathrm{Det}(I+K_{u})_{L^{2}(C_{0})}&=\mathrm{Det}(I-A_{1}\sigma_{u}A_{2}A_{3})_{L^{2}(C_{0})}\\
&=\mathrm{Det}(I-\sigma_{u}A_{2}A_{3}A_{1})_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\\
&=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{\ell=1}^{+\infty}(1-\sigma_{u}(a_{\ell}))\right]\end{split}

(SE.24)

where from the first to the second line we have used Sylvester’s identity. In the third line we
have formally interpreted the Fredholm determinant of the second line as an expectation value over some determinantal point process
\{a_{\ell}\}_{\ell\geqslant 1}
which correlations are controlled by the kernel
\mathcal{A}=A_{2}A_{3}A_{1}
.

\begin{split}\mathrm{Det}(I+K_{u})_{L^{2}(C_{0})}&=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{\ell=1}^{+\infty}(1-\sigma_{u}(a_{\ell}))\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{\ell=1}^{+\infty}e^{-\varsigma(a_{\ell})}\right]\end{split}

(SE.25)

where we have introduced the function
\varsigma
defined as

e^{-\varsigma}=1-\sigma_{u}=\frac{1}{1+uq^{r}}
, which allows to interpret Eq. (
SE.25
) as a linear statistics of the point process
\{a_{\ell}\}_{\ell\geqslant 1}
over the observable
\varsigma(r)=\log(1+uq^{r})=-\textrm{Li}_{1}(-uq^{r})
.

E.2
First cumulant approximation in the limit
q\to 1

We now apply the first cumulant method
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2018
;
Krajenbrink et al. 2018
;
Krajenbrink 2019
which asserts that as some control parameter (here
\log q
) goes to 0, the linear statistics over the point process self-averages, i.e., to the leading order one has

\begin{split}\mathrm{Det}(I+K_{u})_{L^{2}(C_{0})}=\mathrm{Det}(I-\sigma_{u}A_{2}A_{3}A_{1})_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{\ell=1}^{+\infty}e^{-\varsigma(a_{\ell})}\right]\sim e^{-{{\rm Tr}}(\varsigma A_{2}A_{3}A_{1})}\end{split}

(SE.26)

This means that to leading order, the quantity of interest only involves the diagonal part of the kernel
\mathcal{A}=A_{2}A_{3}A_{1}
, i.e., its density
\varrho(a)
, via
{{\rm Tr}}(\varsigma\mathcal{A})=\int\mathrm{d}a\varrho(a)\varsigma(a)

One has

\varrho(r)=(A_{2}A_{3}A_{1})(r,r)=\int_{C_{0}}\frac{\mathrm{d}v}{2\mathbf{i}\pi}\int_{D^{\prime}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\zeta}{2\mathbf{i}\pi\zeta}\frac{v^{r}g(v)}{\zeta^{r}g(\zeta)}\frac{1}{\zeta-v}

(SE.27)

The first cumulant reads

\begin{split}{{\rm Tr}}(\varsigma A_{2}A_{3}A_{1})&=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}r\varrho(r)\log(1+uq^{r})\\
&=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}r\int_{C_{0}}\frac{\mathrm{d}v}{2\mathbf{i}\pi}\int_{D^{\prime}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\zeta}{2\mathbf{i}\pi\zeta}\mathrm{Li}_{1}(-uq^{r})\frac{v^{r}g(v)}{\zeta^{r}g(\zeta)}\frac{1}{\zeta-v}\\
&=\frac{1}{\log q}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}r\int_{C_{0}}\frac{\mathrm{d}v}{2\mathbf{i}\pi}\int_{D^{\prime}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\zeta}{2\mathbf{i}\pi\zeta}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-uq^{r})\frac{v^{r}g(v)}{\zeta^{r}g(\zeta)}\frac{\log{\zeta}-\log v}{\zeta-v}\\
\end{split}

(SE.28)

We have proceeded to an integration by part on the variable
r
from the second to the third line, note that there is no boundary term. In the regime where we want to apply the first cumulant approximation, we rescale the variables as

\{q=e^{-\eta},r=\frac{\tilde{r}}{\eta}\}

(SE.29)

and we will study the limit
\eta\to 0
. Note that to apply this method to the main text we will use
\eta=2\nu\varepsilon
.
This will allow us to further evaluate the integrals at their saddle point. Further dropping the tilde, we find that at leading order

\begin{split}{{\rm Tr}}(\varsigma A_{2}A_{3}A_{1})&=-\frac{1}{\eta^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}r\int_{C_{0}}\frac{\mathrm{d}v}{2\mathbf{i}\pi}\int_{D^{\prime}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\zeta}{2\mathbf{i}\pi\zeta}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-ue^{-r})\frac{e^{\frac{r}{\eta}\log v+\log g(v)}}{e^{\frac{r}{\eta}\log\zeta+\log g(\zeta)}}\frac{\log{\zeta}-\log v}{\zeta-v}\\
&=-\frac{1}{\eta^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}r\int_{C_{0}}\frac{\mathrm{d}v}{2\mathbf{i}\pi}\int_{D^{\prime}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\zeta}{2\mathbf{i}\pi\zeta}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-ue^{-r})\frac{e^{\frac{1}{\eta}\tilde{\Phi}(v)}}{e^{\frac{1}{\eta}\tilde{\Phi}(\zeta)}}\frac{\log{\zeta}-\log v}{\zeta-v}\end{split}

(SE.30)

where we have defined
\tilde{\Phi}(v)=r\log v+\eta\log g(v)
.
One obtains

{{\rm Tr}}(\varsigma\mathcal{A})=\frac{1}{\eta^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-ue^{r})I(r)

(SE.31)

where the integrand is

I(r)=-\int_{C_{0}}\frac{\mathrm{d}v}{2\mathbf{i}\pi}\int_{D^{\prime}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\zeta}{2\mathbf{i}\pi\zeta}\frac{e^{\frac{1}{\eta}\tilde{\Phi}(v)}}{e^{\frac{1}{\eta}\tilde{\Phi}(\zeta)}}\frac{\log{\zeta}-\log v}{\zeta-v}

(SE.32)

where the rate function at the lowest order reads

\begin{split}\tilde{\Phi}(v)&r\log v+\varphi(v)\\
\end{split}

(SE.33)

since the function
g(v)
is generally
q
-dependent, we only need to study its first order

\varphi(v)=\lim_{\eta\to 0}\eta\log g(v)|_{q=e^{-\eta}}

(SE.34)

The function depends on
g(v)
, hence
\varphi(v)
depends on the model.
For the present model (ASEP with Bernoulli initial conditions) the function
\varphi(v)
was obtained in (
SA.8
). The saddle point of the integral in (
SE.32
) is thus the solution of

\tilde{\Phi}^{\prime}(v)=0\Rightarrow\varphi^{\prime}(v)=-\frac{r}{v}

(SE.35)

Note that this leads to

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}r=-(\varphi^{\prime}(v)+v\varphi^{\prime\prime}(v))\mathrm{d}v,\\
\tilde{\Phi}^{\prime\prime}(v)=\varphi^{\prime\prime}(v)+\frac{\varphi^{\prime}(v)}{v}\end{cases}

(SE.36)

This implies the following Jacobian between
v
and
r

\mathrm{d}r=-v\tilde{\Phi}^{\prime\prime}(v)\mathrm{d}v

(SE.37)

The saddle point on
v
and
\zeta
is the same by contour deformation and the factor
\frac{\log{\zeta}-\log v}{\zeta-v}
simply becomes the derivative
\frac{1}{v}
. Using the saddle point formula we obtain

\begin{split}{{\rm Tr}}(\varsigma\mathcal{A})&\simeq\frac{1}{\eta}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{2\mathbf{i}\pi}\frac{1}{v(r)^{2}}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-ue^{-r})\frac{1}{\tilde{\Phi}^{\prime\prime}(v(r))}\\
&\simeq-\frac{1}{\eta}\int_{C}\frac{\mathrm{d}v}{2\mathbf{i}\pi}\frac{1}{v}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-ue^{v\varphi^{\prime}(v)})\\
\end{split}

(SE.38)

where
C
is the limit of
C_{0}
when
\eta\to 0
(i.e.,
q\to 1
). Hence, we obtain

\mathrm{Det}(I+K_{u})_{L^{2}(C_{0})}\sim\exp\left(\frac{1}{\eta}\int_{C}\frac{\mathrm{d}v}{2\mathbf{i}\pi}\frac{1}{v}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-ue^{v\varphi^{\prime}(v)})\right)

(SE.39)

Setting
\eta=2\nu\varepsilon
this leads to (
SA.9
) and (
SA.10
) in the text.
In the case of the ASEP the contours are as follows
Aggarwal 2018
;
Aggarwal and Borodin 2019

D
is a positively oriented contour in the complex plane containing
0
and
\tau\theta_{2}
, but leaving outside
-\tau
and
\tau\theta_{1}
.
C_{0}
is a positively oriented contour in the complex plane contained in
\tau^{-1}D
, containing
-\tau
,
\tau\theta_{2}
, 0 and the contour
D
, but leaving outside
-1
and
\tau\theta_{1}
. In that
case the contour
C
encloses
0
, remains to the right of
-1
(it contains
-1
as an asymptotic point), and crosses the positive real axis between
\theta_{2}
and
\theta_{1}
.

Appendix F
q
-deformed functions and useful asymptotic formulae

We define the infinite
q
-Pochhammer symbol, its finite
n
version and the the
q
-factorial as

(a,q)_{\infty}=\prod_{\ell=0}^{\infty}(1-q^{\ell}a),\quad(a;q)_{n}=\frac{(a;q)_{\infty}}{(aq^{n};q)_{\infty}}=\prod_{\ell=0}^{n-1}(1-aq^{\ell}),\quad[n]_{q}!=\frac{(q;q)_{n}}{(1-q)^{n}}

(SF.40)

leading to a definition of the
q
-deformed binomial factor as

\frac{(q;q)_{m}}{(q;q)_{m-j}(q;q)_{j}}=\binom{m}{j}_{q}

(SF.41)

We now recall the following two useful asymptotics identities of
q
-Pochhammer functions which are used in this work

\log(x,q)_{\infty}\underset{q\to 1}{\to}-\frac{1}{1-q}{\rm Li}_{2}(x)+\frac{1}{2}\log(1-x)+\mathcal{O}(1-q)

(SF.42)

implying the following result for the finite
n

q
-Pochhammer symbol

\log(a;q)_{n}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}q\to 1,\\
n\to\infty,\\
q^{n}=b\,\mathrm{finite}\end{subarray}}{\to}-\frac{1}{1-q}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(a)+\frac{1}{1-q}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(ab)+\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{1-a}{1-ab}\right)+\mathcal{O}(1-q)

(SF.43)

The asymptotic formula (
SF.43
) was also used in the context of high energy physics in
Faddeev and Kashaev 1994
. A more complete series can be found in Ref.
atabler
. Finally, we need the following asymptotic to obtain estimates on the tails of the current distribution

\mathrm{Li}_{s}(-e^{\mu})\underset{\mu\to+\infty}{\simeq}-\frac{\mu^{s}}{\Gamma(s+1)}

(SF.44)

Appendix G
Definitions and observables

We study in this work the WASEP from different perspectives:

•

a Eulerian perspective where we focus on a single position in space and investigate the number of particles that have crossed this position. For instance we define
Q_{t}=\int_{0}^{+\infty}dy(\varrho(y,t)-\varrho(y,0))
which is the total flux through the origin, i.e., the number of particles which have crossed the origin from left to right minus right to left during time
t
. We can also define an integrated current
J(x,t)=\int_{x}^{+\infty}dy\varrho(y,t)-\int_{0}^{+\infty}dy\varrho(y,0)
so that
Q_{T}=J(0,T)
. More generally the number of particles

Q_{t}(x)
which have crossed the point
x
from left to right minus right to left during time
t

is then
Q_{t}(x)=\int_{x}^{+\infty}dy(\varrho(y,t)-\varrho(y,0))=J(x,t)-J(x,0)
. This definition of
J(x,t)
is the analog in the continuum of
the current (called
{\sf J}_{\sf t}(m)
in our notations) defined in
in
(
Aggarwal 2018
, Eq. (1.1))
for the discrete model. More precisely upon the WASEP rescaling
(
SA.5
), one has
\varepsilon{\sf J}_{\sf t}(m)\to J(x,t)

in the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
.

•

a Lagrangian perspective where we focus on a single tagged particle (the tracer) and investigate the properties of its displacement over time
If such a tracer starts at initial time at a position
X_{0}=0
, then by defining the height field

{h}(x,t)=\int_{0}^{+\infty}dy\varrho(y,0)-\int_{x}^{+\infty}dy\varrho(y,t)
, we have by the conservation of the number of particles to the right of the tracer, that its position at time
t
,
X_{t}
can be obtained as

{h}(X_{t},t)=0

(SG.45)

These two points of view provide dual and equivalent representations of the initial problem. Additionally, we will define
z(x,t)
as the exponential of the height or current which we will identify as a partition function. Here and below, we focus on the fields at the "observation point"
x=X
and
t=T
, and we often
make implicit the dependence in
X,T
, i.e.,

z(X,T)=z=e^{2\nu h}=e^{-2\nu J}=e^{H}

(SG.46)

In order to study the convergence of the WASEP to the KPZ equation, we additionally define a KPZ height field

H_{\rm KPZ}=-2\nu J+\nu^{2}T-\nu X\,,

(SG.47)

Note the minus sign between the definitions of the height and the integrated current
J
, i.e.,
h=-J
, so that events with lower than
average
J
(few particles have moved from left to right)
correspond to the upper tail of the height field, and vice-versa.

Remark G.1
.

In most of the sections below we find useful to keep the explicit dependence in the WASEP
time
T
. In fact since the MFT stochastic equation (
1
) is statistically invariant under
the change from
(\nu,T,x,t)
to
(\nu\sqrt{T},1,x/\sqrt{T},t/T)
it is not
strictly necessary. Dimensionless quantities, such as
\varrho
or
2\nu J
, are invariant
under this change, and so is the (random) initial condition studied here,
implying e.g., the statistical equivalence (i.e., in law)

J(\nu,X,T)\equiv\sqrt{T}J(\nu\sqrt{T},X/\sqrt{T},1)

(SG.48)

As a consequence the coefficients
\kappa_{n}
defined in the text have the scaling form

\kappa_{n}(\nu,X,T)=\sqrt{T}\kappa_{n}(\nu\sqrt{T},X/\sqrt{T},1)

(SG.49)

Finally, note that large WASEP time
T
is thus equivalent to large
\nu
.

Appendix H
Derivation of the Legendre transforms Eqs. (
10
)–(
11
)

Here we give a derivation of the various Legendre transforms given in the main text in Eqs. (
10
)–(
11
). From the unusual generating function in Eq. (
4
) and since the PDF of
J
takes the large deviation form

P(J)\sim e^{-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Phi(J)}
, the rate function
\Psi(u)
of the WASEP with a two-sided Bernoulli initial condition is determined by the following variational problem

\Psi(u)=\min_{J\in\mathbb{R}}[\Phi(J)+\frac{1}{2\nu}\min_{\omega\in[1,+\infty[}[F(\omega)-{\rm Li}_{2}(-u\omega e^{-2\nu J})]]

(SH.50)

where we recall that
F(\omega)=-\log\omega\log\alpha+\mathrm{Li}_{2}(\alpha)-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(1)+\mathrm{Li}_{2}(\frac{1}{\omega})

with
\alpha
being defined from the densities
\varrho_{1}
and
\varrho_{2}
as

\alpha=\frac{\left(1-\varrho_{1}\right)\varrho_{2}}{\varrho_{1}\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)}\quad,\quad 0<\alpha\leqslant 1

(SH.51)

Let us denote
\omega_{u,z}
the value of
\omega
which realizes
\min_{\omega}
in (
SH.50
) for a given
z=e^{-2\nu J}
.
One finds that it must obey
(\omega-1)(1+u\omega z)=\alpha\omega
. Hence it is only a function of the product
uz
, and
we denote
\omega_{u,z}=\omega_{uz}
. The correct root of this equation, i.e., the
branch which vanishes for
u=0
, reads

\omega=\omega_{u,z}=\omega_{uz}\quad,\quad\omega_{u}=\frac{\alpha-1+u+\sqrt{\left(\alpha+u-1\right)^{2}+4u}}{2u}=\frac{2}{1-\alpha-u+\sqrt{\left(\alpha+u-1\right)^{2}+4u}}

(SH.52)

Remark H.1
.

Note that
\omega_{u}
is well defined as long as
u\geqslant-(\sqrt{\alpha}-1)^{2}
,
with
\omega_{u}\geqslant 1
.

On the other hand taking a derivative of (
SH.50
) w.r.t.
u
it is sufficient to take only the explicit derivative
w.r.t.
u
, which leads to

\Psi^{\prime}(u)=\frac{1}{2\nu}\frac{\log(1+u\omega_{uz}z)}{u}\quad,\quad z=e^{-2\nu J}

(SH.53)

which shows one of the equalities in (
10
). Next we can
write the condition of minimization of (
SH.50
) w.r.t.
J
. This gives, since again
one can take only the explicit derivative w.r.t.
J

\Phi^{\prime}(J)=\log(1+u\omega_{uz}z)\quad,\quad z=e^{-2\nu J}

(SH.54)

showing another equality in (
10
). Next the equation (
9
) implies that

\phi(P)=\max_{J}[PJ-\Phi(J)]

(SH.55)

The value of
J
which realizes the maximum is thus such that

P=\Phi^{\prime}(J)\quad,\quad\phi^{\prime}(P)=J

(SH.56)

Finally let us return to (
SH.53
). One has

\log(1+u\omega_{uz}z)=2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)=P

(SH.57)

It turns out that, inserting (
SH.52
), the equation
1+u\omega_{uz}z=e^{P}
is inverted simply as

uz=(1-e^{-P})(e^{P}-\alpha)

(SH.58)

which, using
z=e^{-2\nu J}
and inserting
P=2\nu\Psi^{\prime}(u)

gives the last equation in (
11
).

Remark H.2
.

The step initial condition
\varrho_{1}=1
,
\varrho_{2}=0
implies
\alpha=0
and amounts to set

\omega_{uz}=1
with
F(1)=0
(which means that the variable
\omega
becomes deterministic).
All the above relations, as well as (
10
)–(
11
),
hold with this substitution as a special case.

Appendix I
Parametric representations

I.1
Parametric representations of
\Phi(J)

Knowing
\Psi(u)
, the rate function
\Phi(J)
of the PDF of the integrated current, and its derivative
\Phi^{\prime}(J)
,
have the parametric representation (obtained by varying
u
)

\displaystyle\Phi(J)=\Psi(u)-\frac{1}{2\nu}[F(\omega_{\zeta(u)})-{\rm Li}_{2}(-\zeta(u)\omega_{\zeta(u)})]

(SI.59)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime}(J)=\log(1+\zeta(u)\omega_{\zeta(u)})=2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)

(SI.60)

\displaystyle J=-\frac{1}{2\nu}\log\frac{\zeta(u)}{u}\quad,\quad\zeta(u)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(1-e^{-2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)})(e^{2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)}-\alpha)

(SI.61)

where the function
u\mapsto\omega_{u}
is given in (
SH.52
) and

\alpha=\frac{\left(1-\varrho_{1}\right)\varrho_{2}}{\varrho_{1}\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)}<1
.
To obtain this result we have performed the inverse Legendre transform of (
SH.50
), and used the relations in Section
H
,
denoting
uz=\zeta(u)
. The case of the step initial condition,
\alpha=0
,
is treated separately in Remark
S.1
.

Remark I.1
.

This is valid for
J>J_{c}
which corresponds to
u>u_{c}
and the main branch of
\Psi(u)

given by (
5
). The other branches are discussed
in Section
S
, and
\Phi(J)
is
given for
J>J_{c}
in Section
S.4

for
X=0
,
\varrho_{1}+\varrho_{2}=1
and in some parameter range defined in (
SS.438
).

I.2
Parametric representations of
\phi(P)

It is also possible to obtain a parametric representation of
\phi(P)
. We integrate the relation

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}(P)=J=-\frac{1}{2\nu}\log\left(\frac{(1-e^{-P})(e^{P}-\alpha)}{u}\right)

(SI.62)

to obtain

\begin{split}\phi(P)&=-\frac{1}{2\nu}\int_{0}^{P}\mathrm{d}P\log(\alpha(e^{-P}-1)+e^{P}-1))+\int_{0}^{u(P)}du(\partial_{u}(u\Psi^{\prime}(u))\log u\\
&=-\Psi(u(P))+\frac{P}{2\nu}\log u(P)-\frac{P^{2}}{4\nu}-\frac{1}{2\nu}\left(\text{Li}_{2}\left(\alpha e^{-P}\right)-\text{Li}_{2}\left(\alpha\right)\right)-\frac{1}{2\nu}\left(\text{Li}_{2}\left(e^{-P}\right)-\text{Li}_{2}\left(1\right)\right)\end{split}

(SI.63)

which provides the parametric representation of
\phi(P)
, where
u(P)
is defined by inverting the
equation
P=2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)
.

Appendix J
Explicit formula for the derivatives of the rate function
\Psi(u)

Let us recall the expression for the rate function
\Psi(u)
(which is an implicit function of the observation space-time point
X,T
)

\Psi(u)=-\frac{1}{2\nu}\int_{\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R}+\delta}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{2\mathbf{i}\pi y(1-y)}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\left(-u\frac{\varrho_{1}\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)(1-y)y}{\left(y-\varrho_{1}\right)\left(\varrho_{2}-y\right)}e^{-4\nu^{2}y(1-y)T+2\nu yX}\right)

(SJ.64)

where the integration contour on
y
is taken along
\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R}+\delta
with
\varrho_{2}<\delta<\varrho_{1}
.
To calculate the cumulants of the current it is useful to use the Taylor series of
\Psi
as

\begin{split}\Psi(u)&=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{u^{n}}{n!}\Psi^{(n)}(0)\quad,\quad\Psi^{(n)}(0)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Psi_{n}(X,T)\end{split}

(SJ.65)

and to first obtain an explicit formula for the multiple derivatives
\Psi^{(n)}(0)
for arbitrary
(X,T)
.
Using the series definition of the dilogarithm
{\rm Li}_{2}(z)=\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{z^{n}}{n^{2}}
we obtain

\begin{split}\Psi^{(n)}(0)&=(-1)^{n-1}(n-1)!\frac{(\varrho_{1}\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right))^{n}}{2\nu n}\int_{\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R}+\delta}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{2\mathbf{i}\pi}\frac{(y(1-y))^{n-1}}{\left(y-\varrho_{1}\right)^{n}\left(\varrho_{2}-y\right)^{n}}e^{-4\nu^{2}ny(1-y)T+2\nu nyX}\end{split}

(SJ.66)

To compute this integral it is useful to note that each term in the expansion (
SJ.65
) verifies the following heat equation

\partial_{T}\Psi_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\partial_{X}^{2}\Psi_{n}-2\nu\partial_{X}\Psi_{n}

(SJ.67)

where the diffusion coefficient depends on the order of the expansion
n
. Hence we can first compute the integral for
T=0
, which can be done using residues, and then propagate it
using the heat equation. We obtain the initial condition at
T=0
as follows.
Let us recall the formula for the residue of a multiple pole. One has, on a positively oriented closed contour
{\cal C}

around
z
, if
g(y)
is analytic within the region enclosed by the contour

\int_{\cal C}\frac{dy}{2\mathbf{i}\pi}\frac{g(y)}{(y-z)^{p}}=\frac{1}{(p-1)!}g^{(p-1)}(z)

(SJ.68)

Hence we find for
X<0
, closing the contour at infinity on the side
\Re\,y>0

\Psi^{(n)}(0)\underset{T=0,X<0}{=}(-1)^{n}\frac{\varrho_{1}^{n}\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)^{n}}{2\nu n}\frac{d^{n-1}}{dy^{n-1}}\left(\frac{e^{2\nu nyX}(y(1-y))^{n-1}}{(\varrho_{2}-y)^{n}}\right)|_{y=\varrho_{1}}

(SJ.69)

And for
X>0
, closing the contour at infinity on the side
\Re\,y<0

\Psi^{(n)}(0)\underset{T=0,X>0}{=}-\frac{\varrho_{1}^{n}\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)^{n}}{2\nu n}\frac{d^{n-1}}{dy^{n-1}}\left(\frac{e^{2\nu nyX}(y(1-y))^{n-1}}{(y-\varrho_{1})^{n}}\right)|_{y=\varrho_{2}}

(SJ.70)

Remark J.1
.

We can check that these expressions are continuous at
X=0
, however the derivative w.r.t
X
has a jump at
X=0
.

We then need to propagate this initial condition through the heat equation (
SJ.67
), using its associated kernel

G_{n}(X,T)=\sqrt{\frac{n}{4\pi T}}e^{-n\frac{(X-2\nu T)^{2}}{4T}}
. We thus propagates each initial condition on each half-space as

e^{2\nu nyX}\Theta(-X)\to\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}G_{n}(X-x^{\prime},T)e^{2\nu nyx^{\prime}}\Theta(-x^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}e^{2\nu ny(2\nu T(y-1)+X)}\text{Erfc}\left(\sqrt{\frac{n}{4T}}(2\nu T(2y-1)+X)\right)

(SJ.71)

and

e^{2\nu nyX}\Theta(X)\to\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}G_{n}(X-x^{\prime},T)e^{2\nu nyx^{\prime}}\Theta(x^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}e^{2\nu ny(2\nu T(y-1)+X)}\text{Erfc}\left(-\sqrt{\frac{n}{4T}}(2\nu T(2y-1)+X)\right)

(SJ.72)

Putting all together we obtain our final result

\begin{split}\Psi^{(n)}(0)=&-\frac{\varrho_{1}^{n}\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)^{n}}{4\nu n}\frac{d^{n-1}}{dy^{n-1}}\left(\frac{(y(1-y))^{n-1}e^{2\nu ny(2\nu T(y-1)+X)}}{(y-\varrho_{1})^{n}}\text{Erfc}\left(-\sqrt{\frac{n}{4T}}(2\nu T(2y-1)+X)\right)\right)|_{y=\varrho_{2}}\\
&+(-1)^{n}\frac{\varrho_{1}^{n}\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)^{n}}{4\nu n}\frac{d^{n-1}}{dy^{n-1}}\left(\frac{(y(1-y))^{n-1}e^{2\nu ny(2\nu T(y-1)+X)}}{(\varrho_{2}-y)^{n}}\text{Erfc}\left(\sqrt{\frac{n}{4T}}(2\nu T(2y-1)+X)\right)\right)|_{y=\varrho_{1}}\end{split}

(SJ.73)

Remark J.2
.

The step initial condition
is recovered as a special case, setting
\varrho_{1}=1
,
\varrho_{2}=0

\begin{split}\Psi^{(n)}(0)&=\frac{(-1)^{n-1}(n-1)!}{2\nu n}\int_{\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R}+\delta}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{2\mathbf{i}\pi}\frac{1}{y(1-y)}e^{-4\nu^{2}ny(1-y)T+2\nu nyX}\end{split}

(SJ.74)

At initial time
T=0
one finds

\Psi^{(n)}(0)\underset{T=0}{=}\frac{(-1)^{n-1}(n-1)!}{2\nu n}(\Theta(-X)e^{2\nu nX}+\Theta(X))

(SJ.75)

and for later times
T>0
one has

\Psi^{(n)}(0)=\frac{(-1)^{n-1}(n-1)!}{4\nu n}\left(e^{2\nu nX}{\rm Erfc}\left(\sqrt{\frac{n}{4T}}(2\nu T+X)\right)+{\rm Erfc}\left(\sqrt{\frac{n}{4T}}(2\nu T-X)\right)\right)

(SJ.76)

Appendix K
Cumulants of the integrated current
J

Using the Legendre transform equations (
10
)–(
11
) we can obtain the cumulants of the time-integrated current
J=-h
at any order, i.e., the coefficients

\kappa_{n}

\langle J^{n}\rangle^{c}=\varepsilon^{n-1}\kappa_{n}

(SK.77)

Note that
J
here denotes
J(X,T)
defined in Section
G
.

Remark K.1
.

Let us recall that the total flux in time
T
through point
X
is

Q_{T}(X)=J(X,T)-J(X,0)
, with
J(0,0)=0
. Hence
J(0,T)

is also the total flux
Q_{T}=Q_{T}(0)
through the origin. Knowledge of the
cumulants of
J(X,T)
alone does not allow to determine the cumulants of
Q_{T}(X)

for general
X
, except in two cases (i) for the step initial condition: in that case

J(X,0)=-X\theta(-X)
is deterministic and one has

\langle Q_{T}(X)^{n}\rangle^{c}=\langle J(X,T)^{n}\rangle^{c}
for
n\geqslant 2

(ii) for the stationary initial condition
\varrho_{1}=\varrho_{2}

in which case by translational invariance

\langle Q_{T}(X)^{n}\rangle^{c}=\langle Q_{T}(0)^{n}\rangle^{c}=\langle J(0,T)^{n}\rangle^{c}
.

To obtain the cumulants of
J
, we first express them in terms of the derivatives
\Psi^{(n)}(0)
of the rate function
\Psi(u)
. To obtain these relations we applied two equivalent methods.

1.

The first one is to invert, from (
10
), the series of
P=2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)
which gives
u=u_{P}
as
a series in
P
,
and then, from (
11
), to insert it into

\kappa_{n}=[\partial_{P}^{n-1}\Phi^{\prime}(P)]_{P=0}=-\frac{1}{2\nu}\left[\partial_{P}^{n-1}\left(\log((1-e^{-P})(e^{P}-\alpha))-\log u_{P}\right)\right]_{P=0}

(SK.78)

2.

The second one is to write using the Jacobian of the map
u\to P

\kappa_{n}=-\frac{1}{2\nu}\left[\left(\frac{1}{2\nu\partial_{u}(u\Psi^{\prime}(u))}\partial_{u}\right)^{n-1}\log\left(\frac{(1-e^{-2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)})(e^{2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)}-\alpha)}{u}\right)\right]_{u=0}

(SK.79)

We can further work on this expression to express the
n
-th cumulant as

\begin{split}\kappa_{n}&=-\frac{1}{2\nu}\left[\partial_{P}^{n-1}\log\left(\frac{(1-e^{-P})(e^{P}-\alpha)}{P}\right)\right]_{P=0}-\frac{1}{(2\nu)^{n}}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\partial_{u}(u\Psi^{\prime}(u))}\partial_{u}\right)^{n-1}\log(2\nu\Psi^{\prime}(u))\right]_{u=0}\\
\end{split}

(SK.80)

The first part can be computed exactly. Indeed, we can use the following Taylor expansions

\log\left(\frac{1-e^{-P}}{P}\right)=-\frac{P}{2}+\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{B_{2n}}{(2n)!2n}P^{2n},\quad\log(e^{P}-\alpha)=P+\sum_{n\geqslant 0}\frac{(-1)^{n+1}}{n!}{\rm Li}_{1-n}(\alpha)\,P^{n}

(SK.81)

where
B_{n}
are the Bernoulli numbers. This expansion is valid for
\alpha<1
. The limit
\alpha\to 1

is studied in Section
L
. Hence we obtain

\displaystyle\kappa_{n}=\kappa_{n}^{0}+\kappa_{n}^{1}\quad,\quad\kappa^{0}_{1}=-\frac{1}{2\nu}\log(1-\alpha)

(SK.82)

\displaystyle\kappa^{0}_{n=2q}=-\frac{1}{2\nu}({\rm Li}_{2-n}(\alpha)+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{n,2})\quad,\quad\kappa^{0}_{n=2q+1}=\frac{1}{2\nu}({\rm Li}_{2-n}(\alpha)-\frac{B_{n-1}}{n-1})\quad,\quad q\geqslant 1

(SK.83)

\displaystyle\kappa^{1}_{n}=-\frac{1}{(2\nu)^{n}}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\partial_{u}(u\Psi^{\prime}(u))}\partial_{u}\right)^{n-1}\log(2\nu\Psi^{\prime}(u))\right]_{u=0}

(SK.84)

We recall that
\alpha
is defined in Eq. (
SH.51
). Defining the ratios
r_{m}=\Psi^{(m)}(0)/\Psi^{\prime}(0)^{m}
, we obtain the first six cumulants as

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}=-\frac{1}{2\nu}\log\left(2\nu(1-\alpha)\Psi^{\prime}(0)\right)\quad,\quad\kappa_{2}=\frac{1+\alpha}{4\nu(\alpha-1)}-\frac{r_{2}}{4\nu^{2}}

(SK.85)

\displaystyle\kappa_{3}=-\frac{\alpha^{2}-14\alpha+1}{24(\alpha-1)^{2}\nu}+\frac{3r_{2}^{2}-r_{3}}{8\nu^{3}}\quad,\quad\kappa_{4}=\frac{\alpha(\alpha+1)}{2(\alpha-1)^{3}\nu}+\frac{-20r_{2}^{3}+12r_{3}r_{2}-r_{4}}{16\nu^{4}}

(SK.86)

\displaystyle\kappa_{5}=\frac{1}{2\nu}\left(\frac{\alpha(\alpha(\alpha+4)+1)}{(\alpha-1)^{4}}+\frac{1}{120}\right)-\frac{1}{(2\nu)^{5}}\left(r_{5}-5\left(42r_{2}^{4}-36r_{3}r_{2}^{2}+4r_{4}r_{2}+3r_{3}^{2}\right)\right)

(SK.87)

\displaystyle\kappa_{6}=\frac{1}{2\nu}\frac{\alpha(\alpha+1)(\alpha(\alpha+10)+1)}{(\alpha-1)^{5}}-\frac{1}{(2\nu)^{6}}\left(6\left(504r_{2}^{5}-560r_{3}r_{2}^{3}+70r_{4}r_{2}^{2}+5(21r_{3}^{2}-r_{5})r_{2}-10r_{3}r_{4}\right)+r_{6}\right)

We can now insert the explicit formula for the
\Psi^{(n)}(0)
obtained in the previous section and
obtain the explicit formula for the cumulants for arbitrary
(X,T)
and
(\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2})
. Let us
give some examples

K.1
First moment

The average integrated current at the space-time observation point
(X,T)
reads

\displaystyle\langle J(X,T)\rangle=\kappa_{1}=-\frac{1}{2\nu}\log\bigg(\frac{1}{2}e^{2\nu\varrho_{1}(2\nu(\varrho_{1}-1)T+X)}\text{Erfc}(\sqrt{\frac{1}{4T}}(2\nu(2\varrho_{1}-1)T+X))

(SK.88)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}e^{2\nu\varrho_{2}(2\nu(\varrho_{2}-1)T+X)}\text{Erfc}(-\sqrt{\frac{1}{4T}}(2\nu(2\varrho_{2}-1)T+X))\bigg)

and we recall that
\langle J(X,0)\rangle=-\varrho_{1}X\Theta(-X)-\varrho_{2}X\Theta(X)
. We now obtain the two special cases of step and stationary initial conditions.

•

In the stationary limit
\varrho_{2}=\varrho_{1}=\varrho
, it simplifies as

\langle J(X,T)\rangle=2\nu\varrho(1-\varrho)T-\varrho X

(SK.89)

•

For the step (determinist) initial condition,
\varrho_{2}=0
and
\varrho_{1}=1
, one finds

\langle J(X,T)\rangle=-\frac{\log\left(\frac{1}{2}\text{Erfc}\left(-\sqrt{\frac{1}{4T}}(X-2\nu T)\right)+\frac{1}{2}e^{2\nu X}\text{Erfc}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{4T}}(2\nu T+X)\right)\right)}{2\nu}

(SK.90)

which becomes for
X=0

\langle J\rangle=-\frac{\log(\text{Erfc}(\nu\sqrt{T}))}{2\nu}=\begin{cases}\frac{\sqrt{T}}{\sqrt{\pi}}+\frac{\nu T}{\pi}+\mathcal{O}\left(\nu^{2}\right)\quad,\quad&\nu\to 0\\
\frac{\nu T}{2}+\frac{1}{2\nu}\log(\nu\sqrt{T}\sqrt{\pi})+\mathcal{O}(1/\nu^{3})\quad,\quad&\nu\to+\infty\\
\frac{\log 2}{2|\nu|}+\mathcal{O}(e^{-T\nu^{2}})\quad,\quad&\nu\to-\infty\\
\end{cases}

(SK.91)

which is an increasing function of
\nu
. For
\nu=0
setting
T=1
we find

\langle J\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}
in agreement with the result for the SSEP in

(
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009a
, Eqs. (1-3))
, see also (
SQ.356
).
Note that for the step initial condition the integrated current
J
must be positive for
X=0
. Furthermore
J
vanishes when
\nu\to-\infty
, since in that case the drift forbids the particles
to cross the origin.

•

More generally for arbitrary densities, in the SSEP limit
\nu\to 0
we obtain

\langle J(X,T)\rangle\underset{\nu=0}{=}\left(\varrho_{1}-\varrho_{2}\right)\sqrt{T}{\cal G}\left(\frac{X}{2\sqrt{T}}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\left(\varrho_{1}+\varrho_{2}\right)X

(SK.92)

where here and below we define the function

{\cal G}(y)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=y{\rm Erf}(y)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-y^{2}}

(SK.93)

For
X=0
,
T=1
one finds
\langle J\rangle=\frac{\varrho_{1}-\varrho_{2}}{\sqrt{\pi}}

in agreement with
(
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009a
;
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009b
, Eqs. (1-3))
.

Remark K.2
.

Equation (
SK.89
) for
X=0
matches the result

\langle{\sf J}_{\sf T}(0)\rangle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\langle J\rangle=\sqrt{\frac{{\sf T}}{T}}2\nu\varrho(1-\varrho)T=(R-L){\sf T}\varrho(1-\varrho)
for the ASEP microscopic
integrated current
\langle{\sf J}_{\sf T}(0)\rangle
in
Ferrari and Fontes 1994
.

Remark K.3
.

The error functions in (
SK.88
) show diffusive broadening around the space-time rays

X=v_{i}T
, where
v_{i}=\nu\sigma^{\prime}(\varrho_{i})=2\nu(1-2\varrho_{i})
are the sound velocities
obtained by linearizing (
1
), and become discontinuous as a
function of
X/T
as
T\to+\infty
. In Ref.
Aggarwal and Borodin 2019
the
large time limit of the ASEP is studied, and a phase transition in the
fluctuations of the current
\langle{\sf J}_{t}(m)\rangle
occurs along the corresponding rays

m={\sf T}(1-2\varrho)
.

Remark K.4
.

For large WASEP time
T
, and for
\nu>0
, the mean current (
SK.88
) becomes, denoting
v=X/T

\displaystyle\frac{\langle J(X,T)\rangle}{T}\underset{T\gg 1}{\simeq}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}2\nu\varrho_{1}(1-\varrho_{1})-\varrho_{1}v\quad,&\quad v<2\nu(1-2\varrho_{1})\\
\frac{(v-2\nu)^{2}}{8\nu}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \quad,&\quad 2\nu(1-2\varrho_{2})<v<2\nu(1-2\varrho_{1})\\
2\nu\varrho_{2}(1-\varrho_{2})-\varrho_{2}v\quad,&\quad v>2\nu(1-2\varrho_{2})\end{cases}

(SK.94)

The derivative of the profile remains continuous in the limit.
For
\nu<0
there is a shock in the limit, and one finds instead

\displaystyle\frac{\langle J(X,T)\rangle}{T}\underset{T\gg 1}{\simeq}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}2\nu\varrho_{1}(1-\varrho_{1})-\varrho_{1}v\quad,\quad v<2|\nu|(\varrho_{1}+\varrho_{2}-1)\\
2\nu\varrho_{2}(1-\varrho_{2})-\varrho_{2}v\quad,\quad v>2|\nu|(\varrho_{1}+\varrho_{2}-1)\end{cases}

(SK.95)

The occurrence of a shock in ASEP or TASEP in that case is well known, see e.g.,
Zhang 2024
and
references therein.

K.2
Second cumulant

The expression for arbitrary
\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2}
is already quite bulky.
It reads for
X=0
and
T=1

\displaystyle\kappa_{2}=\frac{1}{2\nu(\varrho_{1}-\varrho_{2})}(\varrho_{1}\varrho_{2}-\frac{\varrho_{1}+\varrho_{2}}{2}+\frac{A+B}{C})

(SK.96)

\displaystyle A=-4\nu e^{-2\nu^{2}}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}(\varrho_{1}-\varrho_{2})(\varrho_{1}(\varrho_{1}-1)+\varrho_{2}(\varrho_{2}-1))

(SK.97)

\displaystyle B=(\varrho_{1}+\varrho_{2}-2\varrho_{1}\varrho_{2})(g_{\nu}(\varrho_{1})+g_{-\nu}(\varrho_{2}))+8\nu^{2}(\varrho_{1}-\varrho_{2})(\tilde{g}_{\nu}(\varrho_{1})-\tilde{g}_{-\nu}(\varrho_{2}))

(SK.98)

\displaystyle C=(g_{\frac{\nu}{\sqrt{2}}}(\varrho_{1})+g_{-\frac{\nu}{\sqrt{2}}}(\varrho_{2}))^{2}

(SK.99)

\displaystyle g_{\nu}(\varrho)=e^{8\nu^{2}\varrho(\varrho-1)}{\rm Erfc}(\sqrt{2}\nu(2\varrho-1))\quad,\quad\tilde{g}_{\nu}(\varrho)=\varrho(\varrho-1)(2\varrho-1)g_{\nu}(\varrho)

(SK.100)

We now obtain the three special cases of the SSEP limit and then the step and stationary initial conditions.

•

In the SSEP limit
\nu\to 0
for arbitrary densities it gives

\kappa_{2}\underset{\nu=0}{=}\frac{-\sqrt{2}\left(\varrho_{1}-\varrho_{2}\right){}^{2}+2\varrho_{1}-4\varrho_{1}\varrho_{2}+2\varrho_{2}}{2\sqrt{\pi}}

(SK.101)

in agreement with
(
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009a
;
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009b
, Eqs. (1-3))
.

•

For the step (determinist) initial condition,
\varrho_{2}=0
and
\varrho_{1}=1
, one finds

\kappa_{2}=\frac{1}{4\nu}\bigg(\frac{2\left(\text{Erfc}\left(\frac{2\nu T-X}{\sqrt{2T}}\right)+e^{4\nu X}\text{Erfc}\left(\frac{2\nu T+X}{\sqrt{2T}}\right)\right)}{\left(\text{Erfc}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{4T}}(2\nu T-X)\right)+e^{2\nu X}\text{Erfc}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{4T}}(2\nu T+X)\right)\right)^{2}}-1\bigg)

(SK.102)

which for
X=0
,
T=1
simplifies to

\kappa_{2}=\frac{\frac{\text{Erfc}\left(\sqrt{2}\nu\right)}{\text{Erfc}(\nu)^{2}}-1}{4\nu}=\begin{cases}\frac{\left(2-\sqrt{2}\right)}{2\sqrt{\pi}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\nu\right)\\
\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\nu}\right)\quad,\quad&\nu\to+\infty\\
\frac{1}{8|\nu|}+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\nu^{2}})\quad,\quad&\nu\to-\infty\\
\end{cases}

(SK.103)

which is an increasing function of
\nu
.

•

In the stationary limit
\varrho_{1}=\varrho_{2}=\varrho
, it simplifies for arbitrary
X,T
as

\displaystyle\kappa_{2}=\varrho(1-\varrho)\left((2\nu(2\varrho-1)T+X)\text{Erf}\left(\frac{2\nu(2\varrho-1)T+X}{2\sqrt{T}}\right)+\frac{2\sqrt{T}e^{-\frac{(2\nu(2\varrho-1)T+X)^{2}}{4T}}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\right)

(SK.104)

which recovers the expression (
16
) given in the text.
As a function of
X
it has a minimum for
X=2\nu(1-2\varrho)T
.
It has the symmetry
(\varrho,X)\to(1-\varrho,-X)
.
For
X=0
,
\kappa_{2}
is even in
\nu
and has its minimum for
\nu=0
. Setting
T=1
one finds the asymptotics
for fixed
\varrho\neq 1/2
and large
\nu

\kappa_{2}=\begin{cases}\frac{2\varrho(1-\varrho)}{\sqrt{\pi}}+\mathcal{O}(\nu^{2})\quad,\quad&\nu\to 0\\
2|\nu|\varrho(1-\varrho)|1-2\varrho|+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\nu^{2}(1-2\varrho)^{2}})\quad,\quad&\nu\to\pm\infty\end{cases}

(SK.105)

As in Remark
K.2
, the limit
|\nu|\to+\infty
matches
the result

\langle{\sf J}_{\sf T}(0)^{2}\rangle\simeq|R-L|\varrho(1-\varrho)|(1-2\varrho)|{\sf T}

for the second cumulant of the ASEP microscopic
integrated current through the origin in
Ferrari and Fontes 1994
. However, note that

\kappa_{2}
simplifies for
\varrho=1/2
, in which case
\kappa_{2}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}
for arbitrary
\nu
. This value does not match (
SK.105
) at large
\nu
. Indeed, the correction terms in (
SK.105
)
cannot be neglected when
\varrho\to 1/2
. As a result, there is a crossover for

\varrho-1/2\sim 1/\nu
at large
\nu
. This crossover is precisely the
crossover from stationary to droplet initial conditions in the KPZ limit,
obtained in Eq. (
SP.336
) by increasing
\tilde{w}
from zero to
+\infty
,
with
\varrho_{1,2}=\frac{1}{2}\pm\frac{\tilde{w}}{2\nu}
.
One can check that
\kappa_{2}
in Eq. (
SP.336
) indeed varies from

\kappa_{2}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}
for
\tilde{w}=0
,
to
\kappa_{2}=\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}
for
\tilde{w}\to+\infty

which is the result for the step initial condition, see (
SK.103
).

K.3
Third cumulant

The expression for arbitrary
\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2}
is very bulky.

•

For the step initial condition,
\varrho_{2}=0
and
\varrho_{1}=1
, one finds for
X=0
and
T=1

\kappa_{3}=\frac{f(\nu)-1}{24\nu}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ f(\nu)=\frac{9\text{Erfc}\left(\sqrt{2}\nu\right)^{2}-8\text{Erfc}(\nu)\text{Erfc}\left(\sqrt{3}\nu\right)}{\text{Erfc}(\nu)^{4}}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \kappa_{3}=\begin{cases}\frac{6-9\sqrt{2}+4\sqrt{3}}{6\sqrt{\pi}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\nu\right)\\
\left(\frac{3}{16}-\frac{1}{3\sqrt{3}}\right)\pi\nu+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\nu}\right),\quad&\nu\to+\infty\\
\frac{1}{32|\nu|}+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\nu^{2}}),\quad&\nu\to-\infty\end{cases}

(SK.106)

which has a maximum equal to
\kappa_{3}^{\rm\max}=0.0239568
at
\nu=-0.715953
.

•

In the stationary limit
\varrho_{1}=\varrho_{2}=\varrho
one finds, for
X=0
and
T=1

\begin{split}\kappa_{3}&=2\nu(\varrho-1)\varrho\left(6(\varrho-1)\varrho\left(-\nu^{2}(1-2\varrho)^{2}+\frac{e^{-2\nu^{2}(1-2\varrho)^{2}}}{\pi}-1\right)-1\right)\\
&+12\nu^{3}(\varrho-1)^{2}\varrho^{2}(2\varrho-1)^{2}\text{Erf}(\nu-2\nu\varrho)^{2}+\frac{24\nu^{2}(\varrho-1)^{2}\varrho^{2}(1-2\varrho)e^{-\nu^{2}(1-2\varrho)^{2}}\text{Erf}(\nu-2\nu\varrho)}{\sqrt{\pi}}\end{split}

(SK.107)

which is odd in
\nu
, and invariant by
\varrho\to 1-\varrho
. It behaves at small asymmetry
\nu
as

\kappa_{3}\underset{|\nu|\ll 1}{=}-\frac{2\nu((\varrho-1)\varrho(6\pi(\varrho-1)\varrho-6(\varrho-1)\varrho+\pi))}{\pi}+\mathcal{O}(\nu^{3})

(SK.108)

and at large asymmetry
\nu\to\pm\infty
, for
\varrho\neq 1/2
, as

\kappa_{3}\underset{|\nu|\gg 1}{\simeq}2\nu(1-\varrho)\varrho(6(\varrho-1)\varrho+1)+\mathcal{O}(e^{-(2\varrho-1)^{2}\nu^{2}})

(SK.109)

which in principle could be matched to third cumulant for the ASEP, as above.
Again
\kappa_{3}
simplifies for
\varrho=1/2
in which case
\kappa_{3}=-\frac{(\pi-3)\nu}{4\pi}
for arbitrary
\nu
, and again it does not match (
SK.109
) at large
\nu
.
The crossover for
\varrho-1/2\sim 1/\nu
at large
\nu

is discussed in the KPZ limit in Eq. (
SP.338
)
as a function of
\tilde{w}
. Again one finds a crossover from
the value at
\varrho=1/2
for
\tilde{w}=0
, to the

\nu\to+\infty
asymptotics for the step initial condition displayed in (
SK.106
),
for
\tilde{w}\to+\infty
.

For fixed
\varrho
,
\kappa_{3}
has a rich behavior as a function of
\nu
.
Since
\kappa_{3}
is odd let us discuss
\nu>0
.
One can already
see from the large
\nu
asymptotics in (
SK.109
) that for

\varrho\in[1-\varrho_{c},\varrho_{c}]
the prefactor is negative, and
\kappa_{3}\to-\infty

as
\nu\to+\infty
, while for
\varrho\in[0,1-\varrho_{c}]\cup[\varrho_{c},1]

one has
\kappa_{3}\to-\infty
as
\nu\to+\infty
. Here one has
\varrho_{c}=\frac{1}{6}(3+\sqrt{3})=0.788675
. In addition, as
\varrho
increases from
1/2
a global maximum of

\kappa_{3}
for
\nu>0
appears when
the coefficient of the small
\nu
asymptotics in (
SK.109
)
changes sign, i.e for
\varrho=\varrho^{\prime}_{c}=0.574227
. Beyond this
point
\kappa_{3}
becomes non monotonic. This global maximum becomes
local for
\varrho>\varrho_{c}
and completely disappears for some
value of
\varrho
in
[\varrho_{c},1]
, so that
\kappa_{3}
becomes again
monotonic as a function of
\nu
.

Remark K.5
.

One can compare with the very recent result of
Berlioz et al. 2024
for the stationary initial condition.
For models with
D(\varrho)=1
, they read

\displaystyle\kappa_{2}=\sigma(\varrho){\cal G}(y=\frac{\nu}{2}\sigma^{\prime}(\varrho))\quad,\quad{\cal G}(y)=y{\rm Erf}(y)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-y^{2}}

(SK.110)

\displaystyle\kappa_{3}=\frac{\nu}{4}\sigma(\varrho)(\sigma^{\prime}(\varrho)^{2}+\sigma(\varrho)\sigma^{\prime\prime}(\varrho)(1+3(y^{2}-{\cal G}(y)^{2}))

(SK.111)

Inserting
\sigma(\varrho)=2\varrho(1-\varrho)
one finds agreement with our results for
X=0
and
T=1
.

K.4
Fourth cumulant

We restrict here to the formula for the step initial condition as well as the stationary one.

•

For the step initial condition for
X=0
and
T=1
we obtain

\kappa_{4}=\frac{5\text{Erfc}\left(\sqrt{2}\nu\right)^{3}-8\text{Erfc}(\nu)\text{Erfc}\left(\sqrt{3}\nu\right)\text{Erfc}\left(\sqrt{2}\nu\right)+3\text{Erfc}(\nu)^{2}\text{Erfc}(2\nu)}{4\nu\text{Erfc}(\nu)^{6}}

(SK.112)

It is negative for
\nu<\nu^{*}=2.79349
and positive for
\nu>\nu^{*}
.
It has a negative minimum for
\nu=0.762317

of value
\kappa_{4}^{\rm min}=-0.0151279
. One finds the asymptotics

\kappa_{4}=\begin{cases}\frac{-4-7\sqrt{2}+8\sqrt{3}}{2\sqrt{\pi}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\nu\right)\quad,\quad&\nu\to 0\\
\frac{1}{48}\left(18+15\sqrt{2}-16\sqrt{6}\right)\pi^{3/2}\nu^{2}+\mathcal{O}(1)\quad,\quad&\nu\to+\infty\end{cases}

(SK.113)

and it vanishes for
\nu\to-\infty
. The
value for
\nu=0
recovers the one for the SSEP, obtained from

(
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009a
;
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009b
, Eqs. (1-3))
.

•

In the stationary limit
\varrho_{1}=\varrho_{2}=\varrho
,
X=0
and
T=1
, with some help from
Mathematica, we find, with the same notations as in (
SK.110
)

\displaystyle\kappa_{4}=-\sqrt{2}{\cal G}\left(\sqrt{2}y\right)\sigma(\varrho)^{2}\left(2\nu^{2}\left(3-\left(4y^{2}+9\right)\sigma(\varrho)\right)+3\right)+20\nu^{2}{\cal G}(y)^{3}\sigma(\varrho)^{3}

(SK.114)
