\displaystyle+{\cal G}(y)\sigma(\varrho)\left(12\nu^{2}\sigma(\varrho)\left(1-3\left(y^{2}+1\right)\sigma(\varrho)\right)+1\right)+\frac{e^{-2y^{2}}\sigma(\varrho)^{2}\left(-2\nu^{2}+2y^{2}+3\right)}{\sqrt{2\pi}}

(SK.115)

i.e., with
\sigma(\varrho)=2\varrho(1-\varrho)
and
y=\frac{\nu}{2}\sigma^{\prime}(\varrho)
.

Figure SK.1:
Plot of the fourth cumulant
\kappa_{4}
in the stationary limit
\varrho_{1}=\varrho_{2}=\varrho
for
X=0
and
T=1
as a function of
\nu
for various values of
\varrho=\{0.09,0.1,0.35\}
.
The behavior at infinity changes at
\varrho\approx 0.0917
and it can exhibit multiple extrema.

It is an even function of
\nu
and invariant by
\varrho\to 1-\varrho
.
Setting
\nu=0
one finds

\kappa_{4}\underset{\nu=0}{=}\frac{2(1-\varrho)\varrho\left(3\sqrt{2}(\varrho-1)\varrho+1\right)}{\sqrt{\pi}}

(SK.116)

which agrees with the result for the SSEP obtained in
(
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009a
;
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009b
, Eqs. (1-3))
. Moreover, it
agrees with the general result for the fourth cumulant for
\nu=0
given in

(
Grabsch and Bénichou 2024
, Eq. (S61) in the Supp. Mat)
, which reads for
general
\sigma(\varrho)
and
D(\varrho)=1

\displaystyle\kappa_{4}=\frac{2\sigma(\varrho)\sigma^{\prime}(\varrho)^{2}+(3\sqrt{2}-4)\sigma(\varrho)^{2}\sigma^{\prime\prime}(\varrho)}{8\sqrt{\pi}}

(SK.117)

which for the SSEP,
\sigma(\varrho)=2\varrho(1-\varrho)
reduces to (
SK.116
). We note once again that for
\varrho=1/2
the result (
SK.114
) simplifies
for all
\nu

\kappa_{4}\underset{\varrho=1/2}{=}\frac{4\left(5+\left(\sqrt{2}-3\right)\pi\right)\nu^{2}+\left(4-3\sqrt{2}\right)\pi}{8\pi^{3/2}}

(SK.118)

although now it depends on
\nu
, and behaves as
\mathcal{O}(\nu^{2})
at large
\nu
.
By contrast the asymptotics for
\nu\to+\infty
and fixed
\varrho\neq 1/2
is quite different

\kappa_{4}\simeq 2\nu(1-\varrho)\varrho|2\varrho-1|(12(\varrho-1)\varrho+1)

(SK.119)

i.e., it is linear in
\nu
. Again that discrepancy is related to the KPZ crossover,
see discussion above and (
SP.339
).
The behavior of
\kappa_{4}
as a function of
\nu
is again quite rich.
Again the sign of the divergence at infinity changes at
\varrho=\varrho^{\prime}_{c}=\frac{1}{6}(3+\sqrt{6})=0.908248

and
\varrho=1-\varrho^{\prime}_{c}
.
One observes several extrema for a window
of
\varrho
, see Fig.
SK.1
.

Remark K.6
.

For
\varrho_{1}=\varrho_{2}=\varrho=1/2
we conjecture that the cumulants of
J=J(0,T)
are simply polynomials in
\nu
.
Our results are consistent with
\kappa_{n}
being a polynomial of degree
n-1
in
\nu
,
for
n\geqslant 2
(even in
\nu
for
n
even and odd in
\nu
for
n
odd).

Remark K.7
.

In this section we have first obtained the cumulants for
\varrho_{1}\neq\varrho_{2}
and on their expressions
we have performed the limit
\varrho_{1}\to\varrho_{2}=\varrho
. When performing these limits
we have noted that as
\alpha\to 1
all divergent parts of the rational functions cancel with the divergent
part of the second term in the cumulant coming for the
r_{k}
. The
r_{k}
have an expansion
r_{k}=1/\eta^{k-1}+\dots
with
\eta=1-\alpha
.
To obtain
\tilde{\kappa}_{2}
one only needs
r_{2}
to order
\mathcal{O}(1)
. To obtain
\tilde{\kappa}_{3}
one needs
r_{2}^{2}
and
r_{3}
to order
\mathcal{O}(1)

which means one needs
r_{2}
to order
\mathcal{O}(\eta)
. To obtain
\tilde{\kappa}_{4}
one needs
r_{2}^{3}
and
r_{3}r_{2}
and
r_{4}
to order
\mathcal{O}(1)

which means one needs
r_{2}
to order
\mathcal{O}(\eta^{2})
and
r_{3}
to order
\mathcal{O}(\eta)
and so on.
In the next Section we establish a different method which addresses directly the case
\varrho_{1}=\varrho_{2}

Appendix L
Stationary limit
\varrho_{1}=\varrho_{2}

In this Section we address directly the stationary limit
\varrho_{1}=\varrho_{2}
. In that limit however
the rate function
\Psi(u)

is not analytic in
u
anymore around
u=0
, but admits instead an expansion of powers of
\sqrt{u}
. To see that, let us set formally
\alpha=1
. One has

\omega=\omega_{uz}=\frac{uz+\sqrt{u^{2}z^{2}+4uz}}{2uz}=\frac{2}{-uz+\sqrt{u^{2}z^{2}+4uz}}\underset{u\to 0}{\simeq}\frac{1}{\sqrt{uz}}

(SL.120)

A consequence of this is that from (
10
) we have

\Psi^{\prime}(u)=\frac{1}{2\nu}\frac{\log(1+u\omega_{uz}z)}{u}\underset{u\to 0}{\simeq}\frac{1}{2\nu}\sqrt{\frac{z}{u}}

(SL.121)

where
z=z_{u}
has a finite value for
u=0
. Hence we see that
u\Psi^{\prime}(u)
now
starts as
\sqrt{u}
. Note that a similar behavior in
\sqrt{u}
at small
u
was
obtained for the rate function of the weak noise theory stationary KPZ
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
, so it
is not surprising that it appears here.

L.1
Variational problem

We study here the variational problem (
SH.50
). We have that in the limit
\alpha\to 1

\begin{split}F(\omega)-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-u\omega z)&=\mathrm{Li}_{2}\left(\frac{\sqrt{(uz)^{2}+4uz}-uz}{2}\right)-\mathrm{Li}_{2}\left(-\frac{\sqrt{(uz)^{2}+4uz}+uz}{2}\right)\\
&\underset{u\to 0}{=}2\sqrt{uz}-\frac{(uz)^{3/2}}{36}+\frac{3(uz)^{5/2}}{1600}+\mathcal{O}\left(u^{7/2}\right)\\
&\underset{u\to+\infty}{=}\frac{\log(uz)^{2}}{2}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{u}\right)\end{split}

(SL.122)

The variational problem then formally becomes, recalling that
z=e^{-2\nu J}
,

\Psi(u)=\min_{J}\left[\Phi(J)+\frac{1}{2\nu}\left[\mathrm{Li}_{2}\left(\frac{\sqrt{(uz)^{2}+4uz}-uz}{2}\right)-\mathrm{Li}_{2}\left(-\frac{\sqrt{(uz)^{2}+4uz}+uz}{2}\right)\right]\right]

(SL.123)

L.2
Large deviation function
\psi(v)

Consider the case
\varrho_{1}=\varrho_{2}
.
Let us write
\Psi(u)=\psi(v)
with
v=\sqrt{u}
and explore the consequences
for the cumulant generating function
\phi(P)
. If
\psi(v)
starts as
v
at small
v
then upon inversion

P=2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)=\nu v\psi^{\prime}(v)\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad v=v_{P}

(SL.124)

one has that
v_{P}\simeq P/(\nu\psi^{\prime}(0))
is linear in
P
hence one can take the limit in

\phi^{\prime}(P)=-\frac{1}{2\nu}\log\left(\frac{(1-e^{-P})(e^{P}-\alpha)}{v_{P}^{2}}\right)\to-\frac{1}{2\nu}\log\left(\frac{(1-e^{-P})(e^{P}-1)}{v_{P}^{2}}\right)

(SL.125)

which will remain finite with a finite series in
P^{n}
as we want to obtain well defined cumulants. We can
conjecture
that this is the correct limit
\varrho_{1}=\varrho_{2}
.
Let us check that it is correct.

Let us write

v\psi^{\prime}(v)=2u\Psi^{\prime}(u)=\frac{1}{\nu}\int_{\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R}+\delta}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{2\mathbf{i}\pi y(1-y)}\log\left(1+v^{2}\frac{\varrho_{1}\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)(1-y)y}{\left(\varrho_{1}-y\right)\left(y-\varrho_{2}\right)}e^{-4\nu^{2}y(1-y)T+2\nu yX}\right)

(SL.126)

Let us take another derivative w.r.t.
v
and note that here we can set
\varrho_{1}=\varrho_{2}

\begin{split}v\psi^{\prime\prime}(v)+\psi^{\prime}(v)&=\frac{1}{\nu}\int_{\varrho+\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{2\mathbf{i}\pi}\frac{2v}{y(1-y)v^{2}-\frac{(y-\varrho)^{2}}{\varrho(1-\varrho)}e^{4\nu^{2}y(1-y)T-2\nu yX}}\\
&=\frac{1}{\nu}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{2\pi}\frac{2v}{(\varrho+\mathbf{i}k)(1-\varrho-\mathbf{i}k)v^{2}+\frac{k^{2}}{\varrho(1-\varrho)}e^{4\nu^{2}(\varrho+\mathbf{i}k)(1-\varrho-\mathbf{i}k)T-2\nu(\varrho+\mathbf{i}k)X}}\end{split}

(SL.127)

where we have set
y=\varrho+\mathbf{i}k
. It is easy to see that
\psi^{\prime}(0)
is finite.
Indeed using

\lim_{v\to 0^{+}}\frac{v}{av^{2}+bk^{2}}\to\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{ab}}\delta(k)

(SL.128)

and taking
a=\varrho(1-\varrho)
and
b=\frac{1}{\varrho(1-\varrho)}e^{4\nu^{2}\varrho(1-\varrho)T-2\nu\varrho X}
we obtain

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(0)=\frac{1}{2\pi\nu}\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt{ab}}=\frac{1}{\nu}e^{-2\nu^{2}\varrho(1-\varrho)T+\nu\varrho X}

(SL.129)

which incidentally satisfies the heat equation

\partial_{T}=2\partial_{X}^{2}-2\nu\partial_{X}
,
corresponding to formally setting
n=1/2
in (
SJ.67
).
From
\psi^{\prime}(0)
we can recover the first moment of
J
.
Using (
SL.125
) and
v_{P}\simeq P/(\nu\psi^{\prime}(0))
we obtain

\langle J\rangle=\phi^{\prime}(P)|_{P=0}=-\frac{1}{\nu}\log\nu\psi^{\prime}(0)=2\nu\varrho(1-\varrho)T-\varrho X

(SL.130)

which is the correct result (
SK.89
).

We will compute explicitly higher derivatives of
\psi(v)
below and
obtain some cumulants of
J
. Before that, let us give the general relation
between cumulants and derivatives of
\psi(v)
.

L.3
General relation between the cumulants
\kappa_{n}
and the derivatives
\psi^{(n)}(0)

The cumulants can be obtained using the formula (
SK.79
), which becomes

\kappa_{n}=-\frac{1}{2\nu}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\nu\partial_{v}(v\psi^{\prime}(v))}\partial_{v}\right)^{n-1}\log\left(\frac{(1-e^{-\nu v\psi^{\prime}(v)})(e^{\nu v\psi^{\prime}(v)}-1)}{v^{2}}\right)\right]_{v=0}

(SL.131)

As in the non-stationary initial condition, we can slightly simplify (
SL.131
) as follows.

\begin{split}\kappa_{n}&=-\frac{1}{2\nu}\left[\partial_{P}^{n-1}\log\left(\frac{(1-e^{-P})(e^{P}-1)}{P^{2}}\right)\right]_{P=0}-\frac{1}{\nu^{n}}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\partial_{v}(v\psi^{\prime}(v))}\partial_{v}\right)^{n-1}\log(\nu\psi^{\prime}(v))\right]_{v=0}\end{split}

(SL.132)

We now can use the Taylor expansion

\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{(1-e^{-P})(e^{P}-1)}{P^{2}}\right)=\log\left(\frac{\sinh(P/2)}{P/2}\right)=\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{B_{2n}}{(2n)!2n}P^{2n}

(SL.133)

to evaluate explicitly the first part of the
n
-th cumulant. One finds

\kappa_{n}=-\frac{1}{\nu}\frac{B_{n-1}}{n-1}\delta_{n\geqslant 3,{\rm odd}}-\frac{1}{\nu^{n}}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\partial_{v}(v\psi^{\prime}(v))}\partial_{v}\right)^{n-1}\log(\nu\psi^{\prime}(v))\right]_{v=0}

(SL.134)

and the lowest order formula

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}=-\frac{1}{\nu}\log(\psi^{\prime}(0)\nu)\quad,\quad\kappa_{2}=-\frac{R_{2}}{\nu^{2}}\quad,\quad R_{n}=\frac{\psi^{(n)}(0)}{\psi^{\prime}(0)^{n}}

(SL.135)

\displaystyle\kappa_{3}=-\frac{\nu^{2}-36R_{2}^{2}+12R_{3}}{12\nu^{3}}=-\frac{1}{12\nu}+\frac{1}{\nu^{3}}(3R_{2}^{2}-R_{3})\quad,\quad\kappa_{4}=-\frac{20R_{2}^{3}-12R_{3}R_{2}+R_{4}}{\nu^{4}}

Note that these combinations are simpler than those appearing in the first method, and they can be computed by recursion: at each order
n
the higher cumulants can be expressed
as a combination of the
n
-th derivative
R_{n}
and some combination
of the (already computed) lower order cumulants, e.g., one has

\kappa_{3}=-\frac{R_{3}}{\nu^{3}}-\frac{1}{12\nu}+3\nu\kappa_{2}^{2}\quad,\quad\kappa_{4}=-\frac{R_{4}}{\nu^{4}}+\kappa_{2}(1+12\nu\kappa_{3}-16\nu^{2}\kappa_{2}^{3})

(SL.136)

L.4
Calculation of the derivatives
\psi^{(n)}(0)
and results for the cumulants

Now we need to compute the derivatives
\psi^{(n)}(0)
. We will attempt to extend the method used in
(
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
, Section 3.2)

for the stationary KPZ equation. We return to
(
SL.127
) and
define a change of integration variable from
k
to
{\sf z}

where

{\sf z}={\sf z}(k)=\frac{k}{\sqrt{\varrho(1-\varrho)(\varrho+\mathbf{i}k)(1-\varrho-\mathbf{i}k)}}e^{2\nu^{2}(\varrho+\mathbf{i}k)(1-\varrho-\mathbf{i}k)T-\nu X(\varrho+\mathbf{i}k)}

(SL.137)

and rewrite (
SL.127
) as

\begin{split}&v\psi^{\prime\prime}(v)+\psi^{\prime}(v)=\frac{1}{\nu}\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\mathrm{d}{\sf z}}{\pi}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\mathrm{d}{\sf z}}\frac{1}{(\varrho+\mathbf{i}k({\sf z}))(1-\varrho-\mathbf{i}k({\sf z}))}\frac{v}{v^{2}+{\sf z}^{2}}\\
\end{split}

(SL.138)

Then in the limit
v=0
, we find, using
\frac{v}{v^{2}+{\sf z}^{2}}\to\pi\delta({\sf z})

\psi^{\prime}(0)=\frac{1}{\nu}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\mathrm{d}{\sf z}}\frac{1}{(\varrho+\mathbf{i}k({\sf z}))(1-\varrho-\mathbf{i}k({\sf z}))}\right)|_{{\sf z}=0}\\
=\frac{e^{-2\nu^{2}\varrho(1-\varrho)T+\nu\varrho X}}{\nu}

(SL.139)

which agrees with (
SL.129
). Recall that
(
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
, Eq. (65))
for any integer
q\geqslant 1

\partial_{v}^{2q}\frac{v}{v^{2}+{\sf z}^{2}}=(-1)^{q}\partial_{\sf z}^{2q}\frac{v}{v^{2}+{\sf z}^{2}}\quad,\quad\partial_{v}^{2q-1}\frac{v}{v^{2}+{\sf z}^{2}}=(-1)^{q}\partial_{\sf z}^{2q-1}\frac{{\sf z}}{v^{2}+{\sf z}^{2}}

(SL.140)

and we will also use

\displaystyle\partial_{v}^{2q}(v\psi^{\prime\prime}(v)+\psi^{\prime}(v))|_{v=0}=\partial_{v}^{2q}\partial_{v}(v\psi^{\prime}(v))|_{v=0}=(2q+1)\psi^{(2q+1)}(0)

(SL.141)

\displaystyle\partial_{v}^{2q-1}(v\psi^{\prime\prime}(v)+\psi^{\prime}(v))|_{v=0}=\partial_{v}^{2q-1}\partial_{v}(v\psi^{\prime}(v))|_{v=0}=(2q)\psi^{(2q)}(0)

(SL.142)

Odd derivatives

Let us apply
\partial_{v}^{2q}
to (
SL.138
) and set
v\to 0^{+}
. It leads to

\displaystyle(2q+1)\psi^{(2q+1)}(0)=\frac{(-1)^{q}}{\nu}\bigg[\partial_{{\sf z}}^{2q}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\mathrm{d}{\sf z}}\frac{1}{(\varrho+\mathbf{i}k({\sf z}))(1-\varrho-\mathbf{i}k({\sf z}))}\right)\bigg]_{{\sf z}=0}

(SL.143)

where we used (
SL.140
), performed
2q
integrations by part
and sent
v\to 0^{+}
. This leads to the following formula for the odd derivatives as

\psi^{(2q+1)}(0)=\frac{(-1)^{q}}{(2q+1)\nu}\left[\left(\frac{1}{{\sf z}^{\prime}(k)}\partial_{k}\right)^{2q}\left(\frac{1}{{\sf z}^{\prime}(k)}\frac{1}{(\varrho+\mathbf{i}k)(1-\varrho-\mathbf{i}k)}\right)\right]_{k=0}

(SL.144)

where
{\sf z}(k)
is given by (
SL.137
) and
k
is set to zero at the end of the calculation. We see that this is a pretty straightforward
algebraic formula, and that the result will not contain error functions.

Even derivatives

Unfortunately the calculation of the even
derivatives is much more subtle, and more complicated than for
stationary KPZ in
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
. One writes

\begin{split}\psi^{(2q)}(0)=\frac{1}{2q}\partial_{v}^{2q}(v\psi^{\prime}(v))|_{v=0}&=\frac{1}{2q\nu}\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\mathrm{d}{\sf z}}{\pi}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\mathrm{d}{\sf z}}\frac{1}{(\varrho+\mathbf{i}k({\sf z}))(1-\varrho-\mathbf{i}k({\sf z}))}\partial_{v}^{2q-1}\frac{v}{v^{2}+{\sf z}^{2}}\\
&=\frac{(-1)^{q}}{2q\nu}\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\mathrm{d}{\sf z}}{\pi}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\mathrm{d}{\sf z}}\frac{1}{(\varrho+\mathbf{i}k({\sf z}))(1-\varrho-\mathbf{i}k({\sf z}))}\partial_{{\sf z}}^{2q-1}\frac{{\sf z}}{v^{2}+{\sf z}^{2}}\\
&=\frac{(-1)^{q-1}}{2q\nu}\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\mathrm{d}{\sf z}}{\pi}\frac{{\sf z}}{v^{2}+{\sf z}^{2}}\partial_{{\sf z}}^{2q-1}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\mathrm{d}{\sf z}}\frac{1}{(\varrho+\mathbf{i}k({\sf z}))(1-\varrho-\mathbf{i}k({\sf z}))}\right)\\
&\underset{v\to 0}{=}\frac{(-1)^{q-1}}{2q\nu}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\mathrm{d}{\sf z}}{\pi}\frac{1}{{\sf z}}\partial_{{\sf z}}^{2q-1}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\mathrm{d}{\sf z}}\frac{1}{(\varrho+\mathbf{i}k({\sf z}))(1-\varrho-\mathbf{i}k({\sf z}))}\right)\\
&=\frac{(-1)^{q-1}(2q-2)!}{2q\nu}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\mathrm{d}{\sf z}}{\pi}\frac{1}{{\sf z}^{2q-1}}\left[\partial_{\sf z}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\mathrm{d}{\sf z}}\frac{1}{(\varrho+\mathbf{i}k({\sf z}))(1-\varrho-\mathbf{i}k({\sf z}))}\right)\right]|_{\rm reg,{\sf z}^{2q-1}}\end{split}

(SL.145)

where we used the identity in
(
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
, Eqs. (67-68))
.
Here we define

g(y)|_{\mathrm{reg},y^{m}}=g(y)-\sum_{p=0}^{m-1}\frac{g^{(p)}(0)}{p!}y^{p}

(SL.146)

so that the regularized function starts at order
y^{m}
or higher.
Integrating by part once more we find

\psi^{(2q)}(0)=\frac{(-1)^{q-1}(2q-1)!}{2q\nu}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\pi}\left(\frac{1}{{\sf z}^{\prime}(k)(\varrho+\mathbf{i}k)(1-\varrho-\mathbf{i}k)}\right)|_{\rm reg,k^{2q}}\frac{{\sf z}^{\prime}(k)}{{\sf z}(k)^{2q}}

(SL.147)

where we have used that if
{\sf z}(k)\sim k
at small
k
then

A({\sf z})_{\rm reg,{\sf z}^{n}}=A({\sf z}(k))_{\rm reg,k^{n}}
. We now compute explicitly some of these derivatives and
obtain the associated cumulants.

Calculation for
q=1
: second and third cumulant
\kappa_{2}
,
\kappa_{3}

For
q=1
one needs to compute the integral

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}k\left(\frac{1}{{\sf z}^{\prime}(k)(\varrho+\mathbf{i}k)(1-\varrho-\mathbf{i}k)}\right)|_{\rm reg,k^{2}}\frac{{\sf z}^{\prime}(k)}{{\sf z}(k)^{2}}

(SL.148)

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}k\bigg(\frac{(\varrho(1-\varrho))}{k^{2}}e^{-4\nu^{2}(\varrho+\mathbf{i}k)(1-\varrho-\mathbf{i}k)T+2\nu X(\varrho+\mathbf{i}k)}+e^{-2\nu^{2}\varrho(1-\varrho)T+\nu X\varrho}\partial_{k}\frac{1}{{\sf z}(k)}\bigg)

(SL.149)

One can check that the counterterm (second term) cancels the
1/k^{2}
divergence at
k=0

but for generic values of the parameters the principal part remains necessary to
regularize the
\mathcal{O}(1/k)
term. We have checked numerically that this integral
gives the same result as the fourth line in (
SL.145
).
Note that for
T=0
the integrand oscillates and does not decay to zero at infinity because of
the last term, hence
T>0
is needed for convergence. An important property is that
we can
check that this integral obeys the heat equation

(\partial_{T}-(\partial_{X}^{2}-2\nu\partial_{X}))\psi^{\prime\prime}(0)=0
,
corresponding to formally setting
n=1
in (
SJ.67
).

The analytical calculation of the above integral for
T>0
is not easy. So we will resort to a
trick by evaluating it at
T=0^{+}
and then propagating it using the heat equation.
We first note that the first term can be integrated at
T=0
so that the total integral
can be written as
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}k\partial_{k}f(k)
with

f(k)=f_{1}(k)+f_{2}(k)\quad,\quad f_{1}(k)=\frac{(\varrho-1)\varrho e^{2\nu X(\varrho+\mathbf{i}k)}}{k}-2\mathbf{i}\nu(\varrho-1)\varrho Xe^{2\nu\varrho X}\text{Ei}(2\mathbf{i}kX\nu)\quad,\quad f_{2}(k)=e^{\nu X\varrho}\frac{1}{{\sf z}(k)}

(SL.150)

Plotting
f(k)
we see that it does not diverge at
k=0

but that its imaginary part has a
\log
divergence
and its real part has a jump at
k=0
. Hence we must evaluate the principal part with care and the total
integral is thus

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}k\partial_{k}f(k)=f(+\infty)-f(0^{+})+f(0^{-})-f(-\infty)

(SL.151)

The contribution at infinity can be evaluated from the first term only

f(+\infty)-f(-\infty)=f_{1}(+\infty)-f_{1}(-\infty)=-4\pi\nu(1-\varrho)\varrho e^{2\nu\varrho X}|X|\quad\text{with}\;T=0^{+}

(SL.152)

using

\lim_{k\to\pm\infty}\mathrm{Ei}(\mathbf{i}Xk)=\mathbf{i}\pi\,\mathrm{sign}(kX)\quad,\quad(\lim_{k\to+\infty}-\lim_{k\to-\infty})\text{Ei}(\mathbf{i}Xk)=2\mathbf{i}\pi\mathrm{sign}(X)

(SL.153)

Indeed we assume
T=0^{+}
, i.e., a slightly positive
T
,
so that the second term does not contribute (since it contains a
e^{-k^{2}T}
factor).
Next we obtain the jump at
k=0

f(0^{+})-f(0^{-})=-2\pi\nu(1-\varrho)\varrho e^{2\nu\varrho X}|X|

(SL.154)

which we note is
1/2
of the jump at infinity.
To obtain the jump of
f
we cannot simply set
f=f_{1}
because it diverges at
k=0
, but
we can add to it the divergent part of
f_{2}
, i.e., consider the jump of

f_{1}(k)+\varrho(1-\varrho)e^{2\nu\varrho X}/k
. We have further checked (
SL.154
)
numerically. We also find that the log divergence of the imaginary part cancels out.
In total we thus find

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}k\partial_{k}f(k)\underset{T=0^{+}}{=}-2\pi\nu(1-\varrho)\varrho e^{2\nu\varrho X}|X|

(SL.155)

\displaystyle\psi^{(2)}(0)\underset{T=0}{=}\frac{1}{2\pi\nu}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}k\partial_{k}f(k)|_{T=0^{+}}=-(1-\varrho)\varrho e^{2\nu\varrho X}|X|

(SL.156)

One finally obtains (by propagating from
T=0
using the heat kernel
G_{n}(X,T)=\sqrt{\frac{n}{4\pi T}}e^{-n\frac{(X-2\nu T)^{2}}{4T}}

associated to (
SJ.67
), setting
n=1
, as we do in (
SJ.71
))

\psi^{(2)}(0)=(\varrho-1)\varrho e^{-\frac{(X-2\nu T)^{2}}{4T}}\left(e^{\frac{(2\nu(2\varrho-1)T+X)^{2}}{4T}}(2\nu(2\varrho-1)T+X)\text{Erf}\left(\frac{2\nu(2\varrho-1)T+X}{2\sqrt{T}}\right)+\frac{2\sqrt{T}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\right)

Using
\psi^{\prime}(0)=e^{-2\nu^{2}\varrho(1-\varrho)T+\nu\varrho X}/\nu
from
(
SL.139
) and
\kappa_{2}=-\frac{r_{2}}{\nu^{2}}=-\psi^{\prime\prime}(0)/(\nu^{2}\psi^{\prime}(0)^{2})

from (
SL.135
) one obtains exactly the result
SK.104

for the second cumulant
\kappa_{2}
derived by the first method. We
recall that it can be written in a compact form for any
X,T
, as
given in the main text

\kappa_{2}=\kappa_{2}(X,T)=\sqrt{T}\sigma(\varrho){\cal G}(y),\quad y=-\frac{\nu(2\varrho-1)T+X/2}{\sqrt{T}}

(SL.157)

with
\sigma(\varrho)=2\varrho(1-\varrho)
and
{\cal G}(y)=y{\rm Erf}(y)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-y^{2}}

is an even function. The nice feature of the present calculation is that once the even cumulant
is known the next odd one is immediate to compute. Using the formula (
SL.144
)
for
\psi^{2q+1}(0)
for
q=1
we easily obtain the third cumulant in a compact form
for arbitrary
X,T

\kappa_{3}=\kappa_{3}(X,T)=\frac{\nu T}{4}\sigma(\varrho)^{2}\sigma^{\prime\prime}(\varrho)(1+3(y^{2}-{\cal G}(y)^{2}))+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{T}y\sigma(\varrho)\sigma^{\prime}(\varrho)

(SL.158)

and one can check that for
X=0
and
T=1
it is equivalent to the formula
(
SK.110
). In the limit
\nu\to 0
it gives (setting
T=1
)

\kappa_{3}\underset{\nu\to 0}{=}\kappa_{3}(X)=\varrho(1-\varrho)(2\varrho-1)X

(SL.159)

which, one can check, agrees with the result for the SSEP extracted from
(
SQ.354
) and the first line of
(
SQ.376
) (in agreement with
(
Mallick et al. 2024
, Eq. (6.35))
).

Calculation for
q=2
: fourth and fifth cumulant
\kappa_{4}
,
\kappa_{5}

For
q=2
, from (
SL.147
) we need to compute

\displaystyle\psi^{(4)}(0)=-\frac{3!}{4\nu}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\pi}\bigg(\frac{1}{(\varrho+\mathbf{i}k)(1-\varrho-\mathbf{i}k){\sf z}(k)^{4}}-A_{0}\frac{{\sf z}^{\prime}(k)}{{\sf z}(k)^{4}}-A_{1}\frac{{\sf z}^{\prime}(k)}{{\sf z}(k)^{3}}-A_{2}\frac{{\sf z}^{\prime}(k)}{{\sf z}(k)^{2}}\bigg)

(SL.160)

\displaystyle=-\frac{3!}{4\nu\pi}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{d}k}\partial_{k}f(k)

(SL.161)

where

\frac{1}{{\sf z}^{\prime}(k)(\varrho+\mathbf{i}k)(1-\varrho-\mathbf{i}k)}=A_{0}+A_{1}{\sf z}(k)+A_{2}{\sf z}(k)^{2}+o(k^{2})

(SL.162)

One finds at
T=0

A_{0}=e^{\nu\varrho X}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ A_{1}=-2\mathbf{i}\nu(\varrho-1)\varrho Xe^{2\nu\varrho X}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ A_{2}=-\frac{1}{8}e^{3\nu\varrho X}\left(36\nu^{2}(\varrho-1)^{2}\varrho^{2}X^{2}+12\nu\left(2\varrho^{2}-3\varrho+1\right)\varrho X-1\right)

(SL.163)

One can also obtain these coefficients at
T>0
(not shown).
One can check using Mathematica that the terms
1/k^{4},1/k^{3},1/k^{2}
near
k=0
do cancel in the
integrand at
T=0
and also at
T>0
, so that the integral is well defined.

One can rewrite the first term in the integrand in (
SL.160
) as

\frac{1}{(\varrho+\mathbf{i}k)(1-\varrho-\mathbf{i}k){\sf z}(k)^{4}}=\frac{1}{k^{4}}\Delta_{4}(k)\quad,\quad\Delta_{4}(k)=(\varrho-1)^{2}\varrho^{2}(\varrho+\mathbf{i}k)(1-\varrho-\mathbf{i}k)e^{4\nu X(\varrho+\mathbf{i}k)}

(SL.164)

At
T=0
this term can be integrated, and the primitive
f_{1}(k)
has the same structure as for
q=1
,
with one
{\rm Ei}
function. It is then easy to extract the jump at infinity.
One finds that it can be written as

\displaystyle f_{1}(+\infty)-f_{1}(-\infty)=\frac{\mathbf{i}\pi}{3}{\rm sgn}(X)\,\left[\partial_{k}^{3}\Delta_{4}(k)\right]_{k=0}

(SL.165)

which we double checked with Mathematica.
Returning to
q=1
we see that in that case
f_{1}(+\infty)-f_{1}(-\infty)=2\mathbf{i}\pi{\rm sgn}(X)\,\partial_{k}|_{k=0}\Delta_{2}(k)
.
Hence we conjecture that for general
q

\displaystyle f_{1}(+\infty)-f_{1}(-\infty)=\frac{2\mathbf{i}\pi}{(2q-1)!}{\rm sgn}(X)\,\left[\partial_{k}^{2q-1}\Delta_{2q}(k)\right]_{k=0}

(SL.166)

The primitive of the second term is

\displaystyle f_{2}(k)=\frac{1}{3}A_{0}\frac{1}{{\sf z}(k)^{3}}+\frac{1}{2}A^{\prime}_{1}\frac{1}{{\sf z}(k)^{2}}+A^{\prime}_{2}\frac{1}{{\sf z}(k)}

(SL.167)

By adding its singular part at
k=0
to
f_{1}(k)
we can compute with Mathematica
the jump of
f(k)
at
k=0
and, remarkably, we find

f(0^{+})-f(0^{-})=\frac{1}{2}(f_{1}(+\infty)-f_{1}(-\infty))

(SL.168)

i.e., there is the same factor of
1/2
as noted for
q=1
. Hence we find

\displaystyle\psi^{(4)}(0)

\displaystyle\underset{T=0}{=}

\displaystyle-\frac{3!}{4\nu\pi}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{d}k}\partial_{k}f(k)=-\frac{3!}{8\nu\pi}(f_{1}(+\infty)-f_{1}(-\infty))

(SL.169)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathbf{i}}{4\nu}{\rm sgn}(X)\,\left[\partial_{k}^{3}\Delta_{4}(k)\right]_{k=0}

(SL.170)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2(\varrho-1)^{2}\varrho^{2}|X|e^{4\nu\varrho X}(2\nu X(6\varrho+4\nu(\varrho-1)\varrho X-3)+3)

(SL.171)

To obtain
\psi^{(4)}(0)
for general
T
we simply propagate its value at
T=0
as we did in (
SJ.71
) using
the heat kernel
G_{n}(X,T)=\sqrt{\frac{n}{4\pi T}}e^{-n\frac{(X-2\nu T)^{2}}{4T}}

setting
n=2
. From (
SL.136
) we then obtain the fourth cumulant for arbitrary
X,T
in the form

\begin{split}\kappa_{4}=&\kappa_{2}(1+12\nu\kappa_{3}-16\nu^{2}\kappa_{2}^{3})+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{T}\sigma(\varrho)^{2}\bigg(\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}e^{-2y^{2}}\left(8\nu^{2}T\left(2y^{2}+1\right)\sigma(\varrho)+12\nu(2\varrho-1)\sqrt{T}y-3\right)\\
&-2\text{Erf}\left(\sqrt{2}y\right)\left(-4\nu^{2}Ty\left(4y^{2}+3\right)\sigma(\varrho)-3\nu(2\varrho-1)\sqrt{T}\left(4y^{2}+1\right)+3y\right)\bigg)\end{split}

(SL.172)

where
y=-\frac{\nu(2\varrho-1)T+X/2}{\sqrt{T}}
and
\kappa_{2}
,
\kappa_{3}
,
\sigma
and
{\cal G}

have been given above. In the limit
\nu\to 0
it becomes

\kappa_{4}\underset{\nu=0}{=}2\varrho(1-\varrho)\left(3\sqrt{2}(\varrho-1)\varrho\,{\cal G}\left(\sqrt{2}y\right)+{\cal G}(y)\right)\quad,\quad y=-\frac{X}{2}

(SL.173)

which, one can check, agrees with the result for the SSEP extracted from
(
SQ.354
) and the first line of
(
SQ.376
) (in agreement with
(
Mallick et al. 2024
, Eq. (6.35))
).

A formula for the general stationary cumulant
\kappa_{n}

Conjecturing the formula for the even derivatives of
\psi(v)
leads to
a formula for the general cumulant for
\varrho_{1}=\varrho_{2}
. From
the conjecture (
SL.166
) and assuming
(
SL.168
) holds for any
q
,
we obtain

\displaystyle\psi^{(2q)}(0)\underset{T=0}{=}\frac{\mathbf{i}(-1)^{q-1}(\varrho(1-\varrho))^{q}}{2q\nu}\,\left[\partial_{k}^{2q-1}((\varrho+\mathbf{i}k)(1-\varrho-\mathbf{i}k))^{q-1}e^{2q\nu X(\varrho+\mathbf{i}k)}{\rm sgn}(X)\right]_{k=0}

(SL.174)

We can commute the evolution by the heat kernel and the derivatives so we obtain, setting
p=\mathbf{i}k

\psi^{(2q)}(0)=-\frac{(\varrho(1-\varrho))^{q}}{2q\nu}\left[\partial_{p}^{2q-1}\left(((\varrho+p)(1-\varrho-p))^{q-1}e^{-4q\nu\sqrt{T}(y(\varrho+p)+\nu\sqrt{T}(\varrho^{2}-p^{2}))}{\rm Erf}(\sqrt{q}(2p\nu\sqrt{T}-y))\right)\right]_{p=0}

(SL.175)

with
y=-\frac{\nu(2\varrho-1)T+X/2}{\sqrt{T}}
.

The odd derivatives are given by (
SL.144
), which setting
p=\mathbf{i}k
can be rewritten
as

\displaystyle\psi^{(2q+1)}(0)=\frac{1}{(2q+1)\nu}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\hat{\sf z}^{\prime}(p)}\partial_{p}\right)^{2q}\left(\frac{1}{\hat{\sf z}^{\prime}(p)}\frac{1}{(\varrho+p)(1-\varrho-p)}\right)\right]_{p=0}

(SL.176)

\displaystyle\hat{\sf z}(p)=\frac{pe^{2\nu\sqrt{T}(\varrho+p)\left(\nu\sqrt{T}(\varrho-p)+y\right)}}{\sqrt{\varrho(\varrho-1)(\varrho+p)(\varrho+p-1)}}

(SL.177)

An equivalent way to compute it is to expand
\hat{\sf z}
in series of
p

and inverse the series to get
p
in series of
\hat{\sf z}
, and compute

\displaystyle\psi^{(2q+1)}(0)=\frac{1}{(2q+1)\nu}\left[\left(\frac{d}{d\hat{\sf z}}\right)^{2q}\left(\frac{dp}{d\hat{\sf z}}\frac{1}{(\varrho+p(\hat{{\sf z}})(1-\varrho-p(\hat{{\sf z}}))}\right)\right]_{\hat{\sf z}=0}

(SL.178)

These formula allow to obtain the higher cumulants using
(
SL.134
) and (
SL.135
). We have obtained
for instance the fifth and sixth cumulants, which we give below
in some special case. We have checked that they reduce for
\nu=0

to their SSEP limit which we also computed from
(
SQ.354
) and the first line of
(
SQ.376
) (in agreement with
(
Mallick et al. 2024
, Eq. (6.35))
).

Special case.

In the special case
\varrho=1/2
,
X=0
,
T=1
, which implies
y=0
, we find for the even and odd derivatives
simplify a little as

\displaystyle\psi^{(2q)}(0)=-\frac{1}{2^{2q+1}q\nu}e^{-\nu^{2}q}\left[\partial_{p}^{2q-1}\left((\frac{1}{4}-p^{2})^{q-1}e^{4\nu^{2}p^{2}q}{\rm Erf}(2\nu p\sqrt{q})\right)\right]_{p=0}

(SL.179)

\displaystyle\psi^{(2q+1)}(0)=\frac{1}{(2q+1)\nu}\left[\left(\frac{d}{d\hat{\sf z}}\right)^{2q}\left(\frac{dp}{d\hat{\sf z}}\frac{1}{\frac{1}{4}-p^{2}}\right)\right]_{\hat{\sf z}=0}\quad,\quad\hat{\sf z}=\frac{2pe^{2\nu^{2}(\frac{1}{4}-p^{2})}}{\sqrt{(\frac{1}{4}-p^{2})}}

(SL.180)

As a result all the cumulants are polynomials in
\nu
. We give here the
explicit forms

\displaystyle\kappa_{5}=\frac{5\left(21+2\left(4\sqrt{2}-9\right)\pi\right)\nu^{3}+2\pi\left(15-15\sqrt{2}+2\pi\right)\nu}{8\pi^{2}}

(SL.181)

\displaystyle\kappa_{6}=\frac{1}{8\pi^{5/2}}\bigg(\left(4-15\sqrt{2}+10\sqrt{3}\right)\pi^{2}+5\pi\left(56-63\sqrt{2}+\left(6+13\sqrt{2}-8\sqrt{3}\right)\pi\right)\nu^{2}

(SL.182)

\displaystyle+\left(756+420\left(\sqrt{2}-2\right)\pi+\left(45-60\sqrt{2}+24\sqrt{3}\right)\pi^{2}\right)\nu^{4}\bigg)

(SL.183)

KPZ limit.

Consider now the further limit to stationary KPZ where
in addition we take
\nu\to+\infty
. From Eqs. (
SP.323
), (
SP.325
) we expect that in that limit

\psi(v)\to\frac{1}{\nu^{2}}\psi_{\rm KPZ}(\tilde{v})\quad,\quad v=\frac{2\tilde{v}}{\nu}e^{\nu^{2}/2}

(SL.184)

hence we expect that in that limit

\psi^{(2q)}(0)\underset{\nu\to\infty}{\simeq}\frac{1}{\nu^{2}}\left(\frac{\nu}{2}e^{-\nu^{2}/2}\right)^{2q}\times\psi_{\rm KPZ}^{(2q)}(0)\quad,\quad\psi_{\rm KPZ}^{(2q)}(0)=\frac{2(-1)^{q}q^{q-\frac{3}{2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{3}{2}-q\right)\Gamma(2q-1)}{\pi}

(SL.185)

where the values of
\psi_{\rm KPZ}^{(2q)}(0)
were obtained in
(
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
, Eq. (69))
.
To check that our result agrees with that limit, we rescale
p=\tilde{p}/(2\nu)

in (
SL.179
) and, taking
\nu
large, we replace
1/4-p^{2}\to 1/4
to leading order.
We use that
e^{a^{2}}{\rm Erf}(a)=\sum_{q^{\prime}\geqslant 1}\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+q^{\prime})}a^{2q^{\prime}-1}

and the identity
\frac{(2q-1)!}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+q)}=(-1)^{q-1}\frac{2}{\pi}\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-q)\Gamma(2q-1)
, and we find that
(
SL.185
) holds as required.

For the odd derivatives, one defines
p=\tilde{p}/\nu
in (
SL.179
) so that
in the large
\nu
limit one has
\hat{\sf z}=\tilde{z}/\nu
with
\tilde{z}=4\tilde{p}e^{-2\tilde{p}^{2}}
and one finds

\displaystyle\psi^{(2q+1)}(0)

\displaystyle\underset{\nu\to\infty}{\simeq}4\frac{\nu^{2q}e^{-(2q+1)\nu^{2}/2}}{(2q+1)\nu}\left[\left(\frac{d}{d\tilde{z}}\right)^{2q+1}\tilde{p}\right]_{\tilde{z}=0}

(SL.186)

The relation between
\tilde{z}
and
\tilde{p}
can be explicitly inverted as
\tilde{p}^{2}=-\frac{1}{4}W(-\tilde{z}^{2}/4)
, where
W
represents the main branch of the Lambert function. Using the following series
\sqrt{W(y^{2})/y^{2}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(n+1/2)^{n-1}}{n!}(-y^{2})^{n}
,
we obtain
\tilde{p}=\frac{1}{8}\sum_{q=0}^{\infty}\frac{(q+1/2)^{n-1}}{4^{q}q!}z^{2q+1}
.
This leads to

\psi^{(2q+1)}(0)\underset{\nu\to\infty}{\simeq}\frac{1}{\nu^{2}}\left(\frac{\nu}{2}e^{-\nu^{2}/2}\right)^{2q+1}\times\psi_{\rm KPZ}^{(2q+1)}(0)\quad,\quad\psi_{\rm KPZ}^{(2q+1)}(0)=\frac{(2q)!}{q!}(q+\frac{1}{2})^{q-1}

(SL.187)

where the values of
\psi_{\rm KPZ}^{(2q)}(0)
were obtained in
(
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
, Eq. (66))
.
The agreement with the stationary KPZ limit provides a test on our formula for general
q
.

Remark L.1
.

The above formula for the cumulants are displayed (in a slightly simpler form) in terms of the
R_{n}

in Appendix
B
. Note that performing the limit
\nu\to 0

to the SSEP directly on these formula is delicate due to cancellations
of various orders between the
R_{n}
when computing the cumulants.
The SSEP limit is studied in more details in Section
Q
.

Appendix M
Cumulants of the position of a tracer

If we denote
M_{\sf t}
the position of the tracer in ASEP, i.e., a tagged particle of the ASEP, its position in the continuum model is
Y_{t}=\varepsilon M_{{\sf t}=t/\varepsilon^{2}}

where
1/\varepsilon=\sqrt{{\sf T}/T}
. Consider now the position of a tracer
Y_{t}
starting at the initial position
Y_{0}=0
. It is defined by the conservation of particle number to the right of the tracer

\int_{Y_{t}}^{\infty}dx\varrho(x,t)=\int_{Y_{0}}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}x\varrho(x,0)\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad h(Y_{t},t)=0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad z(Y_{t},t)=1\quad\text{for}\,\,Y_{0}=0

(SM.188)

so that one has, as in Ref.
Imamura et al. 2017
, the equality in probability

\mathbb{P}(Y_{t}<x)=\mathbb{P}(h(x,t)>0)

(SM.189)

(with
N
there being
h
here). Note that if
Y_{0}\neq 0
the above condition becomes
h(Y_{t},t)=h(Y_{0},0)

and
z(Y_{t},t)=e^{2\nu h(Y_{0},0)}
(we recall that
h(0,0)=0
).

We want to compute the CGF and the PDF of the tracer’s position
Y_{T}
at time
T
, which take the large deviation forms

\langle e^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\lambda Y_{T}}\rangle\sim e^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}C(\lambda)}\quad,\quad P(Y_{T}=X)\sim e^{-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\chi(X)}

(SM.190)

where the rate functions are related by
C(\lambda)=\max_{X\in\mathbb{R}}[\lambda X-\chi(X)]
.
In most formula below the dependence in
T
is implicit.
Once
C(\lambda)

is known, its Taylor expansion coefficients
c_{n}=c_{n}(T)
give the cumulants of the tracer position

C(\lambda)=\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{1}{n!}c_{n}(T)\lambda^{n}\quad,\quad\langle Y_{T}^{n}\rangle^{c}\simeq\varepsilon^{n-1}c_{n}(T)\quad,\quad\langle M_{\sf T}^{n}\rangle^{c}\simeq c_{n}(T=1)\sqrt{{\sf T}}

(SM.191)

We can now use our main result for the CGF of
h(X,T)=-J(X,T)
for
arbitrary value of
X
.
Let us now make the
X
dependence explicit
and denote
\phi(p)=\phi(p;X)
and
\Phi(J)=\Phi(J;X)
.
Now, since
J=-h
, we have

P(Y_{T}=X)=P(h(X,T)=0)\sim e^{-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Phi(0;X)}

(SM.192)

which implies
\chi(X)=\Phi(0;X)
. From Legendre inversion of (
SH.55
) one obtains

\Phi(J;X)=\max_{P}[PJ-\phi(P;X)]

(SM.193)

The value of
J
which realizes the maximum is thus such that

P=\partial_{J}\Phi(J;X)\quad,\quad\partial_{P}\phi(P;X)=J

(SM.194)

where the derivatives act on the first argument. The constraint that we are following the tracer can be implemented
by setting
J=0
in the above equations. This gives a relation
between
p
,
u
and
X
which we denote
p^{*}=p^{*}(X)
,
P^{*}=P^{*}(X)
and
u^{*}=u^{*}(X)
.
One then obtains the rate function of the PDF of the tracer as

\displaystyle\chi(X)=\Phi(0;X)=-\phi(P^{*};X)

(SM.195)

\displaystyle\partial_{P}\phi(P;X)|_{P=P*}=0

(SM.196)

where the last equation determines

P^{*}=P^{*}(X)
if
\phi
and
\Phi
are known.
We then obtain the CGF for the tracer’s position from

\displaystyle C^{\prime}(\lambda)=X

(SM.197)

\displaystyle\lambda=\chi^{\prime}(X)=-\partial_{X}\phi(P;X)|_{P=P^{*}(X)}

(SM.198)

where we have used the condition (
SM.196
) to replace
d/dX
by
\partial_{X}
.

M.1
First method using the CGF and cumulants of the current

In summary, once the CGF and PDF rate functions of the current,
\phi
and
\Phi
, are known, i.e., once the cumulants
\kappa_{n}=\kappa_{n}(X)

computed in a previous section are known, we can obtain the cumulants of the tracer
c_{n}
by eliminating

P^{*}
and
X
in the system of
three
equations

\displaystyle\partial_{P}\phi(P;X)|_{P=P*}=\kappa_{1}(X)+\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{1}{n!}\kappa_{n+1}(X)(P^{*})^{n}=0

(SM.199)

\displaystyle-\partial_{X}\phi(P;X)|_{P=P^{*}}=-\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{1}{n!}(\partial_{X}\kappa_{n}(X))(P^{*})^{n}=\lambda

(SM.200)

\displaystyle C^{\prime}(\lambda)=c_{1}+\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{1}{n!}c_{n+1}\lambda^{n}=X

(SM.201)

To understand the structure let us write the lowest order terms. One has

\displaystyle X=c_{1}+c_{2}\lambda+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})

(SM.202)

\displaystyle\lambda=-\kappa_{1}^{\prime}(X)P^{*}+\mathcal{O}((P^{*})^{2})

(SM.203)

\displaystyle 0=\kappa_{1}(X)+\kappa_{2}(X)P^{*}+\mathcal{O}((P^{*})^{2})

(SM.204)

The elimination problem can be solved in a systematic expansion in
\lambda
,
P^{*}=\mathcal{O}(\lambda)
and
X-c_{1}=\mathcal{O}(\lambda)
.
One first obtains that the first moment
c_{1}
is the root of the equation

\kappa_{1}(c_{1})=0

(SM.205)

Next one obtains for the second cumulant

c_{2}=\frac{\kappa_{2}}{\left(\kappa_{1}^{\prime}\right){}^{2}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{\kappa_{2}\left(c_{1}\right)}{\kappa_{1}^{\prime}\left(c_{1}\right){}^{2}}

(SM.206)

To pursue further, one way is to invert the series (
SM.200
) to obtain
P^{*}
as a function of
\lambda
, then insert in (
SM.199
)
and replace
X
by its series (
SM.201
) and expand all in
\lambda
to obtain recursive equations
for the
c_{n}
. The higher cumulants become quickly quite complicated. They can be written in a more economical form by introducing

\tilde{\kappa}_{n}(X)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{\kappa_{n}(X)}{\kappa^{\prime}_{1}(c_{1})^{n}}

(SM.207)

In these notations they read

\displaystyle c_{2}=\tilde{\kappa}_{2}\quad,\quad c_{3}=3\tilde{\kappa}_{2}\left(\tilde{\kappa}_{2}^{\prime}-\tilde{\kappa}_{2}\tilde{\kappa}_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right)-\tilde{\kappa}_{3}

(SM.208)

\displaystyle c_{4}=6\tilde{\kappa}_{2}\left(2\tilde{\kappa}_{3}-7\tilde{\kappa}_{2}\tilde{\kappa}_{2}^{\prime}\right)\tilde{\kappa}_{1}^{\prime\prime}+15\tilde{\kappa}_{2}\left(\tilde{\kappa}_{2}^{\prime}\right){}^{2}-4\tilde{\kappa}_{2}\tilde{\kappa}_{3}^{\prime}-6\tilde{\kappa}_{3}\tilde{\kappa}_{2}^{\prime}+24\tilde{\kappa}_{2}^{3}\left(\tilde{\kappa}_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right){}^{2}+6\tilde{\kappa}_{2}^{2}\tilde{\kappa}_{2}^{\prime\prime}-4\tilde{\kappa}_{1}{}^{(3)}\tilde{\kappa}_{2}^{3}+\tilde{\kappa}_{4}

where the derivatives are taken at
X=c_{1}
, i.e., as in (
SM.206
).

The cumulants of the tracer can thus be obtained from the cumulants of the current at
X,T

although the combinatorics is not simple. This combinatorics is general and valid for any model,
since it is only a consequence of the definitions of the rate functions.

Let us apply it in some simple cases. For the first moment the equation

\kappa_{1}(c_{1})=0
reads, more explicitly

e^{2\nu\varrho_{1}(2\nu(\varrho_{1}-1)T+c_{1})}\text{Erfc}(\frac{\nu(2\varrho_{1}-1)+\frac{c_{1}}{2}}{\sqrt{T}})+e^{2\nu\varrho_{2}(2\nu(\varrho_{2}-1)T+c_{1})}\text{Erfc}(-\frac{\nu(2\varrho_{1}-1)+\frac{c_{1}}{2}}{\sqrt{T}})=2

(SM.209)

In the SSEP limit
\nu\to 0
,
one must cancel the term
\mathcal{O}(\nu)
and
one obtains the equation

2\varrho_{2}\xi=(\varrho_{1}-\varrho_{2})f(\xi)\quad,\quad f(\xi)=\int_{\xi}^{+\infty}du{\rm Erfc}(u)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-\xi^{2}}-\xi{\rm Erfc}(\xi)\quad,\quad\xi=\frac{X}{\sqrt{4T}}

(SM.210)

It agrees with Ref.
(
Imamura et al. 2017
, Eq. (13))
(with the matching
\varrho_{-}=\varrho_{2}

and
\varrho_{+}=\varrho_{1}
).

Let us consider now the higher cumulants and focus on the stationary case
\varrho_{1}=\varrho_{2}
. Then one has from (
SK.89
),
i.e.,
\kappa_{1}(X,T)=2\nu\varrho(1-\varrho)T-\varrho X
, and we obtain the mean position of the tracer as

\langle Y_{T}\rangle=c_{1}=2\nu(1-\varrho)T

(SM.211)

In that case one has
\kappa^{\prime}_{1}(c_{1})=-\varrho
and from the explicit expression
(
SK.104
) for
\tilde{\kappa}_{2}(X,T)

we obtain

c_{2}=\frac{1}{\varrho^{2}}\kappa_{2}(c_{1})=\frac{2(1-\varrho)}{\varrho}\sqrt{T}\left(\nu\varrho\sqrt{T}\text{Erf}\left(\nu\varrho\sqrt{T}\right)+\frac{e^{-(\nu\varrho\sqrt{T})^{2}}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\right)

(SM.212)

which setting our
T=1
recovers
(
Berlioz et al. 2024
, Eq. (S135))
(see also
De Masi and Ferrari 1985
).
It is an even function of
\nu
, which behaves as
c_{2}\simeq 2(1-\varrho)\nu+O(e^{-\varrho^{2}\nu^{2}})
at large
|\nu|
.
For the third cumulant we find (for
T=1
)

\displaystyle c_{3}=\frac{1-\varrho}{\varrho}\bigg(-6\pi\nu(\varrho-1)(2\nu^{2}\varrho^{2}+1)\text{Erf}(\nu\varrho)^{2}-\frac{6\sqrt{\pi}(\varrho-1)e^{-\nu^{2}\varrho^{2}}\left(4\nu^{2}\varrho^{2}+1\right)\text{Erf}(\nu\varrho)}{\varrho}

(SM.213)

\displaystyle+2\nu(\pi(2\varrho(3\nu^{2}(\varrho-1)\varrho+2)-3)-6(\varrho-1)e^{-2\nu^{2}\varrho^{2}})\bigg)

(SM.214)

which can be checked, agrees with
(
Berlioz et al. 2024
, Eq. (S136))
.
It is an odd function of
\nu
, which behaves as
c_{3}\simeq 2(1-\varrho)\nu+O(e^{-\varrho^{2}\nu^{2}})
at large
|\nu|
,
and can be non monotonic as a function of
\nu
in some range of
\varrho
. For the fourth
cumulant we find (setting
T=1
)

\displaystyle c_{4}=\nu(\varrho-1)^{2}\varrho\left(80\nu^{4}\varrho^{4}+84\nu^{2}\varrho^{2}+15\right)\text{Erf}(\nu\varrho)^{3}+\frac{15(\varrho-1)^{2}e^{-\nu^{2}\varrho^{2}}\left(16\nu^{4}\varrho^{4}+12\nu^{2}\varrho^{2}+1\right)\text{Erf}(\nu\varrho)^{2}}{\sqrt{\pi}}

(SM.215)

\displaystyle+\nu\varrho\left(\frac{12(\varrho-1)^{2}e^{-2\nu^{2}\varrho^{2}}\left(20\nu^{2}\varrho^{2}+9\right)}{\pi}+2\varrho\left(27-2\varrho\left(3\nu^{2}(\varrho-1)\left(3\varrho\left(4\nu^{2}(\varrho-1)\varrho+5\right)-13\right)+8\right)\right)-21\right)\text{Erf}(\nu\varrho)

\displaystyle+2\nu(\varrho-1)\varrho\left(\varrho\left(4\nu^{2}\varrho\left(4\varrho\left(2\nu^{2}(\varrho-1)\varrho+3\right)-9\right)+9\right)-3\right)\text{Erf}\left(\sqrt{2}\nu\varrho\right)

(SM.216)

\displaystyle+\frac{e^{-3\nu^{2}\varrho^{2}}}{\pi^{3/2}}\bigg(4(\varrho-1)^{2}\left(20\nu^{2}\varrho^{2}+3\right)+\sqrt{2}\pi\varrho(\varrho-1)e^{\nu^{2}\varrho^{2}}\left(4\nu^{2}\varrho\left(2\varrho\left(4\nu^{2}(\varrho-1)\varrho+5\right)-7\right)+3\right)

(SM.217)

\displaystyle-\pi e^{2\nu^{2}\varrho^{2}}\left(4\varrho\left(6\nu^{2}(\varrho-1)\varrho\left(6\varrho\left(\nu^{2}(\varrho-1)\varrho+1\right)-5\right)+2\varrho-3\right)+3\right)\bigg)

(SM.218)

This bulky expression simplifies in the limit
\nu=0
and reproduces the known result for
the SSEP
Krapivsky et al. 2014
(see also (9) and Section III.A.2 in

Grabsch and Bénichou 2024
). Remarkably, for large
\nu
it again
simplifies to
c_{4}\simeq 2(1-\varrho)\nu T
.

Remark M.1
.

It is reasonable to expect that for the WASEP (see the discussion in
Berlioz et al. 2024
)
in the so-called ballistic regime
\nu\gg 1
with
\varrho
fixed, all cumulants
c_{n}\simeq 2(1-\varrho)\nu T
, i.e.,
the tracer position becomes a Poisson random variable. Indeed, this was
shown for the ASEP in
Ferrari and Fontes 1996
.
Our results for the first four
cumulants are
compatible this result. In terms of the rate functions defined above it would mean that
in the limit
c_{1}=2(1-\varrho)\nu T\gg 1

\displaystyle\chi(Y)\simeq c_{1}\,\chi_{\rm Poisson}(Y/c_{1})\quad,\quad C(\lambda)\simeq c_{1}(e^{\lambda}-1)

(SM.219)

\displaystyle\chi_{\rm Poisson}(y)=y\log y-y+1

(SM.220)

where
\chi_{\rm Poisson}
is the large deviation rate function
of a Poisson random variable of unit mean.

Remark M.2
.

In the case of the step initial condition one finds
c_{1}=+\infty
. This feature also holds for
\nu=0
, i.e.
for the SSEP, see
Eq.(13) in
Imamura et al. 2017
.
It may thus be a degenerate case, as the MFT does not allow to study the edge.
One would need to study the case
Y_{0}<0
where the tracer starts in the bulk.

M.2
Second method using the rate function
\Psi(u)

In the second approach one obtains the tracer
CGF directly from the function
\Psi(u)
that we have computed.
Setting
J=0
in (
10
) leads to a relation
between
u
and
X
, i.e., we define
u=u^{*}(X)
the solution of

\displaystyle 2\nu u^{*}(X)(\partial_{u}\Psi)(u,X)|_{u=u^{*}(X)}=\log(1+u^{*}(X)\omega_{u^{*}(X)})

(SM.221)

From (
SI.59
), setting
J=0
and
making explicit the
X
dependence we obtain

\chi(X)=\Phi(0;X)=\Psi(u^{*}(X),X)+\frac{1}{2\nu}[\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-u^{*}\omega_{u^{*}})-F(\omega_{u^{*}})]|_{u^{*}=u^{*}(X)}

(SM.222)

Differentiating with respect to
X
, we obtain

\begin{split}\chi^{\prime}(X)=\partial_{X}\Phi(0,X)&=u^{*\prime}(X)\left(\partial_{u}\Psi(u^{*},X)-\frac{\log(1+\omega_{u^{*}}u^{*})}{2\nu u^{*}}\right)+(\partial_{X}\Psi)(u^{*},X)=(\partial_{X}\Psi)(u^{*},X)\end{split}

(SM.223)

since the first part is zero by construction. Hence we obtain from (
SM.197
)

\lambda=(\partial_{X}\Psi)(u^{*}(X),X)

(SM.224)

In summary, to obtain the cumulants
c_{n}
of the tracer’s position we must eliminate
u
and
X

in the system of
three
equations

\displaystyle\partial_{u}\Psi(u,X)=\sum_{n\geqslant 0}\frac{\Psi^{(n+1)}(0,X)}{n!}u^{n}=\frac{\log(1+u\omega_{u})}{2\nu u}=\sum_{n\geqslant 0}\frac{a_{n}}{n!}u^{n}

(SM.225)

\displaystyle\partial_{X}\Psi(u,X)=\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{\Psi^{(n,1)}(0,X)}{n!}u^{n}=\lambda\quad,\quad\Psi^{(n,m)}(u,X)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\partial_{u}^{n}\partial_{X}^{m}\Psi(u,X)

(SM.226)

\displaystyle C^{\prime}(\lambda)=c_{1}+\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{1}{n!}c_{n+1}\lambda^{n}=X

(SM.227)

where we recall that
\omega_{u}
is given in (
SH.52
).
An equivalent expression for the
coefficients
a_{n}
can be read from Eqs. (
SQ.351
), (
SQ.362
)

a_{n}=-\frac{\varrho_{1}^{n+1}\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)^{n+1}}{2\nu(n+1)}\frac{d^{n}}{dy^{n}}\left(\frac{(y(1-y))^{n}}{(y-\varrho_{1})^{n+1}}\right)|_{y=\varrho_{2}}

(SM.228)

To order zero we obtain that
c_{1}
is the root of the equation

\Psi^{(1,0)}(0;c_{1})=\frac{1}{2\nu(1-\alpha)}\quad,\quad\alpha=\frac{\left(1-\varrho_{1}\right)\varrho_{2}}{\varrho_{1}\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)}

(SM.229)

which, as expected from (
SK.85
) and (
SK.88
) is equivalent to the condition
\kappa_{1}(c_{1})=0
obtained above.

Further expansion can be performed in terms of the derivatives
\Psi^{(n,m)}(0;c_{1})
. We obtain for
the second cumulant

c_{2}=\frac{\frac{\alpha+1}{4(\alpha-1)^{3}\nu}-\Psi^{(2,0)}}{\left(\Psi^{(1,1)}\right)^{2}}

(SM.230)

and more complicated expressions for the higher cumulants.
Using the expressions for the
\Psi^{n}(0;X)
obtained in Section
J

yields the
c_{n}
for arbitrary
\varrho_{1}
,
\varrho_{2}
. We have checked that
taking the limit
\varrho_{2}=\varrho_{1}=\varrho
of (
SM.230
) recovers
the result (
SM.212
) (taking into account the first order correction
of
c_{1}
in
\varrho_{1}-\varrho_{2}
).

Appendix N
Macroscopic fluctuation theory approach to the large deviations

N.1
Framework and boundary conditions

The fluctuating hydrodynamics equation for the WASEP read (setting
D_{0}=1
and
\sigma(\varrho)=2\varrho(1-\varrho)
)

\partial_{t}\varrho=\partial^{2}_{x}\varrho-\partial_{x}(2\nu\varrho(1-\varrho)+\sqrt{2\varepsilon\varrho(1-\varrho)}\eta)

(SN.231)

In addition we introduce the fields

z(x,t)=e^{2\nu h(x,t)}\quad,\quad h(x,t)=h(0,t)+\int_{0}^{x}dy\varrho(y,t)\quad,\quad h(0,t)=-Q_{t}=-\int_{0}^{+\infty}dx(\varrho(x,t)-\varrho(x,0))

(SN.232)

where
h(x,t)=-J(x,t)
and

Q_{t}
is the number of particles
which have crossed the origin from left to right minus right to left during time
t
,
and is also the integrated current
Q_{t}=J(0,T)=\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}j(0,t^{\prime})
.

The expectation value of any observable of the form
\exp(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}{\cal O})
can be represented as a MSR path integral over the field
\varrho
and the response field

\tilde{\varrho}/\varepsilon
as

\begin{split}&\big\langle e^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}{\cal O}}\big\rangle=\iiint{\cal D}\varrho{\cal D}\tilde{\varrho}e^{-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left[S[\varrho,\tilde{\varrho}]+\mathcal{F}(\varrho)-{\cal O}\right]}\end{split}

(SN.233)

with the dynamical action (after averaging over the noise and integration by part)

S[\varrho,\tilde{\varrho}]=\iint\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\left(\tilde{\varrho}(\partial_{t}\varrho-\partial_{x}^{2}\varrho)-\varrho(1-\varrho)(\partial_{x}\tilde{\varrho})^{2}-2\nu\varrho(1-\varrho)\partial_{x}\tilde{\varrho}\right)

(SN.234)

where we introduced the measure on the initial condition for the density field, parametrized
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009b
by

\mathcal{F}(\varrho)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\bar{\varrho}(x)}^{\varrho(x,0)}\mathrm{d}r\frac{\varrho(x,0)-r}{r(1-r)}

(SN.235)

where
\bar{\varrho}(x)=\varrho_{1}\Theta(-x)+\varrho_{2}\Theta(x)
. We recall
that
\langle\cdot\rangle
denotes the expectation value over
the noise
\eta
and over the initial density. We will only
consider here observables
{\cal O}
which depends on the density field
only at the final and initial times.

As
\varepsilon\to 0
the path integral is dominated by a saddle point.
The saddle point equations give back the standard MFT equations of the WASEP for the fields
(\varrho,\tilde{\varrho})
,
which at the saddle point will be denoted
(\varrho,\tilde{\varrho})|_{SP}=(q,p)
(and for simplicity we keep the same
notation for
z
and
h
). They read

\begin{split}\partial_{t}q&=\partial_{x}\left[\partial_{x}q-2q(1-q)(\partial_{x}p+\nu)\right]\>,\\
-\partial_{t}p&=\partial_{x}^{2}p+(1-2q)\partial_{x}p(\partial_{x}p+2\nu)\>,\end{split}

(SN.236)

These equations have to be supplemented by boundary conditions at the final and initial time. These
conditions depend on the chosen observable
{\cal O}
. Let us discuss two choices of observables.

\displaystyle{\cal O}={\cal O}_{1}=P\,J(X,T)=-P\,h(X,T)

(SN.237)

\displaystyle{\cal O}={\cal O}_{2}=\frac{1}{2\nu}[{\rm Li}_{2}(-u\omega_{uz(X,T)}z(X,T))-F(\omega_{uz(X,T)})]

(SN.238)

The first observable corresponds to the CGF of the current in (
9
) while
the second corresponds to the (more complicated) observable which appears in (
4
),
where in the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
the additional variable

\omega
can be taken at its saddle point value
\omega=\omega_{uz(X,T)}
.

To derive the boundary condition associated to each observable we
express them in terms of the density field, and take the functional
derivative. Recalling that

h(X,T)=\int_{0}^{+\infty}dy\varrho(y,0)-\int_{X}^{+\infty}dy\varrho(y,T)

(SN.239)

One finds

\displaystyle\frac{\delta{\cal O}_{1}}{\delta\varrho(x,t)}=-P(\delta(t)\Theta(x)-\delta(t-T)\Theta(x-X))

(SN.240)

\displaystyle\frac{\delta{\cal O}_{2}}{\delta\varrho(x,t)}=-\log(1+u\omega_{uz(X,T)}z(X,T))(\delta(t)\Theta(x)-\delta(t-T)\Theta(x-X))

(SN.241)

To obtain the second equation we note that the
functional derivative applied to
\omega_{uz(X,T)}
vanishes from the saddle point
condition.

The causality property of the response field imposes that
p(x,0^{-})=0
and
p(x,T+0^{+})=0
.
For the two-sided Bernoulli initial condition one thus obtains that one
must solve the saddle point equations (
SN.236
)
with the following initial and terminal conditions on the fields
p,q
: for the observable
{\cal O}_{1}

one has

\begin{split}&p(x,T)=P\,\Theta(x-X)\\
&p(x,0)=P\,\Theta(x)+{\cal F}^{\prime}(q(x,0))=P\,\Theta(x)+\log\frac{q(x,0)(1-\bar{q}(x))}{\bar{q}(x)(1-q(x,0))}\end{split}

(SN.242)

where
\bar{q}(x)=\bar{\varrho}(x)
.
For the observable
{\cal O}_{2}
one has the same conditions, with to the substitution

P=\log(1+u\omega_{uz(X,T)}z(X,T))

(SN.243)

This is now a self-consistent equation, since
where
z(X,T)=\exp(2\nu(\int_{0}^{+\infty}dyq(y,0)-\int_{X}^{+\infty}dyq(y,T)))

itself depends on the solution. However we see that (
SN.243
) is precisely
the relation obtained in (
10
) from Lagrange inversion at the saddle point. Thus
the calculation is in fact the same for the two observables, but expressed in different
ensembles.

In practice the solution to the above system can be studied in perturbation theory in
P
.
One writes
q(x,t)=\bar{\varrho}(x)+\sum_{n\geqslant 1}P^{n}\varrho_{n}(x,t)
and
p(x,t)=\sum_{n\geqslant 1}P^{n}p_{n}(x,t)
, and computes iteratively the functions
q_{n},p_{n}
. Once
q_{n},p_{n}
is known
one can obtain from them the
n
order cumulant of the integrated current
J
, and
the coefficients
\kappa_{n}
. The procedure becomes quickly extremely tedious as

n
increases. For the general MFT model with driving, including the WASEP as
a special case,
it was performed very recently in
Berlioz et al. 2024
, up to and including
n=3
.

Remark N.1
.

For the step initial condition, the boundary conditions become

\begin{split}p(x,T)&=P\,\Theta(x-X)\quad,\quad P=\log(1+uz(X,T))\\
q(x,0)&=\Theta(-x)\end{split}

(SN.244)

with
h(x,0)=x\Theta(-x)
and
z(x,0)=\Theta(x)+e^{2\nu x}\Theta(-x)

N.2
MFT equations and change of variables

We explain in this Section how to transform the MFT equations (
SN.236
) to reveal their integrability. We expect that a similar derivation will unveil the integrability of all MFT with an asymmetry parameter, a constant diffusion parameter and a quadratic mobility, i.e.,
\sigma(\varrho)
being a second order polynomial in the density. Note that we have shown in Ref.
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2023
that all MFT with quadratic mobility for the symmetric case
\nu=0
were integrable by a direct mapping to the imaginary-time DNLS system, which is itself gauge equivalent to the imaginary-time NLS system.

We start by first performing the transformation
r=\partial_{x}p
, leading to

\begin{split}\partial_{t}q&=\partial_{x}\left[\partial_{x}q-2q(1-q)(r+\nu)\right]\\
-\partial_{t}r&=\partial_{x}^{2}r+\partial_{x}\left(r(1-2q)(r+2\nu)\right)\end{split}

(SN.245)

The Cole-Hopf transformation
q=\frac{1}{2\nu}\partial_{x}\log z
then yields

\begin{split}\partial_{t}z&=\partial_{x}^{2}z-\partial_{x}z\left(2\nu+2r-\frac{1}{\nu}r\partial_{x}\log z\right)\\
-\partial_{t}r&=\partial_{x}^{2}r+\partial_{x}\left(r(1-\frac{1}{\nu}\partial_{x}\log z)(r+2\nu)\right)\end{split}

(SN.246)

These equations seem highly nonsymmetric and we thus propose the following change of variable to make them symmetric
1
1

1

To provide some insight on this change, we note that we originally tried an ansatz
r=f(\tilde{r}z)
, obtained an equation for the time derivative
\partial_{t}\tilde{r}
in which we have canceled the prefactor of
\partial_{x}^{2}z
which gave the explicit expression of
f
.
. Let

\tilde{r}(x,t)=-\frac{r(x,t)}{(2\nu+r(x,t))z(x,t)}\quad\Longleftrightarrow r=-\frac{2\nu\tilde{r}z}{1+\tilde{r}z}

(SN.247)

we then have

\begin{split}\partial_{t}z&=\partial_{x}^{2}z-2\nu\partial_{x}z-2\frac{\tilde{r}\partial_{x}z\left(\partial_{x}z-2\nu z\right)}{1+\tilde{r}z}\\
-\partial_{t}\tilde{r}&=\partial_{x}^{2}\tilde{r}+2\nu\partial_{x}\tilde{r}-2\frac{z\partial_{x}\tilde{r}\left(\partial_{x}\tilde{r}+2\nu\tilde{r}\right)}{1+\tilde{r}z}\end{split}

(SN.248)

where to establish the second equation one needs to use also the first one.
We propose to bias the variables as follows to symmetrize the interaction term and eliminate the drift

z(x,t)=Z(x,t)e^{\nu x-\nu^{2}t}\quad,\quad\tilde{r}(x,t)=R(x,t)e^{-\nu x+\nu^{2}t}

(SN.249)

and we finally obtain a pair of non-linear equations

\begin{split}\partial_{t}Z&=\partial_{x}^{2}Z-\frac{2R}{1+RZ}\left((\partial_{x}Z)^{2}-(\nu Z)^{2}\right)\\
-\partial_{t}R&=\partial_{x}^{2}R-\frac{2Z}{1+RZ}\left((\partial_{x}R)^{2}-(\nu R)^{2}\right)\end{split}

(SN.250)

which we have found to be convenient for multiple reasons that we now expose. The extreme case
\nu\to(0,\infty)
allow to obtain the NLS and DNLS systems which correspond to the KPZ/SSEP limits as follows

1.

To obtain DNLS, one formally takes
\nu=0
and then applies the stereographic change of variable

\begin{split}Q&=\frac{Z}{1+ZR}\\
P&=\partial_{x}R\end{split}

(SN.251)

This leads to

\begin{split}\partial_{t}Q&=\partial_{x}^{2}Q+2\partial_{x}(Q^{2}P)\\
-\partial_{t}P&=\partial_{x}^{2}P-2\partial_{x}(QP^{2})\end{split}

(SN.252)

We can additionally map DNLS to NLS using the following non-local change of variable

u=(Q^{2}P+\partial_{x}Q)e^{2\int^{x}\mathrm{d}yQP},\quad v=-Pe^{-2\int^{x}\mathrm{d}yQP}

(SN.253)

as explained in
(
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2023
, Appendix L)
in the context of the MFT and originally derived in
Wadati and Sogo 1983
.

2.

To obtain NLS, one rescales
R=\tilde{R}/\nu^{2}
and then take the limit
\nu\to\infty
. This leads to the system

\begin{split}\partial_{t}Z&=\partial_{x}^{2}Z+2Z^{2}\tilde{R}\\
-\partial_{t}\tilde{R}&=\partial_{x}^{2}\tilde{R}+2Z\tilde{R}^{2}\end{split}

(SN.254)

Before introducing the last change of variable required to unveil the integrability of the MFT of the ASEP, we briefly comment the structure of the action under the new variables. Performing the change of
variable in the action (
SN.234
) (discarding boundary terms as well as the Jacobian since we study here the large deviations hence the saddle point)
we find that the action in the new variables
can be represented as

\begin{split}S[Z,R]&=\iint\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x\left[-\frac{R}{1+RZ}\left(\partial_{t}Z-\partial_{x}^{2}Z\right)+\frac{R^{2}}{(1+RZ)^{2}}\left(\nu^{2}Z^{2}-(\partial_{x}Z)^{2}\right)\right]\\
&=\iint\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x\left[\partial_{Z}\mathrm{Li}_{1}(-RZ)\partial_{t}Z-[\partial_{Z}\mathrm{Li}_{1}(-RZ)\partial_{x}^{2}Z+\partial^{2}_{Z}\mathrm{Li}_{1}(-RZ)(\partial_{x}Z)^{2}]+\frac{\nu^{2}R^{2}Z^{2}}{(1+RZ)^{2}}\right]\\
&=\iint\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x\left[-\frac{R\partial_{t}Z}{1+RZ}-\frac{\partial_{x}R\partial_{x}Z-\nu^{2}Z^{2}R^{2}}{(1+RZ)^{2}}\right]\\
\end{split}

(SN.255)

It can be checked that the saddle point equations associated to this action indeed reproduces
the system (
SN.250
).

Remark N.2
.

The Hamiltonian appearing in the third line of (
SN.255
) resembles the non-linear Schrodinger Hamiltonian with a coherent dressing, see
(
Stone et al. 2000
, Eq. (3.6))
. The equation (
SN.255
) for
\nu=0
previously appeared in
(
Swann et al. 2023
, Eqs. (35-36))
in the context of a SU(2) Heisenberg chain.

We now show that this action is symmetric in the variables
(Z,R)
up to the time reversal
t\to-t
. Only the time derivative term seems nontrivially symmetric. Using

\partial_{t}\mathrm{Li}_{1}(-RZ)=\partial_{R}\mathrm{Li}_{1}(-RZ)\partial_{t}R+\partial_{Z}\mathrm{Li}_{1}(\gamma RZ)\partial_{t}Z

(SN.256)

we have up to boundary terms

\int\mathrm{d}t\partial_{Z}\mathrm{Li}_{1}(-RZ)\partial_{t}z=-\int\mathrm{d}t\partial_{R}\mathrm{Li}_{1}(-RZ)\partial_{t}R

(SN.257)

The symmetry of the space derivative term expressed in terms of
\mathrm{Li}_{1}
arises from the fact that

\begin{split}&\partial_{x}\left(\partial_{Z}\mathrm{Li}_{1}(-RZ)\partial_{x}Z-\partial_{R}\mathrm{Li}_{1}(-RZ)\partial_{x}R\right)\\
&=(\partial_{Z}\mathrm{Li}_{1}(-RZ)\partial^{2}_{x}z+\partial_{Z}^{2}\mathrm{Li}_{1}(-RZ)(\partial_{x}Z)^{2})-(\partial_{R}\mathrm{Li}_{1}(-RZ)\partial_{x}^{2}R+\partial_{R}^{2}\mathrm{Li}_{1}(-RZ)(\partial_{x}R)^{2})\end{split}

(SN.258)

and thus discarding any boundary term we have

\int\mathrm{d}x(\partial_{Z}\mathrm{Li}_{1}(-RZ)\partial^{2}_{x}Z+\partial_{Z}^{2}\mathrm{Li}_{1}(-RZ)(\partial_{x}Z)^{2})=\int\mathrm{d}x(\partial_{R}\mathrm{Li}_{1}(-RZ)\partial_{x}^{2}R+\partial_{R}^{2}\mathrm{Li}_{1}(-RZ)(\partial_{x}R)^{2})

(SN.259)

Additionally, we guess the first two conserved quantities of the dynamics with the new variables

\partial_{t}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\frac{ZR}{1+ZR}\right)=0,\quad\partial_{t}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\frac{Z\partial_{x}R}{1+ZR}\right)=0

(SN.260)

We can indeed find the associated currents and check that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left(\frac{ZR}{1+ZR}\right)=\partial_{x}\left(\frac{-Z\partial_{x}R+R\partial_{x}Z}{(1+RZ)^{2}}\right)

(SN.261)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left(\frac{-Z\partial_{x}R}{1+ZR}\right)=\partial_{x}\left(\frac{Z}{1+RZ}\partial_{x}^{2}R+\frac{\partial_{x}R(2Z^{2}\partial_{x}R+\partial_{x}Z)}{(1+RZ)^{2}}+\nu^{2}\frac{1+2RZ}{(1+RZ)^{2}}\right)

(SN.262)

N.3
Mapping the WASEP to the anisotropic Landau-Lifshitz model

The last change of variable we now introduce allows us to represent (
SN.250
) as a complex extension of the dynamics of the classical anisotropic Landau-Lifshitz model in its stereographic frame
Lakshmanan and Nakamura 1984
;
Lakshmanan 2011
.
Let us define the matrix spin
\mathcal{S}

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}&=\frac{1}{{1+RZ}}\begin{pmatrix}1-RZ&2R\\
2Z&-(1-RZ)\\
\end{pmatrix}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\begin{pmatrix}S_{z}&S_{+}\\
S_{-}&-S_{z}\\
\end{pmatrix}\end{split}

(SN.263)

The direct properties of this representation are
\mathcal{S}^{2}=I_{d}
,
\mathrm{Det}\mathcal{S}=-1
and
S_{-}S_{+}+S_{z}^{2}=1
. Discarding again the Jacobian and any boundary term (total derivative), the action (
SN.255
) becomes, in the new variables

S[\mathcal{S}]=\iint\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x\left[\frac{S_{-}\partial_{t}S_{+}-S_{+}\partial_{t}S_{-}}{4(1+S_{z})}-\frac{1}{4}(\partial_{x}S_{+}\partial_{x}S_{-}+(\partial_{x}S_{z})^{2}-\nu^{2}(S_{z}-1)^{2})\right]

(SN.264)

The anisotropy of the WASEP is then mapped exactly to the anisotropy of the Landau-Lifshitz model.

To make more precise the connection let us recall that
in
Lakshmanan 2011
the anisotropic Landau-Lifshitz model in a field was
studied. We note that the spin representation in Eq. (4.b) there is consistent with identifying
\omega=R
and
\omega^{*}=Z
.
With this identification, the dynamical equation (13) there (together with the remark below that about adding
a longitudinal field
B^{L}
) is exactly equivalent to our system (
SN.250
)
under the identification

2A=\mu B^{L}=-\nu^{2}\quad,\quad R(x,t)=\omega(x,\tau)|_{\tau\to\mathbf{i}t}\quad,\quad Z(x,t)=\omega^{*}(x,\tau)|_{\tau\to\mathbf{i}t}

(SN.265)

With this choice of parameter the Hamiltonian studied in
Lakshmanan 2011
reads

\mathcal{H}_{LL}=\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}x\,(\partial_{x}S_{+}\partial_{x}S_{-}+(\partial_{x}S_{z})^{2}-\nu^{2}(S_{z}-1)^{2})

(SN.266)

for a classical spin
\vec{S}=(S_{x},S_{y},S_{z})
on the sphere
\vec{S}^{2}=1
,
with
S_{\pm}=S_{x}\pm\mathbf{i}S_{y}
. The complex variable
\omega
is then the
stereographic projection of the spin
\vec{S}
(from the south pole).
This Hamiltonian, using
\frac{d}{d\tau}S_{i}=\{S_{i},H_{LL}\}

and
\{S_{i}(x),S_{j}(x^{\prime})\}=\sum_{k}\epsilon_{ijk}S_{k}(x)\delta(x-x^{\prime})
,
leads to the LL equations (we recall that

\{A,B\}=\sum_{i,j,k}\epsilon_{ijk}(\partial_{S_{i}}A)(\partial_{S_{j}}B)S_{k}
)

\partial_{\tau}\vec{S}=\vec{S}\wedge(\partial_{x}^{2}\vec{S}+\nu^{2}(S_{z}-1)\vec{e}_{z})

(SN.267)

equivalently

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}S_{\pm}=\pm\mathbf{i}\partial_{x}(S_{z}\partial_{x}S_{\pm}-S_{\pm}\partial_{x}S_{z})\pm\mathbf{i}\nu^{2}S_{\pm}(1-S_{z})

(SN.268)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}S_{z}=\frac{\mathbf{i}}{2}\partial_{x}(S_{+}\partial_{x}S_{-}-S_{-}\partial_{x}S_{+})

(SN.269)

Changing
\tau=\mathbf{i}t
and using the correspondence (
SN.263
) one can verify that indeed these equations
are equivalent to (
SN.250
).

Remark N.3

(Spin representation with the MFT variables and separation of variables)
.

We can also represent the spin variable as a function of the original MFT variables
(z,r)
. In this case, we observe a separation of variables where the spin matrix can be factorised into the form
\mathcal{S}=g_{1}gg_{1}^{-1}
where
g_{1}
solely depends on
z
while
g
depends on
r
.

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}&=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\nu+r}{\nu}&-\frac{re^{\nu(x-\nu t)}}{\nu z}\\
\frac{z(2\nu+r)e^{\nu(\nu t-x)}}{\nu}&-\frac{\nu+r}{\nu}\\
\end{pmatrix}\\
&=\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\sqrt{z}}e^{\frac{\nu}{2}x-\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}t}&0\\
0&\sqrt{z}e^{-\frac{\nu}{2}x+\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}t}\\
\end{pmatrix}}_{g_{1}}\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\nu+r}{\nu}&-\frac{r}{\nu}\\
\frac{(2\nu+r)}{\nu}&-\frac{\nu+r}{\nu}\\
\end{pmatrix}}_{g}\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{z}e^{-\frac{\nu}{2}x+\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}t}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{\sqrt{z}}e^{\frac{\nu}{2}x-\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}t}\\
\end{pmatrix}}_{g_{1}^{-1}}\end{split}

(SN.270)

Additionally, we can factorise the inner matrix as
g=g_{2}\sigma_{3}g_{2}^{-1}
, i.e.

\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\nu+r}{\nu}&-\frac{r}{\nu}\\
\frac{(2\nu+r)}{\nu}&-\frac{\nu+r}{\nu}\\
\end{pmatrix}=\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}\frac{r}{2\nu}&\frac{r}{2\nu}\\
\frac{r}{2\nu}&\frac{r}{2\nu}+1\\
\end{pmatrix}}_{g_{2}}\sigma_{3}\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}\frac{r}{2\nu}&\frac{r}{2\nu}\\
\frac{r}{2\nu}&\frac{r}{2\nu}+1\\
\end{pmatrix}^{-1}}_{g_{2}^{-1}}

(SN.271)

Thus, we overall have
\mathcal{S}=g_{1}g_{2}\sigma_{3}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}
.
We comment on this separation of variable later in Section
N.5
. Note that for
\nu\to 0
, one needs to proceed to a change of variable
r\to\nu r
so that the spin remains well defined. This spin representation is a priori different from the one considered for the SSEP in
Tailleur et al. 2008

although they may differ by a gauge transformation.

N.4
Lax pair representation of the MFT

The Lax pair for the anisotropic Landau-Lifshitz magnet has been known for a few decades, see e.g., Ref.
Nakamura and Sasada 1982
. To obtain a Lax pair for the MFT of the WASEP we
use the conventions of this reference, with minor changes: we set
\lambda=-k/2
and since the time there,
which we denote by
\tau
, is
\tau=\mathbf{i}t
(see above), we set
M=-\mathbf{i}M_{1}-\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}\sigma_{3}
,
with an additional term, see below.
Let
\vec{v}(x,t,k)
be a two-dimensional vector depending on space, time and a spectral parameter
k
. We define the linear problem

\partial_{x}\vec{v}=L\vec{v},\quad\partial_{t}\vec{v}=M\vec{v}

(SN.272)

where
L,M
are two
2\times 2
matrices. The compatibility of this system
\partial_{xt}=\partial_{tx}
provides a zero-curvature condition

\partial_{t}L-\partial_{x}M+[L,M]=0

(SN.273)

where
[L,M]=LM-ML
. A nonlinear system is said to be integrable if there exist a pair of Lax matrices
(L,M)
so that their compatibility yields back the original system. In the present case, we define

L=-\frac{\mathbf{i}k}{2}\mathcal{S}+\mu[\sigma_{3},\mathcal{S}]

(SN.274)

and

M=\frac{k^{2}}{2}\mathcal{S}+\frac{\mathbf{i}k}{2}\mathcal{S}\partial_{x}\mathcal{S}+\mathbf{i}\mu k[\sigma_{3},\mathcal{S}]-\mu[\sigma_{3},\mathcal{S}\partial_{x}\mathcal{S}]+4\mu^{2}\{\sigma_{3},\mathcal{S}\}\sigma_{3}-\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}\sigma_{3}

(SN.275)

where
\{\sigma_{3},\mathcal{S}\}=\sigma_{3}\mathcal{S}+\mathcal{S}\sigma_{3}
and

\mu=\sqrt{-2A}/4=\frac{\nu}{4}
from
Nakamura and Sasada 1982
and (
SN.265
)
(although
\mu=-\nu/4
also leads to a Lax pair). Note that we found that it was necessary to add
the term
-\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}\sigma_{3}
in the presence of the magnetic field.
Choosing
\mu=\frac{\nu}{4}
,
we write explicitly the Lax pair in terms of the variables
(Z,R)
as

L=\frac{1}{1+RZ}\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{\mathbf{i}k}{2}\left(1-RZ\right)&(\nu-\mathbf{i}k)R\\
-(\nu+\mathbf{i}k)Z&\frac{\mathbf{i}k}{2}\left(1-RZ\right)\\
\end{pmatrix}

(SN.276)

and

\begin{split}&M=\frac{1}{(1+RZ)^{2}}\\
&\begin{pmatrix}\frac{k^{2}}{2}-\nu^{2}ZR-\mathbf{i}k(Z\partial_{x}R-R\partial_{x}Z)-R^{2}Z^{2}\left(\frac{k^{2}}{2}+\nu^{2}\right)&(k+\mathbf{i}\nu)\left(R\left(k+R\left(kZ+\mathbf{i}\partial_{x}Z\right)\right)+\mathbf{i}\partial_{x}R\right)\\
(k-\mathbf{i}\nu)\left(Z\left(k+Z\left(kR-\mathbf{i}\partial_{x}R\right)\right)-\mathbf{i}\partial_{x}Z\right)&-\frac{k^{2}}{2}+\nu^{2}ZR+\mathbf{i}k(Z\partial_{x}R-R\partial_{x}Z)+R^{2}Z^{2}\left(\frac{k^{2}}{2}+\nu^{2}\right)\\
\end{pmatrix}\end{split}

(SN.277)

One can now show that the zero curvature condition of these two matrices is equivalent to the system (
SN.250
). This is
checked by explicit calculation, inserting these matrices in the l.h.s of (
SN.273
),
and replacing the time derivatives of
Z
and
R
by their expressions from
(
SN.250
). A massive cancellation occurs and one finds zero.
These matrices thus form a good Lax pair for the system (
SN.250
). This open the way to study the scattering and inverse scattering problem associated with this Lax pair,
which is deferred to a subsequent work.

Remark N.4
.

It was noted in
Rizkallah et al. 2023
that a number of dualities do exist in the macroscopic fluctuation theory. It would be interesting to study the implications of such dualities on the mappings we have proposed in this Section.

N.5
Comment on gauge equivalences

There is no uniqueness of the Lax pair to represent (
SN.250
) and thus we cannot exclude that other gauge equivalent representations of this pair can be convenient to solve a scattering problem. A gauge transformation on the Lax matrices is defined as the map
(L,M)\mapsto(\tilde{L},\tilde{M})
involving an invertible gauge
\mathcal{G}
so that

\tilde{L}=\mathcal{G}^{-1}L\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}^{-1}\partial_{x}\mathcal{G},\quad\tilde{M}=\mathcal{G}^{-1}M\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}^{-1}\partial_{t}\mathcal{G}

(SN.278)

Since we have shown above that the spin matrix can get factorised as
\mathcal{S}=g_{1}g_{2}\sigma_{3}(g_{1}g_{2})^{-1}
, proposing the following gauge
\mathcal{G}=g_{1}g_{2}
would ensure that the coefficient in front of the spectral parameter
k
in the space Lax matrix
\tilde{L}
would be independent of the space-time variables
(x,t)
. While this consideration emerges from an algebraic standpoint, gauge equivalences have been historically paramount from a physics standpoint to relate different models together, as we now recall.

We have mapped in this work the MFT of the WASEP to the anisotropic Landau-Lifshitz model. We refer the reader to various references for additional context,
Lakshmanan 2011
for a review on the Landau-Lifshitz model,
Lakshmanan and Nakamura 1984
for the analysis of the Landau-Lifshitz model in the stereographic frame. The mappings between symmetric exclusion processes (including the SSEP) and isotropic spin chains was
considered in
Tailleur et al. 2008
, and
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009a
;
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009b
.
For a recent appearance of the Landau-Lifshitz model in the context of an entanglement entropy calculations in random unitary circuits, see
Swann et al. 2023
.

The anisotropic Landau-Lifshitz model has other remarkable mappings in the context of integrability

•

The anisotropic Landau-Lifshitz model is gauge equivalent to the isotropic Landau-Lifshitz model and the nonlinear Schrodinger equation, see
Nakamura and Sasada 1982
;
Kundu and Pshaev 1983
;
Zakharov and Takhtadzhyan 1979
.

•

The nonlinear Schrodinger equation has been shown to be gauge equivalent to the derivative nonlinear Schrodinger equation, see
Wadati and Sogo 1983
, through a triangular gauge transformation.

These mappings have recently been used to obtain exact results in the context of the study of the large deviations in a number of stochastic integrable models.

•

The weak noise theory of the KPZ equation was solved using its mapping to the imaginary-time NLS system in Ref.
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2021
for droplet initial condition and Ref.
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2022
for the flat and Brownian initial condition.

•

The MFT equations for the SSEP and KMP with quenched initial condition were solved in Refs.
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2023
;
Bettelheim et al. 2022a
;
Bettelheim et al. 2022b
using its mapping to the imaginary-time DNLS equation.

•

The MFT equations for the SSEP with annealed initial condition were solved in Ref.
Mallick et al. 2022
using the gauge transformation of Wadati and Sogo
Wadati and Sogo 1983
. The derivation of
Mallick et al. 2022
showed that the annealed initial condition of SSEP maps to the initial condition studied in
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2021
in the context of the KPZ equation, indicating that the scattering results from
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2021
can be used directly. This points out to the existence of additional duality results between particle models.

We believe more gauge equivalences do exist, both in the continuous and semi-discrete or discrete settings which will lead to a variety of new results in the large deviation study of driven diffusive systems.

N.6
Extension to the MFT of asymmetric models with quadratic mobility

We now extend the integrability argument we have derived to the MFT of asymmetric models with quadratic mobility. We recall that the general MFT depends on two functions
D(q)
and
\sigma(q)
. With the choice

\sigma(q)=\sigma_{A,B}(q)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=2Aq(B-q)
,
D(q)=1
, the MFT reads

\displaystyle\partial_{t}q

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}\left[\partial_{x}q-2Aq(B-q)(\partial_{x}p+\nu)\right]\>,

(SN.279a)

\displaystyle-\partial_{t}p

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}^{2}p+A(B-2q)\partial_{x}p(\partial_{x}p+2\nu)\>,

(SN.279b)

This system maps, under the change of variable

\begin{split}q(x,t)&=\frac{1}{2A\nu}\partial_{x}\log[Z(x,t)e^{AB\nu x-(AB\nu)^{2}t}],\\
\partial_{x}p(x,t)&=-\frac{2\nu R(x,t)Z(x,t)}{1+R(x,t)Z(x,t)}\end{split}

(SN.280)

exactly to (
SN.250
) with
\nu
replaced by
AB\nu
. Hence the
original MFT maps to the complex extension of the classical anisotropic Landau-Lifshitz model with anisotropy
AB\nu
. This mapping allows to solve the MFT for other models
such as the Weakly Asymmetric Simple Inclusion Process (WASIP), setting

A=-1
,
B=-1
, and the driven continuum KMP model (WAKMP) in the limit
A=-1
,
B=0
,
as well as their dual models, see Section
N.7
.

This remark will be most useful to address general initial conditions.
For stationary initial conditions however, i.e., for an initial density distributed as

{\cal P}(\varrho)=e^{-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}{\cal F}(\varrho,\bar{\varrho},\sigma_{A,B})}\quad,\quad{\cal F}(\varrho,\bar{\varrho},\sigma)=2\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\bar{\varrho}(x)}^{\varrho(x)}\mathrm{d}r\,\frac{\varrho(x)-r}{\sigma(r)}

(SN.281)

one can infer the result from the one we have obtained for the WASEP,
by simply extending to the driven case the mapping between quadratic models
introduced in
(
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009b
, Eq. (33))
.
In our notations it amounts to note that the action and the free energy transform as

\displaystyle S[\tilde{\varrho},\varrho,\sigma_{A,B},\nu]=\frac{1}{A}S[AB\tilde{\varrho},\frac{\varrho}{B},\sigma_{1,1},AB\nu]\quad,\quad{\cal F}(\varrho,\bar{\varrho},\sigma_{A,B})=\frac{1}{A}{\cal F}(\frac{\varrho}{B},\frac{\bar{\varrho}}{B},\sigma_{1,1})

(SN.282)

Consider the CGF of the integrated current
J
defined in
(
9
), which we denote
\phi_{A,B}(P)
for the model with
\sigma_{A,B}
.
Since the observable
J
is linear in the density field,
we need to rescale
P\to ABP
so that all terms in the
exponential scale as
1/A
under the
change
\varrho\to\varrho/B
. Hence one obtains

\phi_{A,B}(P,\bar{\varrho}(x),\nu)=\frac{1}{A}\phi_{\rm WASEP}(ABP,\frac{\bar{\varrho}(x)}{B},\nu AB)

(SN.283)

where
\phi_{\rm WASEP}=\phi_{1,1}
.

Hence we obtain the CGF of the integrated current
for the WASIP with
\sigma(\varrho)=2\varrho(1+\varrho)
for the two sided
stationary initial condition for that model, with densities
\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2}

\displaystyle\phi_{\rm WASIP}(P,\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2},\nu)=-\phi_{\rm WASEP}(P,-\varrho_{1},-\varrho_{2},\nu)

(SN.284)

\displaystyle\kappa_{n}^{\rm WASIP}(\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2},\nu)=-\kappa_{n}^{\rm WASEP}(-\varrho_{1},-\varrho_{2},\nu)

(SN.285)

This relation must be understood as an analytical continuation of the parameters (which, order
by order in a perturbative calculation is well defined).
Note that this model was studied recently for a different initial condition

Bettelheim and Meerson 2024
.

Similarly one obtains for the weakly asymmetric KPM model
with
\sigma(\varrho)=2\varrho^{2}

\displaystyle\phi_{\rm WAKMP}(P)=-\lim_{B\to 0}\phi_{\rm WASEP}(-BP,\frac{\varrho_{1}}{B},\frac{\varrho_{2}}{B},-B\nu)

(SN.286)

\displaystyle\kappa_{n}^{\rm WAKMP}(\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2},\nu)=(-)^{n+1}\lim_{B\to 0}B^{n}\kappa_{n}^{\rm WASEP}(\frac{\varrho_{1}}{B},\frac{\varrho_{2}}{B},-B\nu)

(SN.287)

Having the
\kappa_{n}
for the WASIP and the WAKMP we also obtain cumulants
c_{n}
of the
tracer position for these models from the relations (
SM.208
).

N.7
Duality in the MFT

There is a general duality property of the MFT models, see
Berlioz et al. 2024
;
Rizkallah et al. 2023

for more details.
Consider a MFT model with density field
\varrho(x,t)
and
parameters
D(\varrho),\sigma(\varrho),\nu
. The dual MFT model
describes a density field
\tilde{\varrho}(x,t)
and
parameters
\tilde{D}(\varrho),\tilde{\sigma}(\varrho),\tilde{\nu}
,
with the following involutive relations

\displaystyle\varrho(x,t)=\frac{1}{\tilde{\varrho}(k(x,t),t)}\quad,\quad\partial_{x}k(x,t)=\varrho(x,t)

(SN.288)

\displaystyle\tilde{D}(\varrho)=\frac{1}{\varrho^{2}}D\left(\frac{1}{\varrho}\right),\quad\tilde{\sigma}(\varrho)=\varrho\,\sigma\left(\frac{1}{\varrho}\right),\quad\tilde{\nu}=-\nu.

(SN.289)

In the first equation line one should eliminate the "height field"
k(x,t)
.
Denoting
(J(0,T),Y_{T})
the integrated current and position of tracer in
the original MFT model, this duality maps it onto
(-Y_{T},-J(0,T))
in the
dual MFT model
Berlioz et al. 2024
;
Rizkallah et al. 2023
.
This duality in some cases originates from a duality of the microscopic
models, for instance the ASEP is dual to a zero range process (ZRP)
Indeed the evolution of the gaps in the ASEP is described by a ZRP.
In the weakly asymmetric limit this becomes the duality between
the WASEP
D=1
,
\sigma=2\varrho(1-\varrho)
and a ZRP with

D=1/\varrho^{2}
and
\sigma=2(1-\frac{1}{\varrho})
.

This duality thus allows us to immediately obtain the integrated current
and tracer position large deviation functions for the ZRP,
with the corresponding double sided stationary initial condition

\tilde{\varrho}_{1}=1/\varrho_{1}
,
\tilde{\varrho}_{2}=1/\varrho_{2}
. from Section
N.6
,
it also yields the exact results for the dual models to all quadratic models,
which have

\tilde{D}(\varrho)=\frac{1}{\varrho^{2}},\quad\tilde{\sigma}(\varrho)=2A(B-\frac{1}{\varrho})

(SN.290)

in particular the dual of the WASIP and the dual of the WAKMP (an asymmetric version of the random average
process, see Table 1 in
Rizkallah et al. 2023
).

N.8
Optimal density at initial time
T=0

At time
T=0
the density field
\varrho(x)
of the WASEP is the coarse-grained version of the density field of the
ASEP with a double sided i.i.d. Bernoulli initial condition, i.e., for each
x
one can write

\varrho(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{1/\varepsilon}n_{i}
where
n_{i}=0,1
with probabilities
1-\bar{\varrho}(x)

and
\bar{\varrho}(x)
, respectively. Its probability distribution thus decouples
in space and takes the form

{\cal P}(\varrho)\sim e^{-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int dx\hat{f}(\varrho(x),\bar{\varrho}(x))}

(SN.291)

where
\hat{f}(\varrho,\bar{\varrho})
is the large deviation rate function of sums of i.i.d Bernoulli variables.
To obtain it one computes its CGF

\langle e^{\frac{p}{\epsilon}\varrho}\rangle=\langle e^{\frac{p}{\epsilon}\sum_{i=1}^{1/\varepsilon}n_{i}}\rangle=e^{\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log(1-\bar{\varrho}+\bar{\varrho}e^{p})}

(SN.292)

The Legendre inversion of
