\min_{\varrho}(p\varrho-\hat{f}(\varrho,\bar{\varrho}))=\log(1-\bar{\varrho}+\bar{\varrho}e^{p})

(SN.293)

then gives the "free energy density"

\hat{f}(\varrho,\bar{\varrho})=\varrho\log\frac{\varrho}{\bar{\varrho}}+(1-\varrho)\log\frac{1-\varrho}{1-\bar{\varrho}}=\int_{\bar{\varrho}}^{\varrho}\mathrm{d}z\frac{\varrho-z}{z(1-z)}

(SN.294)

for
0<\varrho<1
,
leading to the well known form (
2
) in the main text, see e.g
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009b
;
Bodineau and Derrida 2006
.

Once can then study the large deviations for any given observable at initial time, by computing the associated optimal particle density. In the MSR action, the only term which remains at time
t=0
is the free energy describing the stationary fluctuations of the density compared to the imposed density profile.
The optimal density is found by balancing this free energy with the observable of interest.

Let us illustrate this on the simplest example, and obtain the exact rate function
\Phi(J)
at initial time
T=0
.
The observable is
J(X,0)=-\int_{0}^{X}dy\varrho(y,0)
. We first compute its CGF,

\big\langle e^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}PJ}\big\rangle\sim e^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\phi(P)}
.
One has

\phi(P)=\max_{\varrho}\left[-P\int_{0}^{X}\mathrm{d}y\varrho(y)-\int dx\hat{f}(\varrho(x),\bar{\varrho}(x))\right]

(SN.295)

Taking a functional derivative w.r.t.
\varrho(x)
we find that the
optimum density is the solution of

-P\,{\rm sign}(X)\Theta_{X}(x)(x\in[0,X])-\log\frac{\varrho(x)(1-\bar{\varrho}(x))}{\bar{\varrho}(x)(1-\varrho(x))}=0

(SN.296)

where
\Theta_{X}(x)=\Theta(x\in[0,X])
for
X>0
and

\Theta_{X}(x)=\Theta(x\in[X,0])
for
X<0
. We thus obtain the
optimal density as

\varrho(x,0)=\varrho_{P}(x)=\frac{e^{-P\,{\rm sign}(X)\Theta_{X}(x)}\bar{\varrho}(x)}{1-\bar{\varrho}(x)+e^{-P\,{\rm sign}(X)\Theta_{X}(x)}\bar{\varrho}(x)}

(SN.297)

Using that
\phi^{\prime}(P)=-\int_{0}^{X}dy\varrho_{P}(y)
from taking a derivative of (
SN.295
),
we obtain

\phi(P)=|X|\log(1+\bar{\varrho}(X)(e^{-{\rm sign}(X)P}-1))

(SN.298)

Comparing with (
SN.292
) we see that it is consistent with
J=-{\rm sign}(X)R

where
R
is a sum of
|X|/\epsilon
Bernoulli variables, hence one finds (by the
same inversion as in (
SN.293
))

\displaystyle\Phi(J=J(X,0))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|X|f(\frac{-{\rm sign}(X)J}{|X|},\bar{\varrho}(X))

(SN.299)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-{\rm sgn}(X)J\log\frac{J}{-X\bar{\varrho}(X)}+|X|(1+\frac{J}{X})\log\frac{1+\frac{J}{X}}{1-\bar{\varrho}(X)}

(SN.300)

This gives the large deviation form of the PDF of
J

at time
T=0
, i.e.,
{\cal P}(J)\sim e^{-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Phi(J)}
, and we note that for
X>0
,
J\in[-X,0]
,
while for
X<0
,
J\in[0,X]
. At
X=0
one has
J(0,0)=0
.

One can ask how to match these variational calculations with setting
T=0
in our result for
\Psi(u)
in
(
5
). Taking
X>0
here, the variational problem then become

\Psi(u)\underset{T=0}{=}\max_{\varrho}\left[-f(ue^{2\nu\int_{0}^{X}\mathrm{d}y\varrho(y)})-\int_{\mathbb{R}}dxf(\varrho(x),\bar{\varrho}(x)))\right]

(SN.301)

where the function
f(a)
was defined below (
SO.308
). The optimum density obeys the same equation as (
SN.296
) setting

P=\log(1+uz\omega_{uz})
, where
z=e^{2\nu\int_{0}^{X}\varrho(y)}
and its
solution is given by (
SN.297
).

One has
\varrho(x,0)=\bar{\varrho}(x)
for
x
outside of
[0,X]
. For
x\in[0,X]
, the optimum density is given by

\varrho(x,0)=\frac{1}{1+\frac{1-\bar{\varrho}(x)}{\bar{\varrho}(x)}(1+u\omega e^{2\nu\int_{0}^{X}\varrho(y,0)})}

(SN.302)

with
\bar{\varrho}(x)=\varrho_{1}\Theta(-x)+\varrho_{2}\Theta(x)
and thus the optimal density is constant on the interval
[0,X]
(we will denote
\varrho(x,0)=\varrho(0)
in this interval). By integration, one can find a self-consistent equation for
\int_{0}^{X}\mathrm{d}y\varrho(y,0)
which can be evaluated numerically. We should now verify that to what extend this result should be consistent with the large deviation result

\begin{split}&\int_{1}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}w\big\langle e^{\frac{1}{2\nu\varepsilon}[{\rm Li}_{2}(-u\omega z(X,T=0))-F(\omega)]}\big\rangle\sim e^{-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Psi(u)}\end{split}

(SN.303)

From the MSR representation (
SN.233
), this is equivalent to

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}(\varrho)-\frac{1}{2\nu}[{\rm Li}_{2}(-u\omega_{uz}z)-F(\omega_{uz})]=\Psi(u)=-\frac{1}{2\nu}\int_{\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R}+\delta}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{2\mathbf{i}\pi y(1-y)}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\left(-u\frac{\varrho_{1}\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)(1-y)y}{\left(\varrho_{1}-y\right)\left(y-\varrho_{2}\right)}e^{2\nu yX}\right)\end{split}

(SN.304)

The left hand side can be evaluated using

\begin{split}&z=e^{2\nu X\varrho(0)},\quad\quad\omega_{uz}=\frac{\alpha-1+uz+\sqrt{\left(\alpha+uz-1\right)^{2}+4uz}}{2uz},\quad\quad\varrho(0)=\frac{1}{1+\frac{1-\varrho_{2}}{\varrho_{2}}(1+uz\omega_{uz})}\\
&\mathcal{F}(\varrho)=X\left(\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)\log(\frac{1-\varrho(0)}{1-\varrho_{2}})+\varrho_{2}\log(\frac{\varrho(0)}{\varrho_{2}})\right)\\
\end{split}

(SN.305)

Appendix O
Cumulants of the partition function
z
and perturbative expansion of
z(u)

The aim of this section is two-fold. First, since the field

z=e^{h}=e^{-2\nu J}
plays the role of a partition sum (particularly so in
the KPZ limit) it is interesting to be able to compute
its cumulants at the final point, i.e., the cumulants of the random variable
z=z(X,T)
,
solution of the WASEP-MFT stochastic equation.
These cumulants are of the form

\langle z^{n}\rangle_{c}\simeq z_{n}\varepsilon^{n-1}

(SO.306)

to leading order in
\varepsilon
. Furthermore, since
z
also denotes (and one should not confuse these two
objects, abusively denoted by the same letter) the field which enters in the MFT saddle point equations
(
SN.246
) (after a Cole-Hopf transform),
our second aim is to obtain the solution
z=z(X,T)
of these equations
at the final time, and at the special point
X
(which is also
a function of
u
, which enters the boundary condition
of these equations, see (
SN.242
)
(
SN.243
) and the line below). We will denote
z=z(u)
this latter quantity.

We will show how to
obtain both quantities from the rate function
\Psi(u)
and
its derivatives at
u=0
, for which we have explicit
expressions. To do so we will use our saddle point method, together with Legendre transforms
as in (
10
), (
11
).

Let us define
\hat{\Psi}(u)
to be the CGF of the random variable
z=z(X,T)
, from which
we can obtain the cumulants (
SO.306
). One has

\langle e^{-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}uz}\rangle\sim e^{-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\hat{\Psi}(u)}\quad,\quad z_{n}=(-1)^{n-1}\hat{\Psi}^{(n)}(0)

(SO.307)

By contrast, from the knowledge of
\Psi(u)
, the formula (
4
) gives us
only access to the expectation value of a more complicated observable

\langle e^{-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}f(uz)}\rangle\sim e^{-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Psi(u)}

(SO.308)

where
f(a)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=-\frac{1}{2\nu}[{\rm Li}_{2}(-a\omega_{a})-F(\omega_{a})]

and
F(\omega)=\mathrm{Li}_{2}\left(\frac{1}{\omega}\right)-\log\omega\log\alpha+\mathrm{Li}_{2}(\alpha)-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(1)
, where
\omega_{u}
is the value at the saddle point given in (
SH.52
). The derivative of the function
f
has a simpler
expression,

g(a)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=2\nu af^{\prime}(a)=\log(1+a\omega_{a})
, and we have
f(0)=0
.
We recall that the inverse function
g^{-1}
has a simple form,
g^{-1}(P)=(1-e^{-P})(e^{P}-\alpha)
,
see (
SH.58
).

One way to obtain the cumulants of the random variable
z=z(X,T)
is by expanding in powers of
u
both sides
of (
SO.308
), identifying and matching the powers of
\varepsilon

using (
SO.306
). It is not very convenient however, and a more powerful method
is to use Legendre transforms.

One defines the rate function
\hat{\Phi}(z)
for the PDF of
z
, i.e.,
{\cal P}(z)\sim e^{-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\hat{\Phi}(z)}
and write

\displaystyle\Psi(u)=\min_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{+}}[f(uz)+\hat{\Phi}(z)]

(SO.309)

\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}(u)=\min_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{+}}[uz+\hat{\Phi}(z)]

(SO.310)

Let us first consider the first equation, which yields the pair of equations

\Psi^{\prime}(u)=zf^{\prime}(uz)\quad,\quad\hat{\Phi}^{\prime}(z)=-uf^{\prime}(uz)

(SO.311)

The first equation define
z=z(u)
. It can be rewritten
as
2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)=g(uz)
which can be inverted, see above and
(
SH.58
), leading to

z=z(u)=\frac{(1-e^{-2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)})(e^{2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)}-\alpha)}{u}

(SO.312)

This expression is valid for the main branch of
\Psi(u)
, see discussion in Section
S
. We can Taylor expand this relation around
u=0
which should match the solution of the MFT equations obtained perturbatively term by term in powers of
u
, hence providing a check of the
solution of these equations

\begin{split}z(u)=&2(1-\alpha)\nu\Psi^{\prime}(0)+2\nu u\left((\alpha+1)\nu\Psi^{\prime}(0)^{2}+(1-\alpha)\Psi^{\prime\prime}(0)\right)\\
&+u^{2}\left(\frac{4}{3}(1-\alpha)\nu^{3}\Psi^{\prime}(0)^{3}+4(\alpha+1)\nu^{2}\Psi^{\prime}(0)\Psi^{\prime\prime}(0)+(1-\alpha)\nu\Psi^{(3)}(0)\right)+\mathcal{O}(u^{3})\end{split}

(SO.313)

Remark O.1
.

Note that one cannot obtain
q(X,T)
from
z(u)
as we only have access to a single space-time point
(X,T)
.

Pursuing the calculation to obtain the cumulants the second equation in (
SO.309
)
leads to the pair of equations

\hat{\Psi}^{\prime}(v)=z\quad,\quad\hat{\Phi}^{\prime}(z)=-v

(SO.314)

where we changed the name of one of the variable, but chose to
identify the other one with
z
. Since
z=z(u)
from the first
system, this leads to a relation between
u
and
v
. It is obtained by
noting that

u\Psi^{\prime}(u)=-z\hat{\Phi}^{\prime}(z)=zv\quad\Rightarrow\quad v=\frac{u\Psi^{\prime}(u)}{z(u)}

(SO.315)

so that finally we obtain

\hat{\Psi}^{\prime}(\frac{u\Psi^{\prime}(u)}{z(u)})=z(u)

(SO.316)

Expanding in powers of
u
using (
SO.312
) gives the relation between the
derivatives of
\hat{\Psi}
and those of
\Psi
. We obtain the first three cumulants as

\displaystyle z_{1}=2(1-\alpha)\nu\Psi^{\prime}(0)\quad,\quad z_{2}=-4(1-\alpha)\nu^{2}\left((\alpha+1)\nu\Psi^{\prime}(0)^{2}+(1-\alpha)\Psi^{\prime\prime}(0)\right)

(SO.317)

\displaystyle z_{3}=\frac{8}{3}(\alpha-1)\nu^{3}\left(18\left(\alpha^{2}-1\right)\nu\Psi^{\prime}(0)\Psi^{\prime\prime}(0)-2(\alpha(5\alpha+2)+5)\nu^{2}\Psi^{\prime}(0)^{3}-3(\alpha-1)^{2}\Psi^{(3)}(0)\right)

The first moment is simply
z_{1}=e^{-2\nu\kappa_{1}}
as expected (since
there are typical values). The second cumulant reads, for
\varrho_{1}=\varrho_{2}
,

z_{2}=8\nu^{2}\varrho(1-\varrho)z_{1}^{2}\sqrt{T}{\cal G}(y)\quad,\quad z_{1}=e^{-4\nu^{2}\varrho(1-\varrho)T+2\nu\varrho X}\quad,\quad y=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}(\nu(2\varrho-1)T+X/2)

(SO.318)

Remark O.2

(Cumulants of
z^{1/2}
in the stationary case
\varrho_{1}=\varrho_{2}
)
.

Interestingly the stationary case
\varrho_{1}=\varrho_{2}=\varrho
, the cumulants which can be extracted from
\psi(v)

are those of
z^{1/2}
. Indeed expanding on both sides of the large deviation identity (
SL.123
)
in powers of
v=\sqrt{u}

\displaystyle\big\langle e^{-\frac{1}{2\nu\varepsilon}\left[\mathrm{Li}_{2}\left(\frac{\sqrt{(uz)^{2}+4uz}-uz}{2}\right)-\mathrm{Li}_{2}\left(-\frac{\sqrt{(uz)^{2}+4uz}+uz}{2}\right)\right]}\big\rangle\sim e^{-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\psi(v)}

(SO.319)

we obtain

\begin{split}\psi(v)=&\frac{v}{\nu}\langle z^{\frac{1}{2}}\rangle+\frac{v^{2}}{2\nu^{2}\varepsilon}\left(\langle z^{\frac{1}{2}}\rangle^{2}-\langle z\rangle\right)+\frac{v^{3}}{72\nu^{3}\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\langle z^{\frac{3}{2}}\rangle\left(12-\nu^{2}\varepsilon^{2}\right)+24\langle z^{\frac{1}{2}}\rangle^{3}-36\langle z\rangle\langle z^{\frac{1}{2}}\rangle\right)+\mathcal{O}(v^{4})\\
\end{split}

(SO.320)

Appendix P
Limit to the weak-noise KPZ equation
\nu\to\infty

We study in this Section the
\nu\to\infty
limit of our results and show a perfect matching with the results previously obtained for the weak noise regime of the KPZ equation
for the two-sided Brownian initial condition in
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
;
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2022
, and for the droplet
initial condition in
Le Doussal et al. 2016
;
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2021
. It is known that the ASEP converges under a weak asymmetry
\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{N})
,
to the KPZ equation, see

Bertini and Giacomin 1997
;
Corwin and Shen 2018
.
Here we start from the WASEP, which is defined with an even weaker asymmetry
\mathcal{O}(\nu/N)
.
The large
\nu
limit again brings us
to the KPZ equation, but in its short time/weak noise regime.

In this Section we first parametrize the initial densities as

\varrho_{1}=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\tilde{\varrho}_{1}}{2\nu},\;\varrho_{2}=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\tilde{\varrho}_{2}}{2\nu}

(SP.321)

and in a second stage we study the limit of large
\nu
with
\tilde{\varrho}_{1,2}
fixed,
which amounts to rescale the densities around
1/2
. With these definitions, one has at leading order

\alpha=1-2\frac{\tilde{\varrho}_{1}-\tilde{\varrho}_{2}}{\nu}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\nu^{2}}\right)

(SP.322)

We first study the KPZ limit of the large deviation rate function
\Psi(u)
, then of the cumulants of the current, and finally of the MFT equations.

P.1
KPZ limit of the large deviation rate function

In the KPZ limit, we rescale the generalized Laplace parameter
u
and the partition function
z
as

u=\frac{4\tilde{u}e^{\nu^{2}T-\nu X}}{\nu^{2}},\;z=Ze^{-\nu^{2}T+\nu X}

(SP.323)

and then take the limit
\nu\to\infty
. We proceed to a change of variable in the integrand of the rate function (
5
)

y=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\tilde{y}}{2\nu},\quad\tilde{y}=\tilde{\delta}+\mathbf{i}k

(SP.324)

and one finds that at leading order the rate function takes the scaling form

\displaystyle\Psi(u)\simeq\frac{1}{\nu^{2}}\tilde{\Psi}(\tilde{u})\quad,\quad\tilde{\Psi}(\tilde{u})=-\int_{\tilde{\delta}+\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}}{2\mathbf{i}\pi}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\left(-\frac{\tilde{u}e^{\tilde{y}^{2}T+\tilde{y}X}}{(\tilde{\varrho}_{1}-\tilde{y})(\tilde{y}-\tilde{\varrho}_{2})}\right)

(SP.325)

The integration contour is now chosen so that
\tilde{\varrho}_{2}<\tilde{\delta}<\tilde{\varrho}_{1}
.
Consider for simplicity the case
X=0
,
T=1
and
\tilde{\varrho}_{1}=-\tilde{\varrho}_{2}=\tilde{w}
.
Then we can choose
\tilde{\delta}=0
and
\tilde{y}=\mathbf{i}k
. This leads to

\tilde{\Psi}(\tilde{u})=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{2\pi}\mathrm{Li}_{2}\left(-\frac{\tilde{u}e^{-k^{2}}}{k^{2}+\tilde{w}^{2}}\right)

(SP.326)

which is exactly the rate function for the KPZ equation with a double-sided Brownian initial condition
obtained in Ref.
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
. There is was obtained in another
form which is equivalent to (
SP.326
) as shown in

(
Krajenbrink et al. 2018
, Eq. (156))
(see also
Krajenbrink 2019
).

Hence we obtain that in the large
\nu
limit the r.h.s. of (
4
) takes the
form

\exp\left(-\frac{\Psi(u)}{\varepsilon}\right)\to\exp\left(-\frac{\tilde{\Psi}(\tilde{u})}{\nu^{2}\varepsilon}\right)

(SP.327)

This corresponds to a large deviation form with a speed
1/(\nu^{2}\varepsilon)
,
which we can precisely identify with the speed
1/\sqrt{T_{\rm KPZ}}

of the short-time weak noise KPZ equation, by defining the KPZ time as
T_{\rm KPZ}=\nu^{4}\varepsilon^{2}\ll 1
.

Let us examine now the limit of the l.h.s. of (
4
).
The auxiliary quantities admit the following limit

\omega=\frac{\nu\tilde{\omega}}{2}+\mathcal{O}(1),\quad\tilde{\omega}=\frac{-\tilde{\varrho}_{1}+\tilde{\varrho}_{2}+\sqrt{\left(\tilde{\varrho}_{1}-\tilde{\varrho}_{2}\right)^{2}+4\tilde{u}Z}}{2\tilde{u}Z}

(SP.328)

\displaystyle F(\omega)=\frac{2}{\nu}\tilde{F}(\tilde{\omega})+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\nu^{2}}\right),\quad\tilde{F}(\tilde{\omega})=\left(\tilde{\varrho}_{1}-\tilde{\varrho}_{2}\right)\log\frac{\tilde{\omega}\left(\tilde{\varrho}_{1}-\tilde{\varrho}_{2}\right)}{e}+\frac{1}{\tilde{\omega}}

(SP.329)

The auxiliary function
\tilde{F}(\tilde{\omega})
matches exactly the one in
(
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
, Eq. (71))
for the large deviation of stationary KPZ with the mapping
\tilde{\varrho}_{1}-\tilde{\varrho}_{2}=2\tilde{w}
and
\tilde{\omega}=e^{\chi^{\prime}}
. Note that these quantities solely depend on the difference of the rescaled densities, which corresponds to the local slope difference of the height. Upon our rescaling the observable is linearized as

\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-u\omega z)=-\frac{2\tilde{u}\tilde{\omega}Z}{\nu}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\nu^{2}}\right)

(SP.330)

so that the generalised generating function in the l.h.s. of (
4
)
becomes (since preexponential factors can be neglected)

\int_{1}^{+\infty}d\omega\,\langle e^{\frac{1}{2\nu\varepsilon}[{\rm Li}_{2}(-u\omega z(X,T))-F(\omega)]}\rangle\to\int_{0}^{+\infty}d\tilde{\omega}\,\langle e^{-\frac{1}{\nu^{2}\varepsilon}[\tilde{u}\tilde{w}Z+\tilde{F}(\tilde{\omega})]}\rangle

(SP.331)

In terms of the KPZ time
T_{\rm KPZ}=\nu^{4}\varepsilon^{2}\ll 1
we finally obtain the
large
\nu
limit of Eq. (
4
) in the form

\int_{0}^{+\infty}d\tilde{\omega}\,\bigg\langle\exp\left(-\frac{\tilde{u}\tilde{w}Z+\tilde{F}(\tilde{\omega})}{\sqrt{T_{\rm KPZ}}}\right)\bigg\rangle\sim\exp\left(-\frac{\tilde{\Psi}(\tilde{u})}{\sqrt{T_{\rm KPZ}}}\right)

(SP.332)

which is exactly the same form as obtained in
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
.
There the partition sum
Z
is such that

\log Z=H_{\rm KPZ}=[h_{\rm KPZ}(x,t)+t/12+x^{2}/(4t)]_{t=T_{\rm KPZ}}

(SP.333)

where
h_{\rm KPZ}(x,t)
satisfies the KPZ equation with unit space time white noise,
and with initial condition
h_{\rm KPZ}(x,0)=B(x)-w|x|
, where
B(x)
is a two-sided standard Brownian.
Let us recall that
\tilde{w}=wT_{\rm KPZ}^{1/2}
is the rescaled slope
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
(the same
variable as appears here). The limit
\tilde{w}\gg 1
then corresponds to the limit
of droplet initial conditions.
Here, in the
text we have defined
h=-J=\frac{H}{2\nu}
,
z=e^{H}
, hence here we have

\log Z=H+\nu^{2}T-\nu X

(SP.334)

where (
SP.333
) and (
SP.334
) allow for a precise identification
in the large
\nu
limit.

P.2
KPZ limit of the cumulants of the current

We now check the KPZ limit on the cumulants of the current.
We consider the height field
H=-2\nu J
and use the parameterization
(
SP.321
). The average of
H
reads, from (
SK.88
)

\langle H\rangle=\nu X-\nu^{2}T+\log\left(\frac{e^{\tilde{\varrho}_{1}\left(\tilde{\varrho}_{1}T+X\right)}\text{Erfc}\left(\frac{2\tilde{\varrho}_{1}T+X}{2\sqrt{T}}\right)+e^{\tilde{\varrho}_{2}\left(\tilde{\varrho}_{2}T+X\right)}\text{Erfc}\left(-\frac{2\tilde{\varrho}_{2}T+X}{2\sqrt{T}}\right)}{2}\right)

(SP.335)

which is exact and does not involve any limit. From (
SP.333
) and (
SP.334
)
we see that the first two terms are a simple shift, and that

\langle H_{\rm KPZ}\rangle
is given only by the last term in (
SP.335
).

To compare with previous results, we set from now on
\tilde{\varrho}_{1}=-\tilde{\varrho}_{2}=\tilde{w}

as well as
X=0
and
T=1
. Then (
SP.335
) reproduces exactly the result of Ref.
(
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
, Eq. (107))
, taking into account the shift identified above.
For the second cumulant,
taking the large
\nu
result of our result displayed in
(
SK.104
),
we obtain

\kappa_{2}=\frac{1}{8\tilde{w}}\left(\frac{\left(1-4\tilde{w}^{2}\right)\text{Erfc}\left(\sqrt{2}\tilde{w}\right)+2\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}e^{-2\tilde{w}^{2}}\tilde{w}}{\text{Erfc}(\tilde{w})^{2}}-1\right)+\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{\nu^{2}})

(SP.336)

This predicts that

\displaystyle\langle H^{2}\rangle_{c}=4\nu^{2}\langle J^{2}\rangle_{c}=4\nu^{2}\varepsilon\kappa_{2}=4\kappa_{2}\varepsilon_{\rm KPZ}

(SP.337)

which, thanks to the factor of
4
is exactly the result in
(
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
, Eq. (108))
(with
t^{1/2}=T_{\rm KPZ}^{1/2}=\varepsilon_{\rm KPZ}
). We have also checked the third and fourth cumulant. The calculation
uses the method of Section
K
and was performed using Mathematica.
The prediction from the limit of our result is

\langle H^{3}\rangle_{c}=-8\nu^{3}\langle J^{3}\rangle_{c}=-8\nu^{3}\kappa_{3}\varepsilon^{2}=-\frac{8}{\nu}\kappa_{3}\epsilon_{\rm KPZ}^{2}

(SP.338)

We find indeed that
-\frac{8}{\nu}\kappa_{3}
converges to the result of
(
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
, Eqs. (110), (116))
. Finally we computed

\langle H^{4}\rangle_{c}=16\nu^{4}\langle J^{4}\rangle_{c}=16\nu^{4}\kappa_{4}\varepsilon^{3}=\frac{16}{\nu^{2}}\kappa_{4}\epsilon_{\rm KPZ}^{3}

(SP.339)

and again we find perfect agreement with
(
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
, Eqs. (111) and (117))
.

P.3
KPZ limit of the MFT equations

Finally, we
obtain the KPZ limit of the MFT equations by two methods: first directly on the stochastic version of the MFT equation (as in the text) and then on the nonlinear dynamical saddle point equations.

1.

The stochastic MFT equation of the WASEP reads

\ \partial_{t}\varrho=\partial_{x}(\partial_{x}\varrho-\nu\sigma(\varrho)+\sqrt{\varepsilon\sigma(\varrho)}\eta)

(SP.340)

We first rescale the density around
1/2
, i.e., also the maximum of
\sigma(\varrho)
, as

\varrho=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\tilde{\varrho}}{2\nu}\quad,\quad\sigma(\varrho)=2\varrho(1-\varrho)=\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{\tilde{\varrho}^{2}}{\nu^{2}}\right)

(SP.341)

At large
\nu
, the dynamics of
\tilde{\varrho}
is governed by the Burgers equation

\partial_{t}\tilde{\varrho}=\partial^{2}_{x}\tilde{\varrho}+\partial_{x}\tilde{\varrho}^{2}+2\nu\partial_{x}\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}\eta

(SP.342)

Seeing the density as the slope of the height, i.e.,
\tilde{\varrho}=\partial_{x}H
, which is the same as

\varrho=\partial_{x}h=\frac{1}{2\nu}\partial_{x}H\quad,\quad h=\frac{H}{2\nu}\quad,\quad z=e^{H}

(SP.343)

(we recall
h=-J
)
it leads to the KPZ equation for
H

\partial_{t}H=\partial^{2}_{x}H+(\partial_{x}H)^{2}+2\nu\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}\eta

(SP.344)

and thus
\varepsilon_{\rm KPZ}=\nu^{2}\varepsilon
(recall that the KPZ case has noise with the following convention
\sqrt{2\varepsilon_{\rm KPZ}}\eta
). The regime
\varepsilon_{\rm KPZ}\ll 1
corresponds to the weak noise theory of KPZ, which is also its short-time regime.

2.

The MFT equations directly describe the weak noise regime of the stochastic hydrodynamic equation of the WASEP. We now show that they simply converge to the weak noise equations of KPZ. One starts from the pair

\begin{split}\partial_{t}q&=\partial_{x}\left[\partial_{x}q-2q(1-q)(\partial_{x}p+\nu)\right]\>,\\
-\partial_{t}p&=\partial_{x}^{2}p+(1-2q)\partial_{x}p(\partial_{x}p+2\nu)\>,\end{split}

(SP.345)

and we set the following change of variable

q=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\tilde{q}}{2\nu}\quad,\quad p=\frac{2\tilde{p}}{\nu}

(SP.346)

One obtains at large
\nu
to leading order

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\tilde{q}&=\partial_{x}^{2}\tilde{q}+\partial_{x}\tilde{q}^{2}-2\partial_{x}^{2}\tilde{p}\\
-\partial_{t}\tilde{p}&=\partial_{x}^{2}\tilde{p}-2\tilde{q}\partial_{x}\tilde{p}\end{split}

(SP.347)

Setting
\tilde{q}=\partial_{x}H
,
\tilde{P}=-\partial_{x}\tilde{p}
one obtains

\begin{split}\partial_{t}H&=\partial_{x}^{2}H+(\partial_{x}H)^{2}+2\tilde{P}\\
-\partial_{t}\tilde{P}&=\partial_{x}^{2}\tilde{P}-\partial_{x}(2\tilde{P}\partial_{x}H)\end{split}

(SP.348)

which are exactly the nonlinear equations associated to the weak noise theory of the KPZ equation, see Ref.
(
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2021
, Eqs. (S42)–(S43))
.

Remark P.1
.

The limit from the MFT equation to the weak noise KPZ equation
can be performed around any mean density
\bar{\varrho}
, by going to
a moving frame, and writing
\varrho(x,t)=\bar{\varrho}+\frac{\tilde{\varrho}(y,t)}{2\nu}

where
y=x-2\nu(1-2\bar{\varrho})t
. In the large
\nu
limit
the MFT equation of the WASEP becomes the following stirred Burgers equation

\partial_{t}\tilde{\varrho}=\partial^{2}_{y}\tilde{\varrho}+\partial_{y}(\tilde{\varrho}^{2})+\partial_{y}(\sqrt{8\nu^{2}\varepsilon\bar{\varrho}(1-\bar{\varrho})}\,\eta(y,t))

(SP.349)

which gives the KPZ equation (
25
) for
H
, with
\tilde{\varrho}=\partial_{y}H

and noise
\varepsilon_{\rm KPZ}=4\nu^{2}\varepsilon\bar{\varrho}(1-\bar{\varrho})
. Since
\sigma(\varrho)
is quadratic, the WASEP MFT equation is exactly a Burgers equation,
but with a noise of amplitude
\sqrt{4\nu^{2}\varepsilon\sigma(\varrho)}
,
depending non-linearly on the density field. It is only for large
\nu

and with the above scaling that the noise amplitude can be considered as constant

\sqrt{4\nu^{2}\varepsilon\sigma(\bar{\varrho})}
. Note that the same rescaling can be performed for the general MFT equation,
with arbitrary
D(\varrho)
and
\sigma(\varrho)
, with a boost
y=x-\nu\sigma^{\prime}(\bar{\varrho})t
,
diffusion coefficient
D(\bar{\varrho})
, a non linear term
\frac{1}{4}\sigma^{\prime\prime}(\bar{\varrho})\tilde{\varrho}^{2}
,
and a noise amplitude
\sqrt{4\nu^{2}\varepsilon\sigma(\bar{\varrho})}
.

Appendix Q
Limit to the SSEP
\nu\to 0

Here we show that if the asymmetry becomes zero, i.e.,
\nu\to 0
, the result of this work matches the known results for the SSEP
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009a
;
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009b
for
X=0
, as well as those for
X\neq 0

in
Imamura et al. 2017
;
Imamura et al. 2021
;
Mallick et al. 2024
. Since
z=e^{-2\nu J}
, the
\nu\to 0
limit has to be taken carefully. First of all, denoting
\omega_{u}=\omega_{u,z=1}
, we have that

\frac{1}{2\nu}[{\rm Li}_{2}(-u\omega_{uz}z)-F(\omega_{uz})]\underset{\nu\to 0}{\simeq}-\frac{1}{2\nu}\int_{0}^{u}\frac{\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}}{u^{\prime}}\log(1+u^{\prime}\omega_{u^{\prime}})+\log(1+u\omega_{u})J+\mathcal{O}(\nu)

(SQ.350)

Note that at this order we only need
\omega_{u,z=1}
since
\omega_{u,z}
is by definition the
extremum of the l.h.s. In the remainder of this section we will prove that

\Psi(u)=\frac{1}{2\nu}\int_{0}^{u}\frac{\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}}{u^{\prime}}\log(1+u^{\prime}\omega_{u^{\prime}})+\Psi_{0}(u)+\mathcal{O}(\nu)

(SQ.351)

and obtain
\Psi_{0}(u)
. Reporting both results in either sides of Eq. (
4
)
we see that the leading term
\mathcal{O}(1/\nu)
cancels
on both sides, and we are left with the following large deviation principle for the SSEP, as the limit of the one of the WASEP

{\langle}e^{\frac{\log(1+u\omega_{u})J}{\varepsilon}}{\rangle}\sim e^{-\frac{\Psi_{0}(u)}{\varepsilon}}

(SQ.352)

This large deviation result is more elegantly written using the reduced variable

P=\log(1+u\omega_{u})\Longleftrightarrow u=\left(1-e^{-P}\right)\left(e^{P}-\alpha\right)

(SQ.353)

This implies that in the SSEP limit
\nu\to 0
, the cumulant generating function is

\phi(P)|_{\nu=0}=-\Psi_{0}(u)|_{u=\left(1-e^{-P}\right)\left(e^{P}-\alpha\right)}

(SQ.354)

Let us give here our result for
\Psi_{0}(u)
, which is proved below. In the case
X=0

(setting
T=1
) we obtain

\Psi_{0}(u)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{(-\Omega)^{n}}{n^{3/2}}\quad,\quad\Omega=u\varrho_{1}(1-\varrho_{2})

(SQ.355)

Now let us recall here the result of Derrida and Gershenfeld in
(
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009a
, Eqs. (1-3))
.
In our notations it reads

\phi(P)=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{(-\tilde{\omega})^{n}}{n^{3/2}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\pi}\log(1+\tilde{\omega}e^{-k^{2}})\quad,\quad\tilde{\omega}=\varrho_{1}(1-\varrho_{2})(e^{P}-1)+\varrho_{2}(1-\varrho_{1})(e^{-P}-1)

(SQ.356)

for
\tilde{\omega}>-1
.
It is easy to
check that
\tilde{\omega}=\Omega
and that our results are identical.

For
X\neq 0
our result for
\Psi_{0}(u)
reads

\Psi_{0}(u)=\frac{2\xi\sqrt{T}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{0}^{\xi}\mathrm{d}x\,{\rm Li}_{1/2}(-\Omega e^{-x^{2}})+\sqrt{\frac{T}{\pi}}{\rm Li}_{3/2}(-\Omega e^{-\xi^{2}})+\sqrt{T}\xi\log\left(\frac{\left(1+u\omega_{u}\right)\left(1+\varrho_{1}u\omega_{u}\right)}{\left(1+\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)u\omega_{u}\right)}\right)

(SQ.357)

where here and below we use the notations
\Omega=u\varrho_{1}(1-\varrho_{2})
and
\xi=X/\sqrt{(4T)}
.
We give below an equivalent form which allows to compare with the results of Refs.
Imamura et al. 2017
;
Imamura et al. 2021
;
Mallick et al. 2024
.

In the rest of this Section, we first characterise the expansion of the derivatives of
\Psi(u)
in the first two orders in
\nu
, then obtain the leading and subleading orders of
\Psi(u)
.

Remark Q.1
.

Note that our result (
SQ.354
) for
\nu\to 0

is in agreement with the parametric representation of
\phi(P)
obtained in (
SI.63
)
(valid for any
\nu
). Indeed, we have that

\int_{0}^{u(P)}\frac{\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}}{u^{\prime}}\log(1+u^{\prime}\omega_{u^{\prime}})=P\log u(P)-\frac{P^{2}}{2}-\left({\rm Li}_{2}\left(\alpha e^{-P}\right)-{\rm Li}_{2}\left(\alpha\right)\right)-\left({\rm Li}_{2}\left(e^{-P}\right)-{\rm Li}_{2}\left(1\right)\right)

(SQ.358)

when evaluated at
u(P)=\left(1-e^{-P}\right)\left(e^{P}-\alpha\right)
. This is easily checked by taking a derivative on both sides of (
SQ.358
)
and using the identification
P=\log(1+u\omega_{u})|_{u=u(P)}
which is valid for
\nu\to 0
.

Q.1
Derivatives of
\Psi(u)

Starting from the expression of the derivatives of
\Psi(u)
(
SJ.73
), we obtain the lowest two orders as
\nu\to 0
.

\begin{split}\Psi^{(n)}(0)=&-\frac{\varrho_{1}^{n}\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)^{n}}{4\nu n}\bigg[\frac{d^{n-1}}{dy^{n-1}}\left(\frac{(y(1-y))^{n-1}e^{2\nu ny(2\nu T(y-1)+X)}}{(y-\varrho_{1})^{n}}\text{Erfc}\left(-\sqrt{\frac{n}{4T}}(2\nu T(2y-1)+X)\right)\right)|_{y=\varrho_{2}}\\
&+(-1)^{n-1}\frac{d^{n-1}}{dy^{n-1}}\left(\frac{(y(1-y))^{n-1}e^{2\nu ny(2\nu T(y-1)+X)}}{(\varrho_{2}-y)^{n}}\text{Erfc}\left(\sqrt{\frac{n}{4T}}(2\nu T(2y-1)+X)\right)\right)|_{y=\varrho_{1}}\bigg]\end{split}

(SQ.359)

In order to set
\nu\to 0
, one needs to expand the error and exponential functions as

\begin{split}&e^{2\nu ny(2\nu T(y-1)+X)}\text{Erfc}\left(\pm\sqrt{\frac{n}{4T}}(2\nu T(2y-1)+X)\right)\\
&=\text{Erfc}\left(\pm X\sqrt{\frac{n}{4T}}\right)+\left(2nXy\text{Erfc}\left(\pm X\sqrt{\frac{n}{4T}}\right)\pm\sqrt{\frac{4nT}{\pi}}(1-2y)e^{-\frac{nX^{2}}{4T}}\right)\nu+\mathcal{O}(\nu^{2})\end{split}

(SQ.360)

Using that

\begin{split}\frac{d^{n-1}}{dy^{n-1}}\left(\frac{(y(1-y))^{n-1}}{(y-\varrho_{1})^{n}}\right)|_{y=\varrho_{2}}=(-1)^{n-1}\frac{d^{n-1}}{dy^{n-1}}\left(\frac{(y(1-y))^{n-1}}{(\varrho_{2}-y)^{n}}\right)|_{y=\varrho_{1}}\end{split}

(SQ.361)

and
\text{Erfc}(x)+\text{Erfc}(-x)=2
, we find that the leading order is independent of
(X,T)
and reads

\Psi^{(n)}(0)=-\frac{\varrho_{1}^{n}\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)^{n}}{2\nu n}\frac{d^{n-1}}{dy^{n-1}}\left(\frac{(y(1-y))^{n-1}}{(y-\varrho_{1})^{n}}\right)|_{y=\varrho_{2}}+\Psi_{0}^{(n)}(0)+\mathcal{O}(\nu)

(SQ.362)

To get the next order, we use that

\begin{split}\underbrace{\frac{d^{n-1}}{dy^{n-1}}\left(\frac{y^{n}(1-y)^{n-1}}{(y-\varrho_{1})^{n}}\right)|_{y=\varrho_{2}}}_{I_{1}}=\underbrace{(-1)^{n-1}\frac{d^{n-1}}{dy^{n-1}}\left(\frac{y^{n}(1-y)^{n-1}}{(\varrho_{2}-y)^{n}}\right)|_{y=\varrho_{1}}}_{I_{2}}+(-1)^{n-1}(n-1)!\end{split}

(SQ.363)

Denoting

\begin{split}\Upsilon_{1}&=2nX\text{Erfc}\left(X\sqrt{\frac{n}{4T}}\right)-\sqrt{\frac{16nT}{\pi}}e^{-\frac{nX^{2}}{4T}}=-2n\int_{X}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}x\,\text{Erfc}\left(x\sqrt{\frac{n}{4T}}\right)\\
\Upsilon_{2}&=2nX\end{split}

(SQ.364)

we have that the next order is given by

\begin{split}\Psi_{0}^{(n)}(0)&=-\frac{\varrho_{1}^{n}\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)^{n}}{4n}\left((2\Upsilon_{2}-\Upsilon_{1})I_{1}+\Upsilon_{1}I_{2}\right)\\
&=-\frac{\varrho_{1}^{n}\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)^{n}}{4n}\left(2\Upsilon_{2}I_{1}+(-1)^{n}(n-1)!\Upsilon_{1}\right)\\
&=-\frac{\varrho_{1}^{n}\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)^{n}}{2}\left(2X\frac{d^{n-1}}{dy^{n-1}}\left(\frac{y^{n}(1-y)^{n-1}}{(y-\varrho_{1})^{n}}\right)|_{y=\varrho_{2}}+(-1)^{n-1}(n-1)!\int_{X}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}x\,\text{Erfc}\left(x\sqrt{\frac{n}{4T}}\right)\right)\end{split}

(SQ.365)

Remark Q.2
.

The identities (
SQ.361
) and (
SQ.363
) can be proved by considering the residue at infinity (zero in the first case
and
(-1)^{n}
in the second case) of the contour integral analog to (
SQ.368
) below where the logarithm is expanded as a series.

Q.2
Determination of the leading order of
\Psi(u)

As we now show, to obtain the leading order of the
\nu\to 0
limit of
\Psi(u)
, it is sufficient to set
\nu=0
inside the integrand in (
5
). To see this, first note that from (
SH.53
) we have the following relation at the saddle point

\Psi^{\prime}(u)=\frac{1}{2\nu}\frac{\log(1+u\omega_{u,z}z)}{u}\quad,\quad z=z(u)=e^{-2\nu J(u)}

(SQ.366)

As
\nu\to 0
, the right hand side of this equation can be treated as if
z=1
at leading order

u\Psi^{\prime}(u)=\frac{1}{2\nu}\log(1+u\omega_{u})+\mathcal{O}(\nu^{0})

(SQ.367)

On the other side, by setting
\nu=0
in the integrand of (
5
), we obtain that

u\Psi^{\prime}(u)=\frac{1}{2\nu}\int_{\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R}+\delta}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{2\mathbf{i}\pi y(1-y)}\log\left(1+u\frac{\varrho_{1}\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)(y-1)y}{\left(y-\varrho_{1}\right)\left(y-\varrho_{2}\right)}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\nu^{0})

(SQ.368)

We now show that these two expressions are indeed compatible. The integral (
SQ.368
) can be performed by integration by part and the boundary term vanishes at infinity.
One replaces
u=\frac{1+(\alpha-1)\omega}{\omega(\omega-1)}
where
\omega=\omega_{u}
. The integrand becomes a rational fraction of
y
which behaves as
1/y^{2}
at infinity and with the following poles

y_{i}

y=\left\{\varrho_{1},\frac{\varrho_{1}(\omega-1)}{\omega-\varrho_{1}},\varrho_{2},\frac{\varrho_{2}\omega}{\varrho_{2}+\omega-1}\right\}

(SQ.369)

and associated residues
R_{i}

\begin{split}&R=\left\{\log\left(\frac{\varrho_{1}}{1-\varrho_{1}}\right),\log\left(\frac{\left(1-\varrho_{1}\right)\omega}{\varrho_{1}(\omega-1)}\right),\log\left(\frac{\varrho_{2}}{1-\varrho_{2}}\right),\log\left(\frac{\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)(\omega-1)}{\varrho_{2}\omega}\right)\right\}\end{split}

(SQ.370)

so that up to terms of order
\mathcal{O}(\nu)
we obtain

2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)=R_{2}+R_{3}=-(R_{1}+R_{4})=\log\left(\frac{\alpha\omega}{\omega-1}\right)=\log(1+u\omega_{u})

(SQ.371)

Q.3
Determination of the subleading order of
\Psi(u)

To obtain the expression of
\Psi_{0}(u)
, we proceed to a resummation of its derivatives obtained in (
SQ.365
). We will show a perfect matching with
(
Mallick et al. 2024
, Eq. (6.35))
for any
X
.
We recall that we use
the notations
\Omega=u\varrho_{1}(1-\varrho_{2})
,
\xi=\sqrt{X/(4T)}
and
P=\log(1+u\omega_{u})
.
Note that it implies that
\Omega=\tilde{\omega}
defined in (
SQ.356
).
We first introduce the identity

\begin{split}\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{\varrho_{1}^{n}\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)^{n}u^{n}}{n!}\frac{d^{n-1}}{dy^{n-1}}\left(\frac{y^{n}(1-y)^{n-1}}{(y-\varrho_{1})^{n}}\right)|_{y=\varrho_{2}}&=-\frac{1}{2}\left(-\log(1+u\varrho_{1}(1-\varrho_{2}))+\log\frac{1+(e^{P}-1)\varrho_{1}}{1+(e^{-P}-1)\varrho_{2}}\right)\\
&=-\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{\left(1+u\omega_{u}\right)\left(1+\varrho_{1}u\omega_{u}\right)}{\left(1+\varrho_{1}\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)u\right)\left(1+\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)u\omega_{u}\right)}\right)\end{split}

(SQ.372)

Remark Q.3
.

We checked this identity with Mathematica to high orders. It is possible that it can be proven using the Lagrange inversion theorem.

Part of the resummation of the derivatives of
\Psi_{0}(u)
involve manipulating error functions as follows

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{(u\varrho_{1}(1-\varrho_{2}))^{n}}{n!}(-1)^{n-1}(n-1)!\int_{X}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}x\,\text{Erfc}\left(x\sqrt{\frac{n}{4T}}\right)

(SQ.373)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{T}\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{(-\Omega)^{n}}{n^{3/2}}\left(\frac{e^{-n\xi^{2}}}{\sqrt{\pi}}-\sqrt{n}\xi{\rm Erfc}(\sqrt{n}\xi)\right)

(SQ.374)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{T}\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{(-\Omega)^{n}}{n^{3/2}}\left(\frac{e^{-n\xi^{2}}}{\sqrt{\pi}}+\sqrt{n}\xi{\rm Erf}(\sqrt{n}\xi)\right)-\sqrt{T}\xi\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{(-\Omega)^{n}}{n}

(SQ.375)

where we recall that we introduced the notation
\xi=X/\sqrt{4T}
. Combining these intermediate identities allows to sum the derivatives of
\Psi_{0}(u)
to reconstruct the function as

\begin{split}\Psi_{0}(u)&=\sqrt{T}\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{(-\Omega)^{n}}{n}\left(\frac{e^{-n\xi^{2}}}{\sqrt{n\pi}}+\xi{\rm Erf}(\sqrt{n}\xi)\right)+\sqrt{T}\xi\log\frac{1+(e^{P}-1)\varrho_{1}}{1+(e^{-P}-1)\varrho_{2}}\\
&=\sqrt{T}\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{(-\Omega)^{n}}{n}\left(\int_{0}^{\xi}\mathrm{d}x{\rm Erf}(\sqrt{n}x)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n\pi}}\right)+\sqrt{T}\xi\log\left(\frac{\left(1+u\omega_{u}\right)\left(1+\varrho_{1}u\omega_{u}\right)}{\left(1+\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)u\omega_{u}\right)}\right)\\
&=\sqrt{T}\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{(-\Omega)^{n}}{n}\int_{0}^{\xi}\mathrm{d}x{\rm Erf}(\sqrt{n}x)+\sqrt{\frac{T}{\pi}}{\rm Li}_{3/2}(-\Omega)+\sqrt{T}\xi\log\left(\frac{\left(1+u\omega_{u}\right)\left(1+\varrho_{1}u\omega_{u}\right)}{\left(1+\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)u\omega_{u}\right)}\right)\\
\end{split}

(SQ.376)

The first line is exactly
(
Mallick et al. 2024
, Eq. (6.35))

with the conventions matched as follows

\Psi_{0}(u)\equiv-\sqrt{T}\mu(\lambda)
,
\Omega=u\varrho_{1}(1-\varrho_{2})\equiv\omega
,
P\equiv\lambda
,
\varrho_{1}\equiv\varrho_{-}
,
\varrho_{2}\equiv\varrho_{+}
and
\xi=X/\sqrt{4T}

. One can further resum the first term using the integral definition of the error function
{\rm Erf}(\sqrt{n}x)=\frac{2\sqrt{n}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{0}^{x}\mathrm{d}te^{-nt^{2}}
to obtain

\begin{split}\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{(-\Omega)^{n}}{n}\int_{0}^{\xi}\mathrm{d}x{\rm Erf}(\sqrt{n}x)&=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{0}^{\xi}\mathrm{d}x\int_{0}^{x}\mathrm{d}t\,{\rm Li}_{1/2}(-\Omega e^{-t^{2}})=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{0}^{\xi}\mathrm{d}x\,(\xi-x){\rm Li}_{1/2}(-\Omega e^{-x^{2}})\\
&=\frac{2\xi}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{0}^{\xi}\mathrm{d}x\,{\rm Li}_{1/2}(-\Omega e^{-x^{2}})+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}({\rm Li}_{3/2}(-\Omega e^{-\xi^{2}})-{\rm Li}_{3/2}(-\Omega))\end{split}

(SQ.377)

and thus we obtain our final result displayed in (
SQ.357
).

Remark Q.4
.

In the stationary limit
\varrho_{1}=\varrho_{2}
, the last term will admit an expansion in powers of
\sqrt{u}
.

Appendix R
Tail
J\to+\infty
of the distribution of the integrated current

Here we study the tail of the distribution of the integrated current for
J\to+\infty
,
i.e.,the asymptotic behavior of
\Phi(J)
for
J\to+\infty
. It is obtained from
the asymptotics of the rate function
\Psi(u)
in
the limit
u\to+\infty
. It is simple to extract, as it involves only the main branch of the large deviation function,
i.e.,the formula (
5
) for
\Psi(u)
. This tail corresponds to the
lower tail
for the height field
h=-J
.

This tail is known for the SSEP, in which case it is
cubic
\Phi(J)\propto J^{3}

Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009a
;
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009b
, as well as for the KPZ equation,
in which case it exhibits a
5/2
exponent instead, i.e.,
\Phi(J)\propto J^{5/2}

Le Doussal et al. 2016
;
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
;
Janas et al. 2016
;
Meerson et al. 2016
;
Kolokolov and Korshunov 2007
.
In that case it is called the lower tail since the KPZ height field is
H=-2\nu J
. We first compute the tail for the WASEP, i.e., for a fixed
\nu>0
, and then we will study these two limits,
respectively
\nu\to 0
and
\nu\to+\infty
. This can be done from
our general formula
for
\Phi(J)
(at arbitrary
J
and finite
\nu
) given in Section
I.1
.
Since we find that the tail for the
WASEP is cubic, and with the same amplitude as for the SSEP,
the crossover to the SSEP is simple at leading order (it may
be more delicate at subleading orders).
We will see however that in the
case of the KPZ limit, the crossover from the leading orders
J^{3}
to
J^{5/2}
is nontrivial, and we will
compute the full crossover function which describes it.

R.1
J\to+\infty
tail for the WASEP

For general
\nu>0
, let us examine the equations (
SI.59
) as
u\to+\infty
.
In that limit one has
u\Psi^{\prime}(u)\to+\infty
logarithmically in
u
and
\omega_{u}=1+\frac{\alpha}{u}+\mathcal{O}(1/u^{2})
, thus the various expansions read

\displaystyle\zeta(u)=e^{2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)}-(1+\alpha)+\alpha e^{-2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)}

(SR.378)

\displaystyle J=-u\Psi^{\prime}(u)+\frac{1}{2\nu}\log u+\frac{1+\alpha}{2\nu}e^{-2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)}(1+o(1))

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime}(J)=2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)

Note that we have neglected additional
1/u
corrections since the final expansion will be in
1/\log u
.
It turns out that the terms
e^{-2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)}
will also be of order
1/u

and will be neglected later on.

Let us now evaluate
u\Psi^{\prime}(u)
at large
u
starting from the general expression

u\Psi^{\prime}(u)=\frac{1}{2\nu}\int_{\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R}+\delta}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{2\mathbf{i}\pi y(1-y)}\log\left(1+u\frac{\varrho_{1}\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)(1-y)y}{\left(y-\varrho_{1}\right)\left(\varrho_{2}-y\right)}e^{-4\nu^{2}y(1-y)T+2\nu yX}\right)

(SR.379)

To simplify the analysis we choose
X=0
,
\varrho_{1}=\frac{1}{2}+w
,
\varrho_{2}=\frac{1}{2}-w
, where
0\leqslant w\leqslant 1/2
, and
\delta=1/2
, which leads to

\displaystyle u\Psi^{\prime}(u)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k\,}{\pi\nu\left(4k^{2}+1\right)}\log\left(1+u\frac{\left(4k^{2}+1\right)(2w+1)^{2}e^{-(4k^{2}+1)\nu^{2}T}}{16\left(k^{2}+w^{2}\right)}\right)

(SR.380)

Let us define
k_{0}>0
such that

u=\frac{16\left(k_{0}^{2}+w^{2}\right)}{\left(4k_{0}^{2}+1\right)(2w+1)^{2}e^{-(4k_{0}^{2}+1)\nu^{2}T}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=f(k_{0})

(SR.381)

which always exist for
u\geqslant f(0)
since
f(k_{0})
in an increasing function of
k_{0}^{2}
. We now study the limit
\nu
fixed and
u\to+\infty
, which implies that
k_{0}\to+\infty
since one has

k_{0}^{2}=\frac{\log(u)}{4\nu^{2}{T}}+\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{\log(4)}{\nu^{2}{T}}-1\right)+\frac{\frac{1}{4}-w^{2}}{\log(u)}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\log(u)}\right)^{2}

(SR.382)

In that limit it is convenient
to split the integral in (
SR.380
) in two parts. First, by parity we restrict the integral to
[0,\infty[
and we split the domain of integration on
[0,k_{0}]
and
[k_{0},\infty[
.

•

Let us show that the first part, i.e.,
k\in[k_{0},+\infty[
is
negligible in the limit. To this aim we set

k=k_{0}+q/(8k_{0}\nu{\sqrt{T}})
and expand in powers of
1/k_{0}

\begin{split}&2\int_{k_{0}}^{+\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}k\,}{\pi\nu\left(4k^{2}+1\right)}\log(1+\frac{f(k_{0})}{f(k)})\\
&=\frac{1}{4k_{0}\pi\nu^{2}{T}}\int_{0}^{+\infty}dq(\frac{1}{4k_{0}^{2}}-\frac{\nu^{2}+q}{16k_{0}^{4}\nu^{2}{T}}+\dots)\log(1+e^{-q}-\frac{e^{-q}q^{2}}{16k_{0}^{2}\nu^{2}{T}}+\dots)\\
&\simeq\frac{1}{16k_{0}^{3}\pi\nu^{2}{T}}\frac{\pi^{2}}{12}\end{split}

Hence this part is negligible at large
k_{0}
.

•

In the second part
k\in[0,k_{0}]
, we split the logarithm in the integrand as

\log(1+\frac{f(k_{0})}{f(k)})=\log(\frac{f(k_{0})}{f(k)})+\log(1+\frac{f(k)}{f(k_{0})})

(SR.383)

The integral associated to the second term can be bounded from above by

\begin{split}&2\int_{0}^{k_{0}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k\,}{\pi\nu}\frac{1}{4k^{2}+1}\log(1+\frac{f(k)}{f(k_{0})})<2\int_{0}^{k_{0}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k\,}{\pi\nu}\log(1+\frac{f(k)}{f(k_{0})})\\
&\simeq\frac{2}{8\pi k_{0}\nu^{2}{T}}\int_{0}^{8k_{0}^{2}\nu{\sqrt{T}}}dq\log(1+e^{-q}+\frac{e^{-q}q^{2}}{16k_{0}^{2}\nu^{2}{T}}+\dots)\\
&\simeq\frac{2}{8\pi k_{0}\nu^{2}{T}}\frac{\pi^{2}}{12}\end{split}

(SR.384)

where we have set
k=k_{0}-q/(8k_{0}\nu{\sqrt{T}})
. It is of order
\mathcal{O}(1/k_{0})

and we will neglect it.

•

Hence we only need
to study the first term in (
SR.383
). In summary we
obtain

\begin{split}&u\Psi^{\prime}(u)=2\int_{0}^{k_{0}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k\,}{\pi\nu\left(4k^{2}+1\right)}(\log(f(k_{0}))-\log(f(k)))+\mathcal{O}(1/k_{0})\\
&=\int_{0}^{k_{0}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k\,}{\pi\nu}\arctan(2k)\frac{f^{\prime}(k)}{f(k)}+\mathcal{O}(1/k_{0})\\
\end{split}

(SR.385)

It is easy to obtain the expansion of this integral at large
k_{0}
.

This leads to

u\Psi^{\prime}(u)=2k_{0}^{2}\nu T-\frac{4k_{0}\nu T}{\pi}+\frac{\nu T}{2}+\frac{1}{\nu}B_{w}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{k_{0}}\right)

(SR.386)

where

B_{w}=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}k\,\frac{2k\left(1-4w^{2}\right)\arctan(2k)}{\pi\left(4k^{2}+1\right)\left(k^{2}+w^{2}\right)}\quad,\quad B_{1/2}=0\quad,\quad B_{0}=\log 2

(SR.387)

Inserting (
SR.382
) one finds

\displaystyle u\Psi^{\prime}(u)=\frac{\log u}{2\nu}-\frac{2{\sqrt{T}}}{\pi}\sqrt{\log u}+b+\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{\log u})\quad,\quad b=\frac{1}{\nu}(B_{w}+\log 2)

(SR.388)

Let us now use (
SR.378
), up to terms of order
1/u

\displaystyle J\simeq-u\Psi^{\prime}(u)+\frac{\log u}{2\nu}

(SR.389)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime}(J)=2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)

(SR.390)

Hence we see that there is a cancellation of the leading term
in
J
resulting in

\displaystyle J=\frac{2{\sqrt{T}}}{\pi}\sqrt{\log u}-b+\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{\log u})

(SR.391)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime}(J)=\log u-\frac{4\nu{\sqrt{T}}}{\pi}\sqrt{\log u}+2\nu b+\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{\log u})

(SR.392)

Hence we find for
J\to+\infty

\Phi(J)=\frac{\pi^{2}J^{3}}{12{T}}+J^{2}\left(\frac{\pi^{2}b}{4{T}}-\nu\right)+\mathcal{O}(J)

(SR.393)

R.2
J\to+\infty
tail in the SSEP limit

To obtain the tail of the current distribution in the SSEP limit, we first proceed to the limit
\nu\to 0
and then take
u\to+\infty
. We show in (
SQ.351
) that the rate function
\Psi(u)
admits the following expansion

u\Psi^{\prime}(u)=\frac{\log(1+u\omega_{u})}{2\nu}+u\Psi_{0}^{\prime}(u)+\mathcal{O}(\nu)

(SR.394)

where
\Psi_{0}(u)
is given explicitly in (
SQ.357
). Consider now the equations (
SI.59
)
and perform the expansion at small
\nu
and fixed
u
. One first obtains the expansion of
\zeta(u)

\zeta(u)=u+2\nu\sqrt{\left(\alpha+u-1\right)^{2}+4u}\,u\Psi_{0}^{\prime}(u)+\mathcal{O}(\nu^{2})

(SR.395)

where we used (
SH.52
).
leading to the expansion of the parametric representation of the current rate function

\begin{split}J&=-\sqrt{\left(\alpha+u-1\right)^{2}+4u}\,\Psi_{0}^{\prime}(u)+\mathcal{O}(\nu)\\
\Phi^{\prime}(J)&=\log(1+u\omega_{u})+\mathcal{O}(\nu)=\log\left(\frac{1+u+\alpha+\sqrt{\left(\alpha+u-1\right)^{2}+4u}}{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\nu)\end{split}

(SR.396)

In the limit of large Laplace parameter
u\to+\infty
, we use the asymptotics of the polylogarithm appearing in (
SQ.357
), namely, for
s
a non-negative integers, one has to leading order

\mathrm{Li}_{s}(-e^{\mu})\underset{\mu\to+\infty}{\simeq}-\frac{\mu^{s}}{\Gamma(s+1)}

(SR.397)

From it,
we obtain the leading asymptotics

u\Psi_{0}^{\prime}(u)=-\frac{2\sqrt{T}}{\pi}\sqrt{\log u}+\mathcal{O}(1)

(SR.398)

independently of
\xi=X/\sqrt{4T}
. This leads to
to the parametric representation for large
J\to+\infty

\begin{split}J&\simeq\frac{2\sqrt{T}}{\pi}\sqrt{\log u}\\
\Phi^{\prime}(J)&\simeq\log u\simeq\left(\frac{\pi J}{2\sqrt{T}}\right)^{2}\end{split}

(SR.399)

We thus obtain the right tail of the current distribution as

\Phi(J)\underset{J\gg 1}{\simeq}\frac{\pi^{2}J^{3}}{12T}

(SR.400)

which is consistent with the result of
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009a
;
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009b
.
Note that it holds for any
X,T
, i.e., the
leading term is independent of
\xi=X/\sqrt{4T}

which only appears in subdominant contributions.

Remark R.1
.

For the SSEP the result
(
Derrida and Gerschenfeld 2009a
, Eq. (5))

\Phi(J)=\frac{\pi^{2}}{12}J^{3}-J\log(\varrho_{1}(1-\varrho_{2}))+\mathcal{O}(1)

(SR.401)

Comparing with our result for the WASEP (
SR.393
), this suggests that there will
be a crossover from WASEP to SSEP in the subdominant terms
\mathcal{O}(J,J^{2})

of the tail.

R.3
J\to+\infty
tail in the KPZ limit

To obtain the tail of the current distribution in the KPZ limit, we first proceed to the limit
\nu\to\infty
and only later take the rescaled Laplace parameter
\tilde{u}\to+\infty
. We use the results and notations of Section

P.1
.
In the limit
\nu\to\infty
at fixed
\tilde{u}
the large deviation function and the auxiliary variables admit the following expansion

\displaystyle\Psi(u)=\frac{1}{\nu^{2}}\tilde{\Psi}(\tilde{u})+\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{\nu^{3}})\quad,\quad u=\frac{4\tilde{u}e^{\nu^{2}T-\nu X}}{\nu^{2}}\quad,\quad\alpha=1-4\frac{\tilde{w}}{\nu}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\nu^{2}}\right),\quad\tilde{w}=\frac{\tilde{\varrho}_{1}-\tilde{\varrho}_{2}}{2}

(SR.402)

where
\tilde{\Psi}(\tilde{u})
is given in
(
SP.325
), and in (
SP.326
) for
X=0
and
T=1
.
Consider again the equations (
SI.59
)
and perform the expansion (
SR.402
)
at small
\nu
and fixed
\tilde{u}
. One first finds that

u\Psi^{\prime}(u)=\frac{1}{\nu^{2}}\tilde{u}\tilde{\Psi}^{\prime}(\tilde{u})

and, being careful that one must also expand
\alpha
near unity,
one obtains the following expansion of
\zeta(u)

\zeta(u)=\frac{4}{\nu^{2}}\tilde{u}\tilde{\Psi}^{\prime}(\tilde{u})(\tilde{u}\tilde{\Psi}^{\prime}(\tilde{u})+2\tilde{w})+\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{\nu^{3}})

(SR.403)

Additionally
we define the reduced variables

\zeta(u)=\frac{4}{\nu^{2}}\tilde{\zeta}(\tilde{u}),\quad\omega_{\zeta(u)}=\frac{\nu\tilde{\omega}_{\tilde{\zeta}(\tilde{u})}}{2}+\mathcal{O}(1),\quad\tilde{\omega}_{\tilde{\zeta}(\tilde{u})}=\frac{\sqrt{\tilde{w}^{2}+\tilde{\zeta}(\tilde{u})}-\tilde{w}}{\tilde{\zeta}(\tilde{u})}

(SR.404)

This leads to the expansion of the parametric representation of the current rate function as

\begin{split}J&=-\frac{1}{2\nu}\log\left(\tilde{\Psi}^{\prime}(\tilde{u})(\tilde{u}\tilde{\Psi}^{\prime}(\tilde{u})+2\tilde{w})\right)+\frac{\nu T}{2}-\frac{X}{2}+\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{\nu^{2}})\\
\Phi^{\prime}(J)&=\frac{2}{\nu}\tilde{u}\tilde{\Psi}^{\prime}(\tilde{u})+\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{\nu^{2}})\\
\end{split}

(SR.405)

From (
SP.325
) in the simplest case
\tilde{\varrho}_{2}=-\tilde{\varrho}_{1}
one finds

\tilde{u}\tilde{\Psi}^{\prime}(\tilde{u})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{2\pi}\log\left(1+\frac{\tilde{u}e^{-k^{2}T+\mathbf{i}kX}}{k^{2}+\tilde{w}^{2}}\right)

(SR.406)

The large
\tilde{u}
asymptotics was performed in
(
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
, Eq. (85))

carefully and including subdominant terms. Here we
show the simplest way to obtain the leading asymptotics for
\tilde{u}\to+\infty
.
For this, we rescale the integration variable

k=\frac{\sqrt{\log\tilde{u}}}{\sqrt{T}}\,p

(SR.407)

so that to leading order one has
\tilde{u}e^{-k^{2}T+\mathbf{i}kX}\simeq e^{\log\tilde{u}(1-p^{2})}
.
Replacing
\log(1+e^{Y})\simeq\max(0,Y)
at large
Y
, one finds the asymptotics

\tilde{u}\tilde{\Psi}^{\prime}(\tilde{u})\simeq\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{T}}(\log\tilde{u})^{3/2}\int_{-1}^{+1}dp(1-p^{2})=\frac{2}{3\pi\sqrt{T}}(\log\tilde{u})^{3/2}

(SR.408)

at fixed
X
and
\tilde{w}
(the dependence in these parameters
being subdominant in that limit).
Since the KPZ height is related to the WASEP current as
H_{\rm KPZ}=-2\nu J+\nu^{2}T-\nu X
from (
SR.405
) and (
SR.408
)
we thus obtain the right tail of the distribution of the current

\Phi(J)\underset{J\gg 1}{\simeq}\frac{16\sqrt{2\nu}}{15\pi\sqrt{T}}(J-\frac{\nu T}{2}+\frac{X}{2})_{+}^{5/2}\sim\frac{1}{\nu^{2}\sqrt{T}}\frac{4}{15\pi}|H_{\rm KPZ}|^{5/2}

(SR.409)

which is in agreement with the known results
Le Doussal et al. 2016
;
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
;
Janas et al. 2016
;
Meerson et al. 2016
;
Kolokolov and Korshunov 2007
.

R.4
Crossover between the
J^{3}
tail of the WASEP and the
J^{5/2}
tail of KPZ

For a large
\nu
, it seems natural to expect a crossover between the exponents
3
and
5/2
in the right tail of the current distribution.
The crossover between the two tails occurs when
J-\nu T/2\sim\nu T
and we thus define a reduced variable
\tilde{J}=(J-\nu T/2)/(2\nu T)
and show that the crossover large deviation tail has the scaling form

P(J,\nu,T)\underset{\varepsilon\ll 1}{\sim}\exp\left(-\frac{T^{2}(2\nu)^{3}}{\varepsilon}\Phi_{+}\left(\frac{J-\nu T/2}{2\nu T}\right)\right),\quad J-\nu T/2,\nu\gg 1,\quad\tilde{J}=\frac{J-\nu T/2}{2\nu T}=\mathcal{O}(1)

(SR.410)

where
\Phi_{+}
is the crossover function which interpolates between the regimes

\Phi_{+}(\tilde{J})=\begin{cases}\frac{\pi^{2}}{12}\tilde{J}^{3},\quad&\tilde{J}\gg 1\\
\frac{16}{15\pi}\tilde{J}^{5/2},\quad&\tilde{J}\ll 1\\
\end{cases}

(SR.411)

The purpose of this section is to obtain the parametric representation of the crossover function
\Phi_{+}
. Let us start again from the formula (
SR.380
) for
u\Psi^{\prime}(u)
.

\displaystyle u\Psi^{\prime}(u)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k\,}{\pi\nu\left(4k^{2}+1\right)}\log\left(1+u\frac{\left(4k^{2}+1\right)(2w+1)^{2}e^{-(4k^{2}+1)\nu^{2}T}}{16\left(k^{2}+w^{2}\right)}\right)

(SR.412)

We now consider the double limit where both
u\to+\infty
and
\nu\to+\infty

with
u\sim e^{\nu^{2}T(1+4k_{0}^{2})}
with
k_{0}
fixed, i.e., with

\frac{\log u}{\nu^{2}T}=1+4k_{0}^{2}=\mathcal{O}(1)

(SR.413)

A crossover will occur as
k_{0}
increases from
k_{0}\to 0
(KPZ) and
k_{0}\to+\infty
(WASEP). Neglecting the pre-exponential factors in the argument of the logarithm in (
SR.412
),
it is easy to see that the leading estimate at large
\nu
is

\displaystyle u\Psi^{\prime}(u)\simeq\nu T\int_{-k_{0}}^{k_{0}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k\,}{\pi}\frac{4\left(k_{0}^{2}-k^{2}\right)}{4k^{2}+1}=\frac{\nu T}{\pi}\left((4k_{0}^{2}+1)\arctan(2k_{0})-2k_{0}\right)

(SR.414)

We can now use again the large
u
asymptotics of the parametric representation in (
SR.378
)
where the terms
e^{-2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)}
can be safely neglected, leading to

\displaystyle J\simeq-u\Psi^{\prime}(u)+\frac{\log u}{2\nu}

(SR.415)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime}(J)=2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)

(SR.416)

From (
SR.414
) and (
SR.413
) we see that the first two terms in
J
will be proportional to

\nu
at large
\nu
. Hence we introduce the scaling variable

\tilde{J}=\frac{J}{2\nu T}-\frac{1}{4}

(SR.417)

which will remain of order
\mathcal{O}(1)
in the crossover region (the factor
-1/4
was
introduced for later convenience).
One obtains from (
SR.415
) together with (
SR.414
) and (
SR.413
), that in the double limit

\tilde{J}=\frac{k_{0}}{\pi}+k_{0}^{2}-(k_{0}^{2}+\frac{1}{4})\frac{2}{\pi}\arctan(2k_{0})

(SR.418)

which is a strictly increasing function of
k_{0}
with
\tilde{J}\sim k_{0}^{2}
for
k_{0}\ll 1

and
\tilde{J}\sim 2k_{0}/\pi
for
k_{0}\gg 1
.
The second equation in (
SR.415
) then
shows that in the crossover region
\Phi(J)
will
take the scaling form

\Phi(J)\simeq T^{2}(2\nu)^{3}\Phi_{+}(\tilde{J})

(SR.419)

and one has

\Phi^{\prime}_{+}(\tilde{J})=\frac{u\Psi^{\prime}(u)}{2\nu T}=\frac{\left(4k_{0}^{2}+1\right)\arctan(2k_{0})-2k_{0}}{2\pi}

(SR.420)

In summary the tail of the large deviation form of the PDF of the integrated current
J
takes for
large
\nu
and large
J
the crossover scaling form of Eq. (
SR.410
)
where the function
\Phi_{+}(\tilde{J})
is determined by the
parametric system (where
k_{0}\in[0,+\infty[
should be eliminated)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime}_{+}(\tilde{J})=\frac{\left(4k_{0}^{2}+1\right)\arctan(2k_{0})-2k_{0}}{2\pi}

(SR.421)

\displaystyle\tilde{J}=\frac{k_{0}}{\pi}+k_{0}^{2}-(k_{0}^{2}+\frac{1}{4})\frac{2}{\pi}\arctan(2k_{0})

(SR.422)

From this representation it is easy to obtain the expansion of
\Phi_{+}(\tilde{J})
for small
\tilde{J}

(corresponding to the KPZ limit, and small
k_{0}
) and for large
\tilde{J}

(corresponding to the WASSEP limit, and large
k_{0}
). One find

•

For
\tilde{J}\ll 1
, we have

\Phi_{+}(\tilde{J})=\frac{16\tilde{J}^{5/2}}{15\pi}+\frac{32\tilde{J}^{3}}{9\pi^{2}}-\frac{64\left(3\pi^{2}-70\right)\tilde{J}^{7/2}}{315\pi^{3}}-\frac{256\left(9\pi^{2}-100\right)\tilde{J}^{4}}{405\pi^{4}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\tilde{J}^{9/2}\right)

(SR.423)

•

For
\tilde{J}\gg 1
we have

\Phi_{+}(\tilde{J})=\frac{\pi^{2}}{12}\tilde{J}^{3}+\frac{1}{16}\left(\pi^{2}-8\right)\tilde{J}^{2}+\left(\frac{\pi^{2}}{64}-\frac{1}{6}\right)\tilde{J}+\frac{1}{768}\left(\pi^{2}-8\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\tilde{J}}\right)

(SR.424)

For completeness we also give the parametric representation of
\Phi_{+}(\tilde{J})

\displaystyle\Phi_{+}(\tilde{J})=\int_{0}^{k_{0}}\mathrm{d}k_{0}\tilde{J}^{\prime}(k_{0})\Phi^{\prime}_{+}(\tilde{J})

(SR.425)

\displaystyle=\frac{3\left(4k_{0}^{2}+1\right)\arctan(2k_{0})\left(-2\left(4k_{0}^{2}+1\right)\arctan(2k_{0})+4k_{0}(\pi k_{0}+2)+\pi\right)-2k_{0}(4k_{0}(5\pi k_{0}+3)+3\pi)}{48\pi^{2}}

Appendix S
Domain of definition of
\Psi(u)
, analytic continuation, solitons and branches

For simplicity we restrict in this section to the case
X=0
and
\varrho_{1}+\varrho_{2}=1
.

S.1
Extension of the domain of definition of
\Psi(u)

The range of definition of
\Psi(u)
covers naturally
u\in[0,\infty[
. We show in this Section that this range can be extended to
u\in[u_{c},\infty[
, with
u_{c}<0
, and additionally that
\Psi(u)

possesses several branches. The main branch is given by (
5
).
The secondary branches can be determined by an analytical continuation procedure,
and are required to obtain the tail
J\to-\infty
of the current distribution.
Such a procedure is quite standard in the computation of the large deviations of weak noise theories, see Refs.
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2023
;
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2021
;
Tsai 2023
;
Krajenbrink 2019
;
Bettelheim et al. 2022b
. In order to simplify the analysis, we restrict to
X=0
,
\varrho_{1}=\frac{1}{2}+w
,
\varrho_{2}=\frac{1}{2}-w
, where
0\leqslant w\leqslant 1/2
, and
\delta=1/2
and start from the expression valid for the main branch, from
(
5
)

\begin{split}u\Psi^{\prime}(u)&=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{dk}{\pi\nu\left(4k^{2}+1\right)}\log\left(1+u\frac{\left(4k^{2}+1\right)(2w+1)^{2}e^{-(4k^{2}+1)\nu^{2}T}}{16\left(k^{2}+w^{2}\right)}\right)\\
\end{split}

(SS.426)

Since
w\leqslant 1/2
, the function appearing inside the logarithm

h(k)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\quad k\mapsto\frac{\left(4k^{2}+1\right)(2w+1)^{2}e^{-(4k^{2}+1)\nu^{2}T}}{16\left(k^{2}+w^{2}\right)}

(SS.427)

is decreasing for
k\geqslant 0
, increasing for
k\leqslant 0
and reaches its maximum at
k=0
for the value

h(k=0)=\frac{(2w+1)^{2}e^{-\nu^{2}T}}{16w^{2}}

(SS.428)

Hence, for
u\Psi^{\prime}(u)
and by extension
\Psi(u)
to be properly defined, we need notably
\log(1+uh(0))
to be well defined and thus
u\geqslant-1/h(0)
which provides a lower bound as a necessary condition for the definition of
u\Psi^{\prime}(u)

u\geqslant u_{c}=-\frac{16w^{2}}{(2w+1)^{2}e^{-\nu^{2}T}}=-(\sqrt{\alpha}-1)^{2}e^{\nu^{2}T}

(SS.429)

where
\alpha=\frac{\varrho_{2}(1-\varrho_{1})}{\varrho_{1}(1-\varrho_{2})}=(1-2w)^{2}/(1+2w)^{2}
.
Upon reaching
u=u_{c}
, one can obtain the corresponding
P=P_{c}
and
J=J_{c}
from
(
SI.59
) as

\begin{split}J_{c}&=-\frac{1}{2\nu}\log\left(\frac{(1-e^{-2\nu u_{c}\Psi^{\prime}(u_{c})})(e^{2\nu u_{c}\Psi^{\prime}(u_{c})}-\alpha)}{u_{c}}\right)\\
P_{c}&=2\nu u_{c}\Psi^{\prime}(u_{c})\end{split}

(SS.430)

where
u_{c}\Psi^{\prime}(u_{c})
is given by the above integral (
SS.426
) which is well defined at
u=u_{c}
.
The parametric equations (
SI.59
) with
u\in[u_{c},\infty[
and

u\Psi^{\prime}(u)
given by (
SS.426
)
allow to compute the large deviation function
\Phi(J)
for
J\in[J_{c},\infty[
.
We now address how to compute
\Phi(J)
for
J<J_{c}
. Note that (since
u_{c}<0
) one
has
J_{c}<\bar{J}

where
\bar{J}
is the mean integrated current computed in Section
K
(which corresponds
to
u=0
).

As in Refs.
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2023
;
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2021
;
Bettelheim et al. 2022b
;
Krajenbrink 2019
;
Tsai 2023
, one needs to determine the other branches of
\Psi(u)
. This allows to extend the range of the solution for
\Phi(J)
to
J\in[J_{c},\infty[
. The interpretation within the scattering approach to the equations of the MFT,
is that solitons are spontaneously generated in the regime
J\leqslant J_{c}
, which then provide an additional contribution to the large deviation function. The solitonic structure is unveiled by finding the locations of the zeros of the argument of the logarithm in
u\Psi^{\prime}(u)
in the complex plane, and their positions will define the rapidities of the solitons. We present here a simple derivation
leaving a more refined one using inverse scattering methods (which are in principle
possible from the integrable Lax structure that we have provided in the main text)
to a future work.

S.2
Location of solitons

To study potential solitons, we need to solve for
k\in\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R}
the equation
1+uh(k)=0
, equivalent to

\frac{u}{u_{c}}=\frac{\left(w^{2}-\kappa^{2}\right)e^{-4\kappa^{2}\nu^{2}T}}{\left(1-4\kappa^{2}\right)w^{2}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=g(\kappa)

(SS.431)

Note that since we restrict here to
X=0
, we only need to consider
k\in\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R}
, for
X\neq 0
, the solitons might have a more complicated structure, akin to the one observed in
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2023
;
Bettelheim et al. 2022b
. Its Jacobian reads

\frac{\mathrm{d}u}{u}=\frac{g^{\prime}(\kappa)}{g(\kappa)}\mathrm{d}\kappa=(-8T\nu^{2}\kappa+\frac{\kappa\left(2-8w^{2}\right)}{\left(4\kappa^{2}-1\right)\left(w^{2}-\kappa^{2}\right)})\mathrm{d}\kappa

(SS.432)

Let us study the structure of the solutions, see Fig.
SS.2
,

•

For
u/u_{c}>0
there are two pairs solutions
k=\pm\mathbf{i}\kappa_{0}
and
k=\pm\mathbf{i}\kappa_{-1}
with
\kappa_{0}<w
and
\kappa_{-1}>1/2
. Only
\kappa_{0}
will be of use here at we need the solution
\kappa(u)
to vanish for
u=u_{c}
.

•

For
u/u_{c}<0
there are one or three solutions
k=\pm\mathbf{i}\kappa_{0,1,2}
(with
w<\kappa_{0,1,2}<1/2
). The number of solutions is controlled by the existence of a critical point defined as a real root of

g^{\prime}(\kappa)=0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad 16T\nu^{2}\kappa^{4}-4T\nu^{2}(1+4w^{2})\kappa^{2}+1+4w^{2}(T\nu^{2}-1)=0\quad\text{or}\quad\kappa=0

(SS.433)

Note that by definition
w^{2}\leqslant 1/4
in our model.
For
T\nu^{2}\geqslant 4/(1-4w^{2})
, equivalently if one fixes

\nu^{2}T>4
, for
w\in[0,w_{c}=\sqrt{\frac{T\nu^{2}-4}{4T\nu^{2}}}]
,
there are two pairs of critical points (besides the trivial root
\kappa=0
)

\begin{split}\kappa_{c1}^{2}&=\frac{1}{8}\left(4w^{2}+1-\frac{\sqrt{\nu^{2}T\left(4w^{2}-1\right)\left(\nu^{2}T\left(4w^{2}-1\right)+4\right)}}{\nu^{2}T}\right)\\
\kappa_{c2}^{2}&=\frac{1}{8}\left(4w^{2}+1+\frac{\sqrt{\nu^{2}T\left(4w^{2}-1\right)\left(\nu^{2}T\left(4w^{2}-1\right)+4\right)}}{\nu^{2}T}\right)\\
\end{split}

(SS.434)

while for
T\nu^{2}<4/(1-4w^{2})
there are no such critical points.
If there are no critical points then there is only one pair of solutions
k=\pm\mathbf{i}\kappa_{0}
for
u/u_{c}<0
,
see Figure
SS.2
panels a) and b).
If there are critical points then there exists an interval such that for

u\in[u_{c2},u_{c1}]
there are three pairs of solutions (with
u_{c1}/u_{c}<0
,
u_{c2}/u_{c}<0
),
see Figure
SS.2
panel c). We further associate
u_{c1}\leftrightarrow\kappa_{c1}
and
u_{c2}\leftrightarrow\kappa_{c2}
so that the function
\kappa(u)
is tri-valued in the interval
[u_{c2},u_{c1}]
, see Fig.
SS.2
.

Figure SS.2:
Numerical solution of the soliton rapidity equation (
SS.431
) in different regimes.
(a) top

T=1
,
w=0.2
,
\nu=1
where the physical branch of
\kappa(u)
(i.e., the one departing to the right from
\kappa(u_{c})=0
) is single-valued,
(b) bottom-left

T=1
,
w=1.3w_{c}
,
\nu=2.3
where the physical branch is single-valued,
(c) bottom-right

T=1
,
w=0.5w_{c}
,
\nu=2.3
above the transition where the physical branch is tri-valued between
u_{c1}
and
u_{c2}
.
We will not study this case in detail in this paper.

S.3
Analytical continuation of
u\Psi^{\prime}(u)

Knowing the solitonic structure, we can now obtain the analytical continuation of
u\Psi^{\prime}(u)
using the same derivation as in Ref.
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
(see also
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2022
for subsequent work). We start by proceeding to an integration by part on the integral definition of
u\Psi^{\prime}(u)
as

\begin{split}u\Psi^{\prime}(u)&=2\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{dk}{\pi\nu\left(4k^{2}+1\right)}\log\left(1+uh(k)\right)\\
&=-\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\pi\nu}\arctan(2k)\frac{uh^{\prime}(k)}{uh(k)+1}\end{split}

(SS.435)

where we have defined
h(k)
in (
SS.427
). We apply a further change of variable
b=\frac{1}{h(k)}
so that

\begin{split}u\Psi^{\prime}(u)&=\int_{1/h(0)=-u_{c}}^{1/h(+\infty)=+\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}b}{\nu\pi}\arctan(2k(b))\frac{u}{b}\frac{1}{u+b}\end{split}

(SS.436)

Note that
u_{c}
is a branching point for
u\Psi^{\prime}(u)
, around which one can turn around in the complex plane at the expense of changing the Riemann sheet on which the function is defined
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
. This amounts to add a jump contribution
u\Psi^{\prime}(u)\to u\Psi^{\prime}(u)+u\Delta^{\prime}(u)
where the jump reads

u\Delta^{\prime}(u)=-\frac{2}{\nu}\mathrm{arctanh}(2\kappa(u))

(SS.437)

where
\kappa(u)=g^{-1}(u/u_{c})
where
g(\kappa)
is defined in (
SS.431
). Note that this requires
\kappa<1/2
. A number of subtleties arise at this stage

•

If there are multiple solutions
\kappa_{0,1,2}
to the equation
\frac{u}{u_{c}}=g(\kappa)
, see the previous subsection, then there are multiple possible choices for the value of
\kappa(u)
in the jump function
\Delta(u)
or its derivative
u\Delta^{\prime}(u)
in (
SS.437
). In the analysis of the weak noise theory of the KPZ equation with Brownian initial condition in
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
, similar multiple choices have led to the existence of a phase transition, which in this context is a non-analyticity of the large deviation function
\Phi(J)
, see
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2017
;
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2022
;
Krajenbrink and Le Doussal 2023
and
Janas et al. 2016
;
Bettelheim et al. 2022b
. This subtlety is more easily understood in the context of the inverse scattering methods and thus we leave the analysis of the case of multiple solutions to a future work.

•

In the present paper we focus on the case where
\kappa(u)
is single-valued, i.e., on the case
(see previous subsection)

T\nu^{2}<4,\quad\mathrm{or},\quad T\nu^{2}\geqslant 4,1/2\geqslant w\geqslant w_{c}=\sqrt{\frac{T\nu^{2}-4}{4T\nu^{2}}}

(SS.438)

The function
\kappa(u)
is given by the root
\kappa_{0}
(which belongs to the physical branch, see Fig.
SS.2
) where
0\leqslant\kappa_{0}\leqslant w
for
u/u_{c}\geqslant 0
and
w\leqslant\kappa_{0}\leqslant 1/2
for
u/u_{c}\leqslant 0
(with
\kappa(u_{c})=0
and
\kappa(0)=w
). In that case one can further integrate this relation to obtain
\Delta(u)
, which is the continuation of
\Psi(u)
as

\begin{split}\Delta(u)&=-\frac{2}{\nu}\int_{u_{c}}^{u}\frac{\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}}{u^{\prime}}\mathrm{arctanh}(2\kappa(u^{\prime}))\\
&=\frac{2}{\nu}\int_{0}^{\kappa(u)}\mathrm{d}\kappa(8T\nu^{2}\kappa+\frac{2\kappa}{(w-\kappa)(\kappa+w)}+\frac{8\kappa}{(2\kappa-1)(2\kappa+1)})\mathrm{arctanh}(2\kappa)\end{split}

(SS.439)

where we have used from the first line to the second line the expression of the Jacobian (
SS.432
). For general
w<1/2
Eq. (
SS.439
) provides a parametric representation of
\Delta(u)
, where
u\in[u_{c},\infty[
(equivalently
\kappa\in[0,1/2[
).
The integral in (
SS.439
) can be computed explicitly but leads to a complicated expression
for general
w
.

For the step initial condition, i.e.,
w=1/2
, the expression of the jump simplifies and reads

\Delta(u)=2\nu T\left(\left(4\kappa^{2}-1\right)\mathrm{arctanh}(2\kappa)+2\kappa\right),\quad\kappa=\kappa(u)=\sqrt{\frac{\log(u_{c}/u)}{4\nu^{2}T}},\quad u_{c}=-e^{\nu^{2}T}

(SS.440)

where
u
now varies in
[u_{c},-1]
.

S.4
Continuation of the parametric representation of
\Phi(J)
for
J<J_{c}

Let us recall that the main branch (
SI.59
) corresponds to
u\in[u_{c},+\infty[
and
J>J_{c}
.
Let us now give our result for the parametric representation of
\Phi(J)
for
J<J_{c}
,
where again
u\in[u_{c},+\infty[

\begin{split}\Phi^{\prime}(J)&=2\nu u(\Psi^{\prime}(u)+\Delta^{\prime}(u))\\
J&=-\frac{1}{2\nu}\log\frac{\zeta(u)}{u}\\
\zeta(u)&\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=e^{2\nu u(\Psi^{\prime}(u)+\Delta^{\prime}(u))}-(1+\alpha)+\alpha e^{-2\nu u(\Psi^{\prime}(u)+\Delta^{\prime}(u))}\\
&=\left(\frac{1-2\kappa(u)}{1+2\kappa(u)}\right)^{2}e^{2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)}-(1+\alpha)+\alpha\left(\frac{1+2\kappa(u)}{1-2\kappa(u)}\right)^{2}e^{-2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)}\\
\end{split}

(SS.441)

where
\Delta(u)
and
\kappa(u)
where defined in the previous subsection.
In the last line we have used (
SS.437
).
We recall that (
SS.441
) is valid for
X=0
,
\varrho_{1}+\varrho_{2}=1
and
in the parameter range defined in (
SS.438
). In addition we
also assumed
\alpha>0
, i.e., we excluded the step initial condition
which we now address.

Remark S.1
.

Case of step initial condition: in that case
the integrated current at
X=0
,
J(0,T)
, is
the total number of particles which are on the right of zero at time
T
and
must thus be positive. Hence the large deviation rate function
\Phi(J)
is
only defined for
J>0
. It is obtained parametrically from

\begin{split}\Phi^{\prime}(J)&=2\nu(u\Psi^{\prime}(u)+u\Delta^{\prime}(u))\\
J&=-\frac{1}{2\nu}\log\frac{(e^{2\nu(u\Psi^{\prime}(u)+u\Delta^{\prime}(u))}-1)}{u}=-\frac{1}{2\nu}\log\left(\frac{\left(\frac{1-2\kappa(u)}{1+2\kappa(u)}\right)^{2}e^{2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)}-1}{u}\right)\end{split}

(SS.442)

where for
J>J_{c}
,
u\Delta^{\prime}(u)=0
(and one can set
\kappa(u)\equiv 0
) and
u

varies from
u=+\infty
(i.e.,
J=+\infty
)
to
u=u_{c}=-e^{\nu^{2}T}
(i.e.,
J=J_{c}
),
while for
0<J<J_{c}
one has

u\Delta^{\prime}(u)
given by (
SS.437
), and
u\in[u_{c},-1]

(where
u=-1
corresponds to
J=0
). In addition one has

\begin{split}u\Psi^{\prime}(u)&=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k\,}{\pi\nu\left(4k^{2}+1\right)}\log\left(1+ue^{-(4k^{2}+1)\nu^{2}T}\right)\\
\end{split}

(SS.443)

Hence

J_{c}=\frac{\nu T}{2}-\frac{1}{2\nu}\log(1-e^{2\nu u_{c}\Psi^{\prime}(u_{c})})\quad,\quad 2\nu u_{c}\Psi^{\prime}(u_{c})=\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k\,}{\left(4k^{2}+1\right)}\log\left(1-e^{-4k^{2}\nu^{2}T}\right)

(SS.444)

Since the second term is always positive we see that
J_{c}>\frac{\nu T}{2}
(which is
positive since we assume everywhere
\nu>0
).

These subtleties cleared, we will show in the next appendix how to obtain the upper tail of the current distribution from the jump
u\Delta^{\prime}(u)
in the case (
SS.438
).

Remark S.2

(Analytical continuation of the observable)
.

Let us assume
\alpha>0
.
Although (
SS.441
) always holds, there is a hidden branching point at
u=u_{\zeta_{c}}>u_{c}

and
\zeta(u)=\zeta_{c}
, where
\zeta_{c}
is defined below. This is
associated to the fact that the observable itself contains polylogarithm functions and also
needs to be continued. It
has no consequence for (
SS.441
).
We show that this continuation amounts to change the branch of
\omega
obtained in (
SH.52
).
Starting from the relation (obtained in Section
Q.2
for any
\zeta(u)
) for the derivative of the observable with respect to
u

\log(1+\zeta(u)\omega_{\zeta(u)})=\int_{\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R}+\delta}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{2\mathbf{i}\pi y(1-y)}\log\left(1+\zeta(u)\frac{\varrho_{1}\left(1-\varrho_{2}\right)(y-1)y}{\left(y-\varrho_{1}\right)\left(y-\varrho_{2}\right)}\right)

(SS.445)

we see that this integral is the same one as the one which defines
2\nu u\Psi^{\prime}(u)
, upon choosing
\nu\to 0
and
u\to\zeta(u)
. We can thus use the results on the continuation of
u\Psi^{\prime}(u)
and obtain that
\omega_{\zeta(u)}
and this expression are well defined as long as
\zeta(u)\geqslant\zeta_{c}=-(\sqrt{\alpha}-1)^{2}=-16w^{2}/(1+2w)^{2}
. This corresponds to
P_{\zeta_{c}}=\log\sqrt{\alpha}
,
\omega(\zeta_{c})=1/(1-\sqrt{\alpha})
. The jump of the observable is now obtained in a similar way as in (
SS.437
) and reads

\begin{split}\log(1+\zeta(u)\omega_{\zeta(u)})&\to\log(1+\zeta(u)\omega_{\zeta(u)})-4\mathrm{arctanh}\,\left(\frac{2w\sqrt{\zeta(u)/\zeta_{c}-1}}{\sqrt{4\zeta(u)w^{2}/\zeta_{c}-1}}\right)\\
&=-\log(1+\zeta(u)\omega_{\zeta(u)})+\log\alpha\end{split}

(SS.446)

We note that
-\log(1+\zeta(u)\omega_{\zeta(u)})+\log\alpha=\log(1+\zeta(u)\hat{\omega}_{\zeta(u)})
where
\hat{\omega}
is the second branch of the solution of (
SH.52
). For the continuations to be compatible, we assume that
u_{\zeta_{c}}\geqslant u_{c}
and
\zeta(u_{c})\geqslant\zeta_{c}
. This implies that

-(\sqrt{\alpha}-1)^{2}e^{2\nu J_{\zeta_{c}}}\geqslant-(\sqrt{\alpha}-1)^{2}e^{\nu^{2}T}\Longleftrightarrow J_{\zeta_{c}}<\nu T/2

(SS.447)

and

-(\sqrt{\alpha}-1)^{2}e^{-2\nu J_{c}+\nu^{2}T}\geqslant-(\sqrt{\alpha}-1)^{2}\Longleftrightarrow J_{c}>\nu T/2

(SS.448)

Denoting by
J_{\zeta_{c}}
the current at the position
u_{\zeta_{c}}
, this further implies that
J_{c}\geqslant J_{\zeta_{c}}
. Finally, we need to complement the parametric representation (
SS.441
) with the final equations

•

For
u\in[u_{c},u_{\zeta_{c}}]
and
J\in[J_{\zeta_{c}},J_{c}]
, we have

\begin{split}\log(1+\zeta(u)\omega_{\zeta(u)})&=2\nu u(\Psi^{\prime}(u)+\Delta^{\prime}(u))\\
\end{split}

(SS.449)

•

For
u\in[u_{\zeta_{c}},+\infty[
and
J\in]-\infty,J_{\zeta_{c}}]
we have

\begin{split}-\log(1+\zeta(u)\omega_{\zeta(u)})+\log\alpha&=2\nu u(\Psi^{\prime}(u)+\Delta^{\prime}(u))\\
\end{split}

(SS.450)

S.5
Plots of
\Phi(J)
and
\Phi^{\prime}(J)

We plot in this Section the functions
\Phi(J)
and
\Phi^{\prime}(J)
using the parametric representations in the main branch (
SI.59
) and in the second branch (
SS.441
). We report the plots in Fig.
SS.3
.

Figure SS.3:
Plot of
\Phi(J)
obtained numerically parametrically for the values
X=0
,
T=1
,
\nu=1
and various densities
\varrho_{1}=1-\varrho_{2}=\{0.6,0.95,1\}
. In the right plots representing
\Phi^{\prime}(J)
the blue curve represents the main branch and the orange curve represents the second branch where the continuation is introduced.

Appendix T
Tail for
J\to-\infty
(or
J\to 0
for step initial condition)

This tail corresponds to the
upper tail
for the height field
h=-J
.
For simplicity, as in the previous Section, we restrict
to the case
X=0
and
\varrho_{1}+\varrho_{2}=1
, and to the parameter range defined in (
SS.438
).

T.1
Tail for
J\to-\infty
for the WASEP (double-sided Bernoulli initial condition)

The upper tail of the WASEP for
\alpha>0
(which excludes the step initial condition)
is obtained by taking
u\to+\infty
adding the contribution of the jump to the large deviation function. We will use the results from Section
R.1
for the asymptotics of
u\Psi^{\prime}(u)
, which we will complement with the one of
u\Delta^{\prime}(u)
obtained above.

We first provide the asymptotics of the derivative of the jump for
u\to+\infty

\begin{split}u\Delta^{\prime}(u)=-\frac{2}{\nu}\mathrm{arctanh}(2\kappa(u))&=-\frac{\left(2\log(u)-\log\alpha\right)}{2\nu}-\frac{-4\nu^{2}T+16\nu^{2}Tw^{2}+8w^{2}+2}{\nu u(2w+1)^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{u}\right)^{2}\\
\end{split}

(ST.451)

where we recall that
\alpha=\left(\frac{1-2w}{1+2w}\right)^{2}
. This series is obtained by expanding (
SS.431
) around
\kappa=1/2
(equivalent to
u\to+\infty
), inverting the series and injecting it inside the hyperbolic arctangent. Adding this contribution to the
asymptotics in (
SR.388
) we obtain for
u\to+\infty

u\Psi^{\prime}(u)+u\Delta^{\prime}(u)=-\frac{\log u}{2\nu}-\frac{2\sqrt{T}}{\pi}\sqrt{\log u}+\tilde{b}+\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{\log u})\quad,\quad\tilde{b}=\frac{1}{\nu}(B_{w}+\log 2+\frac{\log\alpha}{2})

(ST.452)

Let us now examine the parametric equations (
SS.441
). From
(
ST.452
) we see that in the equation
of
\zeta(u)

it now the last term which is dominant at large
u

leading to

\displaystyle J\simeq u(\Psi^{\prime}(u)+\Delta^{\prime}(u))+\frac{\log u-\log\alpha}{2\nu}

(ST.453)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime}(J)=2\nu u(\Psi^{\prime}(u)+\Delta^{\prime}(u))

(ST.454)

up to much smaller terms of order
\mathcal{O}(1/u)
which we can neglect in the present
expansion which is in
1/\log u
.

Let us now use (
ST.452
), up to leading order

\displaystyle J=-\frac{2\sqrt{T}}{\pi}\sqrt{\log u}+b+\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{\log u})

(ST.455)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime}(J)=-\log u-\frac{4\nu\sqrt{T}}{\pi}\sqrt{\log u}+2\nu\tilde{b}+\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{\log u})

(ST.456)

Hence we find for
J\to-\infty
the following tail

\Phi(J)=\frac{\pi^{2}|J|^{3}}{12T}+J^{2}\left(\frac{\pi^{2}b}{4T}+\nu\right)+\mathcal{O}(J)

(ST.457)

Note that the first difference with the
J\to+\infty
tail (
SR.393
) appears in the subdominant
\mathcal{O}(J^{2})
term.

T.2
Tail near the wall
J\to 0
for the WASEP (step initial condition)

In the case of the step initial condition there is a "wall" at
J=0
, since

\Phi(J)
is defined only for
J>0
. One can ask about the asymptotic
behavior near the wall. It corresponds to
u\to-1^{-}

and
\kappa(u)\to 1/2^{-}
. Setting
u=-(1+\delta)

and using Eqs. (
SS.437
), (
SS.440
) and
(
SS.442
) we obtain

u\Delta^{\prime}(u)=\frac{1}{\nu}\log\left(\frac{\delta}{4\nu^{2}T}\right)+\frac{\delta\left(1-\nu^{2}T\right)}{2\nu^{3}T}+\mathcal{O}\left(\delta^{2}\right)

(ST.458)

J=\frac{\delta}{2\nu}+\frac{\delta^{2}}{32\nu^{5}}\left(\frac{e^{-2\nu\Psi^{\prime}(-1)}}{T^{2}}-8\nu^{4}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\delta^{3}\right)

(ST.459)

So that for small
\delta

\Phi^{\prime}(J)=2\log\left(\frac{\delta}{4\nu^{2}T}\right)-2\nu\Psi^{\prime}(-1)+\mathcal{O}(\delta)

(ST.460)

This leads to

\Phi^{\prime}(J)=2\log(\frac{J}{2\nu T})-2\nu\Psi^{\prime}(-1)+\mathcal{O}(J)

(ST.461)

Hence near the wall for
J\to 0
we find that

\Phi(J)=\Phi(0^{+})+2J\log(\frac{J}{2\nu T})-(2\nu\Psi^{\prime}(-1)+2)J+\mathcal{O}(J^{2})

(ST.462)

where
\Phi(0^{+})
is determined by the fact that one must have simultaneously

\Phi(J_{\rm typ})=\Phi^{\prime}(J_{\rm typ})=0
for the typical value (also
equal to the mean)
J_{\rm typ}=\langle J\rangle
, which was computed in (
SK.88
).

T.3
Large
\nu
limit and crossover from the wall to the typical value (step initial condition)

Here we study the step initial condition in the regime where
\nu\to+\infty
.
In that regime we will find that
J_{c}\simeq\langle J\rangle\simeq\frac{\nu T}{2}
.
We study here the region
J<J_{c}
.
Interestingly, it describes a crossover between the "wall" region described
in the previous section and a regime where KPZ behavior emerges. Surprisingly,
it can be matched completely to a recent result on ASEP, obtained in an a priori
different limit (see below).

Let us start with the estimation of
J_{c}
and of the typical value,
\langle J\rangle
, in the limit of large
\nu
(which corresponds to
\kappa=0
).
One has from (
SS.444
) for large
\nu

\displaystyle 2\nu u_{c}\Psi^{\prime}(u_{c})=-\frac{\zeta(3/2)}{\sqrt{\pi T}\nu}+\frac{\zeta\left(5/2\right)}{2\sqrt{\pi}\nu^{3}T^{3/2}}+\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{\nu^{5}})

(ST.463)

\displaystyle J_{c}\simeq\frac{\nu T}{2}+\frac{1}{2\nu}\log(\frac{\nu\sqrt{\pi T}}{\zeta(3/2)})+\mathcal{O}(1/\nu^{2})

(ST.464)

while we recall that

\langle J\rangle=-\frac{\log(\text{Erfc}(\nu\sqrt{T})))}{2\nu}=\frac{\nu T}{2}+\frac{1}{2\nu}\log(\nu\sqrt{\pi})+\mathcal{O}(1/\nu^{3})\quad\nu\to+\infty

(ST.465)

Hence we see that
J_{c}
and
\langle J\rangle
are both large, but their
difference vanish at large
\nu
as
\langle J\rangle-J_{c}\simeq\frac{1}{2\nu}\log(\zeta(3/2))
.
These results can be compared with the KPZ equation, as provided in the following remark.

Remark T.1
.

In
Le Doussal et al. 2016
the KPZ equation was studied for short time
T_{\rm KPZ}\ll 1
with droplet initial condition.
Let us denote here
{\cal H}
the variable
H
defined in that paper.
It is related to
H_{\rm KPZ}
defined here in (
SG.47
) as follows

{\cal H}=H_{\rm KPZ}+\log(\sqrt{4\pi T_{\rm KPZ}})-\log(2\nu\varepsilon)

(ST.466)

The correspondence is read from (
SP.331
)
and from
Le Doussal et al. 2016
.
It was shown there that the position of the branching point is

{\cal H}_{c}=\log(\zeta(3/2))
. This is consistent with the present
result, using
T_{\rm KPZ}=\nu^{4}\varepsilon^{2}T
(for general
T
) since here we obtain

H^{c}_{\rm KPZ}=-2\nu(J_{c}-\frac{\nu T}{2})={\cal H}_{c}-\log(\nu\sqrt{\pi T})+o(1)

(ST.467)

We now study the crossover regime corresponding to
u_{c}=-e^{\nu^{2}T}<u\ll-1
, with
\kappa=\kappa(u)=\mathcal{O}(1)
and
u\to-\infty
with (from (
SS.440
))

\frac{\log(-u)}{\nu^{2}T}=1-4\kappa^{2}\quad,\quad 0<\kappa<\frac{1}{2}

(ST.468)

so that
\kappa=0
corresponds to
u=u_{c}
and
\kappa=1/2
to
u/u_{c}\to 0
. In the regime
\kappa=\mathcal{O}(1)
it is easy to see that
u\Psi^{\prime}(u)+u\Delta^{\prime}(u)\simeq u\Delta^{\prime}(u)
up to exponentially small correction terms, indeed one has, from (
SS.443
)

u\Psi^{\prime}(u)=\frac{1}{\nu}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k\,}{\pi\left(4k^{2}+1\right)}\log\left(1-e^{-(4k^{2}+4\kappa^{2})\nu^{2}T}\right)\simeq-\frac{1}{\nu}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k\,}{\pi\left(4k^{2}+1\right)}e^{-(4k^{2}+4\kappa^{2})\nu^{2}T}\sim\frac{1}{\nu^{2}}e^{-4\kappa^{2}\nu^{2}T}

(ST.469)

which is negligible for
\kappa\gg 1/\nu^{2}
.

Using Eqs. (
SS.442
), (
SS.437
) and (
ST.468
), we then obtain the parametric representation for
0<J\leqslant J_{c}

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime}(J)\simeq 2\nu u\Delta^{\prime}(u)+\mathcal{O}(1/\nu)=-4\,\mathrm{arctanh}(2\kappa)+\mathcal{O}(1/\nu)

(ST.470)

\displaystyle J\simeq\frac{1}{2\nu}\log|u|-\frac{1}{2\nu}\log\left(1-(\frac{1-2\kappa}{1+2\kappa})^{2}\right)\simeq(1-4\kappa^{2})\frac{\nu T}{2}+\mathcal{O}(1/\nu)

(ST.471)

To this leading order we also find

2\kappa=\sqrt{1-\frac{J}{J_{c}}}+\mathcal{O}(1/\nu^{2})
and

\Phi^{\prime}(J)=-4\,\mathrm{arctanh}(\sqrt{1-\frac{J}{J_{c}}})+\mathcal{O}(1/\nu)

(ST.472)

leading to

\displaystyle\Phi(J)

\displaystyle\simeq 2\nu T\left(\left(4\kappa^{2}-1\right)\mathrm{arctanh}(2\kappa)+2\kappa\right)

(ST.474)

\displaystyle\simeq 4J_{c}\left(\sqrt{1-\frac{J}{J_{c}}}-\frac{J}{J_{c}}\mathrm{arctanh}(\sqrt{1-\frac{J}{J_{c}}})\right)

which vanishes at
J=J_{c}\simeq\langle J\rangle
as it should, and
where corrections are of order
1/\nu
.
Note that
\Phi(J)
coincides exactly with
\Delta(u)
obtained in (
SS.440
).
The reason is that for the step initial condition one has, from the Legendre
inversion formula at the saddle point
see Section
H

\begin{split}\Phi(J)=\Psi(u)+\Delta(u)+\frac{1}{2\nu}{\rm Li}_{2}(-ue^{-2\nu J})\end{split}

(ST.475)

and since
ue^{-2\nu J}=\mathcal{O}(1)
from (
ST.471
), and
that
\Psi(u)=\mathcal{O}(\nu T)
we see that
the third term is subdominant in the limit
\nu\to+\infty
. One can perform the expansions around
J\to 0
and
J\to J_{c}
and
one finds

\Phi(J)=\begin{cases}4J_{c}\left(1-\frac{J}{2J_{c}}(1-\log\frac{J}{4J_{c}})\right)+\mathcal{O}(J^{2})\\
\frac{8}{3}J_{c}\left(1-\frac{J}{J_{c}}\right){}^{3/2}+\frac{8}{15}J_{c}\left(1-\frac{J}{J_{c}}\right){}^{5/2}+\mathcal{O}\left((1-\frac{J}{J_{c}})^{7/2}\right)\end{cases}

(ST.476)

Remark T.2
.

Remarkably, the expression (
ST.474
) coincides with the large deviation
function for the upper tail of the ASEP for the step initial condition
obtained recently in
(
Das and Zhu 2022
, Eq. (1.4))
. Let us recall that result. With the same initial
condition as in the present paper, they find that

H_{0}(t)={\sf J}_{\sf t}(0)
, i.e., the number of particles to the right of zero,
satisfies the large deviation principle

{\rm Prob}\left(\frac{\gamma{\sf t}/4-H_{0}({\sf t})}{\gamma{\sf t}/4}>y\right)\sim e^{-\gamma{\sf t}\Phi_{+}(y)}\quad,\quad\Phi_{+}(y)=\sqrt{y}-(1-y)\mathrm{arctanh}(\sqrt{y})\quad,\quad 0<y<1

(ST.477)

where
\gamma=R-L
. At small
y
one has
\Phi_{+}(y)\simeq\frac{2}{3}y^{3/2}
, i.e.
it matches both the tail of the GUE Tracy-Widom distribution as it should, see
(
Das and Zhu 2022
, Remark 1.5)
, and
the upper tail of the KPZ equation. This result is a priori in a
completely different regime (fixed asymmetry
R-L=\mathcal{O}(1)
, and large ASEP time)
from the one studied here. The coincidence between the two results,
which can be checked using that
\gamma{\sf t}\to 2\nu T/\varepsilon=4J_{c}/\varepsilon
, indicates
that no intermediate regime exists in the large deviations between these two limits.
